Anderson Localization for the hierarchical Anderson-Bernoulli model on ℤ d \mathbb{Z}^{d}
Shihe Liu, Yunfeng Shi, Zhifei Zhang

Abstract
. In this paper, we prove Anderson localization for a hierarchical Anderson-Bernoulli model on ℤ d \mathbb{Z}^{d} with arbitrary d ≥ 1 d\geq 1 , where the potential is characterized by a geometric hierarchical structure combined with fluctuations induced by independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) Bernoulli random variables. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first localization result for a model with i.i.d. two‑point Bernoulli potential in dimensions d ≥ 4 d\geq 4 . Notably, our approach is independent of any unique continuation result, instead combining a weak (one-dimensional) transversality estimate with a martingale argument. As such, we hope that our method may shed new light on the eventual proof of Anderson localization for the standard Anderson-Bernoulli model on ℤ d \mathbb{Z}^{d} with d ≥ 4 . d\geq 4. Our method is also applicable to proving a probabilistic unique continuation result on ℤ d \mathbb{Z}^{d} .



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background and related results

The study of Anderson localization (i.e., pure point spectrum with exponentially decaying eigenfunctions) for Schrödinger operator on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}

with independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) random potentials has attracted considerable attention over the decades. For the Anderson model—where the random potential of the Schrödinger operator is is uniformly distributed over an interval—Anderson conjectured in
[
And58
]
that sufficiently strong disorder would induce eigenstates to be exponentially localized within a finite region. This conjecture was later independently proven in
[
FMSS85
,
DLS85
,
SW86
]
, building on the pivotal Green’s function estimates developed by Fröhlich-Spencer
[
FS83
]
. In that work, the authors devised a novel multiscale analysis framework to address resonant phenomena, with a core focus on the
hierarchical structure of resonant blocks
—subsystems where Green’s functions cannot be tightly controlled (e.g., lacking off-diagonal exponential decay bounds). Specifically, they demonstrated that in the regime of strong disorder or extreme energies, any finite subset can be decomposed, with high probability, into a union of non-resonant blocks of varying sizes
d_{k}
that satisfy the scaling relation
d_{k+1}=d_{k}^{\alpha},\ \alpha>1
, known as Newtonian scales. The probabilistic bounds underpinning this decomposition were derived using the a priori Wegner estimate
[
Weg81
]
—which requires the random potential to have at least a continuous distribution—and a suite of combinatorial entropy estimates.

Because the Green’s function estimates in
[
FS83
]
were established for a fixed energy, the result only confirmed the absence of diffusion (or equivalently, vanishing conductivity) rather than full Anderson localization. Later, Martinelli and Scoppola
[
MS85
]
integrated the estimates from
[
FS83
]
with a generalized eigenvalue argument (namely, Shnol’s theorem) to rigorously establish the absence of absolutely continuous spectrum. Notably, to elucidate the physical mechanism driving localization, Jona-Lasinio, Martinelli, and Scoppola
[
JLMS84
,
JLMS85
]
introduced the concept of a
deterministic hierarchical potential
, constructed by iterating a basic geometric building block across an ascending sequence of Newtonian-type length scales. This innovation enabled the first formal connection between the quantum tunneling analysis of semiclassical limit Schrödinger equations
[
JLMS81
]
and the theory of Anderson localization. In particular, they proved that for the Schrödinger operator on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with a deterministic symmetric hierarchical potential, even weak perturbations by i.i.d. random potentials (with
absolutely continuous distributions
) trigger a delocalization-to-localization transition for the low-lying spectrum. This hierarchical Anderson model can be regarded as a simplified “toy mode” of the standard Anderson model—one that is, in principle, physically realizable
[
JLMS84
,
WSPZ89
]
—as it presupposes that resonant sets inherently exhibit a geometrically hierarchical structure. From a quantum tunneling perspective, this hierarchical architecture dictates the spatial arrangement of potential barriers and wells in the system. Since localization fundamentally implies that a quantum particle cannot escape a fixed region over long timescales (i.e., it remains confined within a potential well without undergoing tunneling), localization becomes increasingly probable as barriers grow higher and wider. In fact, the authors of
[
FMSS85
]
(see
[
MS87
]
for a comprehensive exposition) successfully proved Anderson localization for the standard Anderson model by showing that typical configurations of its random potential share a similar hierarchical structure to those in the model proposed in
[
JLMS85
]
. It is worth noting that the hierarchical structure of resonant sets also manifests in the context of deterministic quasi-periodic Schrödinger operators, as documented in
[
FSW90
,
BGS02
,
Bou07
,
CSZ23
]
.

However, the results discussed above primarily concern Schrödinger operators on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with random potentials possessing bounded densities—even the work in
[
JLMS85
]
only established localization for random potentials with bounded densities—and we refer readers to
[
Kir08
,
AW15
,
AM93
]
for additional findings pertaining to continuously distributed random potentials. In that framework, the proof of the Wegner estimate, which quantifies the probability of resonance events, is standard and straightforward. By contrast, our focus is directed toward the scenario where the i.i.d. random potential follows a two-point Bernoulli distribution, namely the well-known Anderson-Bernoulli model (ABM). In the ABM, the measure concentration effects induced by the potential invalidate the traditional approaches to deriving the Wegner estimate, thereby posing a major obstacle to proving localization. Indeed, in the case of Bernoulli potentials, probabilistic bounds on the Green’s function cannot be obtained by perturbing a single random variable; instead, bounds on “rare events” must be established by accounting for the dependence of eigenvalues on a large ensemble of random variables.

For the one-dimensional ABM, this challenge can be circumvented via methods specific to one-dimensional lattices, such as transfer matrix-based methods and the Furstenberg–LePage approach (cf.
[
CKM87
,
SVW98
,
DSS02
]
). In higher dimensions, however, the transfer matrices essential to one-dimensional dynamical systems techniques are generally unavailable, rendering multiscale analysis the more viable methodological candidate. The foundational work addressing the localization problem for the alloy-type ABM in higher dimensions was pioneered by Bourgain in
[
Bou04
]
, where he developed a novel multiscale iteration scheme for Green’s function estimates incorporating a key
free sites argument
and proved localization at the spectral edges. The methodology introduced in
[
Bou04
]
was further advanced by Bourgain and Kenig in
[
BK05
]
, in which they achieved a breakthrough by establishing localization at the spectral edges for the ABM defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

In
[
Bou04
]
and
[
BK05
]
(see
[
GK13
]
for refinements), transversality served as a critical ingredient for proving localization. In the former work, transversality is inherent to the structure of the alloy-type potential; in the latter, it relies on a unique continuation theorem for elliptic equations in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(cf.
[
BK05
, Lemma 3.15]
). However, as noted by Jitomirskaya
[
Jit07
]
, an analogous unique continuation statement fails on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
—a limitation that posed a major obstacle to proving localization for the discrete ABM. The problem of establishing localization at the spectral edges for the ABM on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
d\geq 2

remained open until the influential work of Ding and Smart
[
DS20
]
, in which they established a probabilistic variant of the unique continuation result on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, partially building on the techniques of
[
BLMS22
]
, and thereby proved Anderson localization for the ABM on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Subsequently, Li and Zhang
[
LZ22
]
extended this two-dimensional framework to prove a unique continuation result and resolve the localization problem at the spectral edges for the ABM on
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, supplying the necessary transversality. Furthermore, the transversality result on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
has been leveraged to establish localization for the ABM on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}

under strong disorder
[
Li22
]
. For localization results concerning alloy-type ABM models with long-range hopping, we also refer readers to
[
LSZ25b
]
.

The problem of establishing localization at the spectral edges for the ABM on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
d\geq 4
, and even for the hierarchical ABM as investigated in
[
JLMS85
]
, remains open
. (Some attempts have been made to prove localization for higher-dimensional Schrödinger operators with singular potentials in
[
Imb21
]
, where the random potential is discrete and assumes
N\gg 1
distinct values.) A fundamental obstacle lies in the absence of an appropriate characterization of transversality on higher-dimensional lattices. In this article, we establish localization in a neighborhood of the bottom of the spectrum for the higher-dimensional hierarchical ABM in arbitrary dimensions. We consider a significant contribution of this work to be our ability to prove localization using a weaker form of transversality than that required by unique continuation theorems, as well as our uncovering of a special martingale structure inherent to higher-dimensional lattices in the course of the proof. To the best of our knowledge, this constitutes the first localization result for a model with an i.i.d. two-point Bernoulli potential in dimensions
d\geq 4
.

Finally, we emphasize that
the method developed in this paper can also be applied to proving a probabilistic unique continuation result (where the probability depends on the initial data) on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for arbitrary
d\geq 2
(see Theorem
2.6
).
In its proof, the same martingale with a special structure reemerges—a fact we believe is of independent interest.

1.2.
The deterministic hierarchical potential

In this section, we present the precise definitions of our core models. This class of models was originally introduced in
[
JLMS84
,
JLMS85
]
for the purpose of investigating the quantum tunneling behavior of quantum particles.

Let
d_{0}>1,\alpha>1
and consider the
Newtonian scales

(1.1)

d_{k+1}=d_{k}^{\alpha},\ k\geq 0.

Let
|\cdot|
and
|\cdot|_{1}
be the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm and
\ell^{1}
-norm on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, respectively. Denote for
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
L\geq 0,

Q_{L}(j)=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ |x-j|\leq L\}.

Denote by
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
by the integer part of a real number. Throughout this paper, we let

(1.2)

\Lambda_{k}(j):=\left\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ |x-j|\leq 2\lfloor 3d_{k}\rfloor\right\},\ k\geq 0.

In particular, write
\Lambda_{k}=\Lambda_{k}(0)
.

Now, consider a deterministic potential
V:\ \mathbb{Z}^{d}\rightarrow\{0,h\}
with some
h>4d+1
.
The
potential well
of
V
is defined by

\mathcal{W}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ V(x)=0\right\},

and the
potential barrier
is defined by

\mathcal{B}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ V(x)=h\right\}.

Here,
h
is called the
height
of the potential barrier, and

\alpha>1
in the Newtonian scales describes
the width
of the potential barrier.

In this article, we mainly focus on potentials with a specific geometric hierarchical structure. Let
N\geq 1
be a fixed positive integer.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
V:\ \mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\{0,h\}
and
\mathcal{W}
be as above. We say that
V
is
(1,N)
-hierarchical in
\Lambda_{1}(j)
if

\mathcal{W}\cap\Lambda_{1}(j)=\bigcup_{1\leq s\leq N_{1}}C^{s}_{0}

with

•

N_{1}\leq N
,

•

{\rm diam}(C_{0}^{s})\leq 4\lfloor 3d_{0}\rfloor
for all
1\leq s\leq N_{1}
,

•

C_{0}^{1}\subset\Lambda_{0}(j)
and
{\rm dist}(C_{0}^{s},C_{0}^{s^{\prime}})\geq 2d_{1}
for any
s\neq s^{\prime}
,

where both
{\rm diam}(\cdot)
and
{\rm dist}(\cdot,\cdot)
are induced by the
\ell^{\infty}
-norm
|\cdot|
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Next, we iteratively define the hierarchical structure of higher levels.

Definition 1.2
.

For
k\geq 2
, we say that
V
is
(k,N)
-hierarchical in
\Lambda_{k}(j)
if

\mathcal{W}\cap\Lambda_{k}(j)=\bigcup_{1\leq s\leq N_{k}}C^{s}_{k-1}

with

•

N_{k}\leq N
,

•

C_{k-1}^{1}\subset\Lambda_{k-1}(j)
and
{\rm dist}(C_{k-1}^{s},C_{k-1}^{s^{\prime}})\geq 2d_{k}
for any
s\neq s^{\prime}
,

•

V
is
(k-1,N)
-hierarchical in
\Lambda_{k-1}(j)
,

•

For each
2\leq s\leq N_{k}
, there exists some
\Lambda_{k-1}(j_{s})\subset\Lambda_{k}(j)
such that
C_{k-1}^{s}\subset\Lambda_{k-1}(j_{s})
and
V
is
(k-1,N)
-hierarchical in
\Lambda_{k-1}(j_{s})
.

Definition 1.3
.

We say
V:\ \mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\{0,h\}
is a
hierarchical potential
if it is
(k,N)
-hierarchical in
\Lambda_{k}
for all
k\geq 1
.

Remark 1.1
.

The parameter
N
characterizing the number of potential wells at each hierarchical level, is referred to the
well density
(cf. Figure
1
).

\Lambda_{k}(j)
\Lambda_{k-1}(j)
\sim d_{k}
\sim d_{k-1}
\Lambda_{k-2}(j)

Figure 1.
Hierarchical structure with well density
N\leq 5
.

An important example of a hierarchical potential is the
symmetric hierarchical potential
introduced in
[
JLMS84
,
JLMS85
]
:

Example 1.1

(cf.
[
JLMS84
,
JLMS85
]
)
.

The hierarchical potential
V_{\text{sy}}:\ \mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\{0,h\}
is called a
symmetric hierarchical potential
if, within
\Lambda_{1}
,
C_{0}^{1}=\Lambda_{0}
, and for every
\Lambda_{k}
with
k\geq 1
,

•

N_{k}=2d+1
,

•

Each component
C_{k-1}^{s}
for
2\leq s\leq 2d+1
is obtained via translating
C_{k-1}^{1}
along each coordinate axis by the distance of
\pm 2(\lfloor 3d_{k}\rfloor-\lfloor 3d_{k-1}\rfloor)
.

See
[
JLMS85
, Fig. 2]
for an intuitive illustration.

1.3.
Main results

We define

(1.3)

\Delta f(x)=\sum_{y:\ y\sim x}f(y),

where
y\sim x
means
|x-y|_{1}=1
. Let
V_{\text{hi}}:\ \mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\{0,h\}
be a hierarchical potential defined in the the previous section satisfying the following properties:

•

The height of potential barrier
h>4d+1
,

•

The width of the potential barrier
\alpha>1
,

•

The well density
N\geq 1
.

Consider the deterministic Hamiltonian

H_{0}=2d-\Delta+V_{\text{hi}}.

Denote by
{\rm Spec}(\cdot)
the spectrum of an operator. According to
[
JLMS85
, Proposition 3.1]
, the spectrum of
H_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle{\rm Spec}(H_{0})\cap[h,\infty)=[h,h+4d],\ {\rm Spec}(H_{0})\cap[0,h)\subset[0,4d].

It was established in
[
JLMS85
, Theorem 4.2]
that, at the bottom of the spectrum (i.e.,
{\rm Spec}(H_{0})\cap[0,h)
),
H_{0}
(with a symmetric hierarchical potential) can exhibit the
delocalization behavior
. More precisely, if we take
V_{\text{hi}}=V_{\text{sy}}
as in Example
1.1
, then the mean square displacement (MSD)

r^{2}(t)=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|x|^{2}\cdot\left|(e^{-itH_{0}}P_{[0,4d]}(H_{0})\delta_{0})(x)\right|^{2}

satisfies a lower bound

(1.4)

\displaystyle r^{2}(t_{k})\gtrsim(2d+1)^{-k}d_{k}\to+\infty\ {\rm as}\ k\to\infty

for a subsequence
{t_{k}}
with
t_{k}\to\infty
, where
\delta_{0}(x)=\delta_{x,0}
and
P_{[0,4d]}(H_{0})
is the spectral projection of
H_{0}
on
[0,4d]
.

The celebrated work
[
JLMS85
]
showed that once the i.i.d. random perturbations with
the absolutely continuous distribution
were introduced into
H_{0}
, both Anderson localization and dynamical localization (i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\sup\limits_{t\in\mathbb{R}}r^{2}(t)<+\infty
) hold true. This result thus illustrates the phenomenon of the instability of quantum tunneling.

Notably, the proof in
[
JLMS85
]
relies on the a priori Wegner estimate and Fröhlich-Spencer estimates
[
FS83
]
—the former of which is unavailable for singular random distributions, such as i.i.d. Bernoulli distributions. This limitation motivates our study of localization at the bottom of the spectrum for the randomly perturbed Hamiltonian
H_{0}
with Bernoulli random variables. We therefore consider the
random hierarchical potential

(1.5)

V(\omega)=V_{\text{hi}}+\beta V_{\text{r}}(\omega),

where
\{V_{\text{r},x}(\omega)\}_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is a family of i.i.d. random variables on the probability space
\left(\Omega=\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},\ \mathbb{P}\right)
with

(1.6)

\mathbb{P}(V_{\text{r},x}(\omega)=0)=\mathbb{P}(V_{\text{r},x}(\omega)=1)=\frac{1}{2}\ {\rm for\ all}\ \forall x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

For simplicity, we also write
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\ni\omega
with
\omega(x)=V_{\text{r},x}(\omega)
for
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

In this paper, we study the hierarchical ABM on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}

(1.7)

H(\omega)=H_{0}+V(\omega)=2d-\Delta+V_{\rm hi}+\beta V_{\text{r}}(\omega),

where the coupling strength
0<\beta\leq 1
and
V_{\text{r}}(\omega)
is the random Bernoulli potential.
A standard argument (see, e.g.,
[
Kri08
, Lemma 2.1.2]
) shows for all
\omega
,

(1.8)

{\rm Spec}(H(\omega))\subset[0,4d+\beta]\cup[h,h+4d+\beta].

Remark 1.2
.

The condition
h>4d+1
is imposed solely to ensure that the spectrum decomposes into two disjoint intervals, as illustrated in (
1.8
).

Our main results are stated as follows.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
H(\omega)
be defined by (
1.7
) with
1\leq d\leq 3
. Then for any
0<\beta\leq 1
, the operator
H(\omega)
exhibits the Anderson localization in the interval
[0,h)\cap{\rm Spec}(H(\omega))
almost surely.

If additional conditions on
h
,
\alpha
and
N
are imposed, we can establish the Anderson localization for the hierarchical ABM in higher dimensions.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
H(\omega)
be defined by (
1.7
) with
d\geq 4
. There is some
h_{0}=h_{0}(d)>4d+1
depending only on
d
such that, if
h>h_{0}
and
\alpha>N
, then for any
0<\beta\leq 1
, the operator
H(\omega)
exhibits the Anderson localization in the interval
[0,h)\cap{\rm Spec}(H(\omega))
almost surely.

Remark 1.3
.

Indeed, the proof is also valid for dimensions
1\leq d\leq 3
. The constraints imposed in Theorem
1.3
are motivated by the underlying mechanism of quantum tunneling: localization arises when particle tunneling is suppressed, a condition that is favored by taller (larger

h
) and wider (larger
\alpha
) potential barriers, as well as a lower density of potential wells (smaller
N
). Given that the propensity toward delocalization intensifies with increasing spatial dimensions
[
Bou03
,
LSZ25a
]
, the enhanced barrier configurations (large
h
,
\alpha>N
) are precisely the adjustments required to counteract this dimensional delocalization effect.

Finally, under the assumptions of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
, we can prove the following dynamical localization result.

Theorem 1.4
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.2
(
d\leq 3
) and Theorem
1.3
(
d\geq 4
), the following holds true for any
0<\beta\leq 1
: for
\mathbb{P}
a.e.
\omega
, we have

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}r^{2}(t,\omega)=\sup_{t\geq 0}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|x|^{2}\cdot\left|(e^{-itH(\omega)}P_{[0,h)}(H(\omega))\delta_{0})(x)\right|^{2}\leq C(\omega)<\infty.

Remark 1.4
.

Considering the delocalization estimate (
1.4
) for the deterministic
H_{0}
with a symmetric hierarchical potential, this theorem indicates the instability of quantum tunneling under any non-zero
singular
random perturbations.

1.4.
New ingredients of proof

The proof follows the spirit of
[
FS83
,
FMSS85
]
. However, in the present work, we must overcome the core challenge posed by the lack of a priori Wegner estimates when dealing with Bernoulli random potentials. The main novel contributions of this paper are as follows:

•

We establish Wegner estimates for the hierarchical ABM across different dimensions by leveraging distinct forms of transversality. For dimensions
d\leq 3
, we employ existing unique continuation results in conjunction with the techniques developed in
[
DS20
]
; for dimensions
d\geq 4
, we rely solely on the weaker transversality afforded by the so-called “cone property” (cf. Proposition
2.1
). The proof for the case
d\geq 4
necessitates increasing the height and width of the model’s potential barriers—a step that admits a natural interpretation from the perspective of quantum tunneling: taller and wider barriers compensate for the weaker transversality, thereby suppressing quantum tunneling effects.

•

The proof for
d\geq 4
draws substantial inspiration from the methodology in
[
IM16
,
Imb21
]
. For our model, we refine and extend the “iterative Schur complement argument” introduced in those works. We devise a simpler and more effective approach to isolate the dominant transversal term in the decomposition (Lemma
4.7
), which enables us to eliminate the dependence of the barrier height on the coupling constant (see Section
4.4
). Additionally, we invoke the approximate orthogonality of eigenvectors to achieve sharp control over the number of eigenvalues within a given interval.

•

In standard multi-scale analysis, iterations are performed over Newtonian scales. A novel facet of our proof for the higher-dimensional Wegner estimate is the interpolation of a finer

a
-adic scale (
a\geq 5
) between consecutive Newtonian scales. On this
a
-adic scale, we prove that the eigenvalue count is strictly monotonic in the large deviation sense. We then revert to the Newtonian scale to derive the desired Wegner estimate. We anticipate that this scale-comparison strategy offers a fresh perspective on multi-scale analysis itself.

•

For dimensions
d\leq 3
, we apply the Sperner family lemma introduced by Bourgain (cf. Lemma 2.1 in
[
Bou04
]
, later extended in
[
DS20
]
) to bound the relevant probabilities. In contrast, for higher dimensions, we construct a so-called “site-mixed” martingale to control the tail probabilities in the Wegner estimate (see Section
4.5
). This martingale possesses a distinctive characteristic: its randomness stems not only from the potential values at individual sites but also from the selection of the sites themselves. Furthermore, the same martingale argument is utilized to prove a unique continuation result on lattices of arbitrary dimension (see Appendix
A
).

1.5.
Notations

The notations used in this paper can be collected as follows.

•

For two nonnegative quantities
f
and
g
, we write
f\lesssim g
, if there is an absolute constant

C>0
such that
f\leq Cg
. If we want to emphasize that
C
depends on some parameters

a,b,\cdots
independent of
f,g
, then we write
f\lesssim_{a,b,\cdots}g
. We denote
f\sim g
if both
f\lesssim g
and
g\lesssim f
(this should be distinguished with
x\sim y
whenever
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, which means
|x-y|_{1}=1
). In some cases, we denote
f\ll g
if there is some small enough
c>0
independent of
f,g
so that
f\leq cg.

•

For any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, denote
a\wedge b=\min\{a,b\}
and
a\vee b=\max\{a,b\}
.

•

Denote by
\#
the cardinality of a set. In particular, when applied to a subset of the spectrum, for instance,
\#({\rm Spec}(H)\cap A)
, it means the cardinality counting multiplicities.

•

Throughout this paper, the symbols
{\rm dist}(\cdot)
and
{\rm diam}(\cdot,\cdot)
denote the metric induced by
|\cdot|
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
or the standard Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{R}
, respectively, with the understanding that the context will prevent any ambiguity.

•

Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denote the standard inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

For a subset
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, let
R_{\Lambda}
denote the restriction operator on
\ell^{2}(\Lambda)
. The restriction of
H
to
\Lambda
with Dirichlet boundary condition is then given by
H_{\Lambda}=R_{\Lambda}HR_{\Lambda}
. We also denote
V_{\Lambda}=R_{\Lambda}VR_{\Lambda}
; the notations
V_{\text{hi},\Lambda}
and
V_{\text{r},\Lambda}
are defined analogously.

•

For a subset
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we define its boundaries to be

\partial^{-}\Lambda=\{y\in\Lambda:\ \exists x\notin\Lambda\ {\rm such\ that}\ x\sim y\},

\partial^{+}\Lambda=\{y\notin\Lambda:\ \exists x\in\Lambda\ {\rm such\ that}\ x\sim y\}.

•

By
\bm{1}_{A}(\cdot)
we mean the characteristic function on
A
.

1.6.
Structure of the paper

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce some basic facts on the transversality analysis. The central part of the work, namely, a proof of the Wegner estimate involving the Bernoulli random variables, is presented in Section
3
(
d\leq 3
) and Section
4
(
d\geq 4
). In Section
5
, we will eliminate the energy parameters from the (probabilistic) Wegner estimate, leading to the proof of our main localization results (Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
, and
1.4
). Supplementary materials, including a probabilistic unique continuation result and some useful lemmas, are provided in the Appendices.

2.
The transversality analysis

This section presents some basic facts regarding transversality estimates, which play a crucial and fundamental role in the analysis of tight-binding models involving Bernoulli random potentials. In such problems, transversality typically manifests itself by ensuring that the corresponding eigenfunctions do not decay rapidly.

We first introduce the useful cone property with consideration to the propagation of solutions to the discrete Schrödinger equation induced by our hierarchical ABM. Next, we prove several unique continuation results across different spatial dimensions. Of particular importance is a probabilistic unique continuation result (cf. Theorem
2.6
) on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for
d\geq 4,
whose proof is based on the cone property combined with the martingale argument.

Consider the Schrödinger equation on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

(2.1)

\Delta u=Wu,

where
\Delta
is defined in (
1.3
) and
W\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})
is a bounded potential. Our aim is to achieve certain lower bound on the decay rate of its solutions, at least on a portion of the lattice.
Now, recalling (
1.8
) and
h>4d+1\geq 4d+\beta
, we fix throughout this paper

(2.2)

\iota=\min\left\{\frac{1}{100},\frac{h-4d-\beta}{2}\right\}.

Then
h>4d+\beta+\iota
. We focus on solutions of the random equation

(2.3)

H(\omega)u-\lambda u=\big(2d+V(\omega)-\lambda\big)u-\Delta u=0,

where
V(\omega)
is given by (
1.5
) and
\lambda\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
. Clearly, (
2.3
) is a special case of (
2.1
) with

W=2d+V(\omega)-\lambda,\ \|W\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|W(x)|\leq 2d+h+\beta+\iota.

2.1.
Cone property

A simple yet important form of transversality is characterized by the ”cone property.” This property leverages a pigeonhole argument to derive a lower bound for solutions propagating within a specified cone.

In the following, we let
\{\mathbf{e}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{d}
denote the standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, namely, for each
1\leq k\leq d
,
({\bm{e}}_{k})(j)=\delta_{k,j}
. For any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, any
1\leq k\leq d
and
\sigma\in\{1,-1\}
, define the cone with vertex
x_{0}
and direction
\sigma\mathbf{e}_{k}
to be

\mathfrak{C}(x_{0},\sigma\mathbf{e}_{k})=\{y\sim x_{0}+\sigma\mathbf{e}_{k}:\ y\neq x_{0}\}.

Proposition 2.1

(
Cone property).

Let
u
be a solution of (
2.1
).
Then we have

(2.4)

\max_{y\in\mathfrak{C}(x_{0},\sigma\mathbf{e}_{k})}|u(y)|\geq\frac{1}{\|W\|_{\infty}+2d-1}|u(x_{0})|.

Proof of Proposition
2.1
.

Evaluating at the site
x_{0}+\sigma\mathbf{e}_{k}
to the equation (
2.1
), we get

\sum_{y\sim x_{0}+\sigma\mathbf{e}_{k}}u(y)=W(x_{0}+\sigma\mathbf{e}_{k})\cdot u(x_{0}+\sigma\mathbf{e}_{k}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle|u(x_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{y\sim x_{0}+\sigma\mathbf{e}_{k}\atop y\neq x_{0}}|u(y)|+\|W\|_{\infty}|u(x_{0}+\sigma\mathbf{e}_{k})|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\|W\|_{\infty}+2d-1\right)\cdot\max_{y\in\mathfrak{C}(x_{0},\sigma\mathbf{e}_{k})}|u(y)|,

which implies (
2.4
).
∎

An immediate corollary of the cone property is the following lemma concerning solutions to the Dirichlet problem for (
2.3
) within a block.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
B_{1}=Q_{l}(x)
be a block and let
B_{2}=Q_{L+\ell}(x)
be a larger one with
L,\ell>0
. Consider a solution
u
to the Dirichlet problem

\begin{cases}H(\omega)u-\lambda u=0\ \text{in }B_{2},\\
u\equiv 0\ \text{on }\partial^{+}B_{2},\end{cases}

where
\lambda\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
. Define the influence of
u
at a boundary point
y\in\partial^{+}B_{2}
to be

(2.5)

\mathcal{I}_{u}(y):=\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\sim y\\
x\in B_{2}\end{subarray}}u(x)\right|.

Then there exists a point
\bar{y}\in\partial^{+}B_{2}
such that

\mathcal{I}_{u}(\bar{y})\geq\gamma^{L+\ell}\cdot\max_{n\in B_{1}}|u(n)|,

where
\gamma=\dfrac{1}{4d+h+\beta}
.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

Suppose that
|u|
attains its maximum (in
B_{1}
) at some
n_{0}\in B_{1}
. We choose the coordinate direction
\mathbf{e}_{d}
(the argument works for any direction). Applying Proposition
2.1
with

(2.6)

W=2d+V(\omega)-\lambda,\ \frac{1}{\|W\|_{\infty}+2d-1}\geq\frac{1}{4d+h+\beta+\iota-1}\geq\gamma,

we can construct a chain of sites
n_{0},n_{1},n_{2},\cdots,n_{s}
such that

(2.7)

n_{j+1}\in\mathfrak{C}(n_{j},\mathbf{e}_{d})\cap B_{2}\ {\rm for}\ 0\leq j\leq s-1

and

(2.8)

\ |u(n_{j})|\geq\gamma^{j}|u(n_{0})|\ {\rm for}\ 0\leq j\leq s.

B_{1}
B_{2}
L
\ell
\bar{y}
n_{j}

Figure 2.
Visualization of the proof of Lemma
2.2
.

Regarding (
2.7
), if
n_{j}\in\partial^{-}B_{2}
, then the Dirichlet boundary condition ensures that
n_{j+1}
remains inside
B_{2}
. Moreover, (
2.7
) implies

(2.9)

1\leq\langle n_{j+1}-n_{j},\mathbf{e}_{d}\rangle\leq 2,

which gives
s\leq\langle n_{s}-n_{0},\mathbf{e}_{d}\rangle\leq L+\ell
. Consequently,

|u(n_{s})|\geq\gamma^{L+\ell}|u(n_{0})|.

The inequality (
2.9
) together with the Dirichlet boundary condition also guarantees that the terminal point
n_{s}
lies on
\partial^{-}B_{2}
in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{d}
. Taking
\bar{y}=n_{s}+\mathbf{e}_{d}
, we obtain

\mathcal{I}_{u}(\bar{y})=|u(n_{s})|\geq\gamma^{L+\ell}\cdot|u(n_{0})|.

∎

The proof of Lemma
2.2
reveals that the cone property essentially furnishes a transversality estimate only along a one-dimensional subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}

. This is somewhat elementary, as one typically expects transversality estimates to hold over
a larger portion of the lattice
. We therefore proceed to present several unique continuation results, which yield stronger transversality estimates.

2.2.
The unique continuation results

Unique continuation results were first employed by Bourgain
[
BK05
]
to derive the transversality estimates required for proving Anderson localization in Anderson-Bernoulli models defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In the continuous setting, solutions to Equation (
2.1
)—where the discrete operator is replaced by the standard Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
—satisfy the following lower bound

\sup_{|x-y|\leq 1}|u(y)|\geq c^{\prime}\exp\{-c^{\prime}|x|^{\frac{4}{3}}\log|x|\}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
|x|>R
, where the constants
c^{\prime},R>0
depend only on
\|W\|_{\infty}
and
d
(cf.
[
BK05
, Lemma 3.10]
). In other words, transversality estimate holds “almost everywhere” on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

However, in the discrete setting (on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
), several examples (e.g.,
[
Jit07
, Theorem 2]
and
[
BLMS22
, Remark 1.3]
) demonstrate that deterministic transversality estimates cannot be expected to hold over a full-dimensional subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. The existing results in this framework can be summarized as follows:

For the one-dimensional case on
\mathbb{Z}
, the unique continuation property is deterministic and follows directly from the cone property.

Theorem 2.3

(
A deterministic unique continuation result on
\mathbb{Z}
).

Let
u
satisfy (
2.3
) in an interval
Q_{L}(j)\subset\mathbb{Z}
. Then there exists a subset
T\subset Q_{L}(j)
such that

(2.10)

\#T\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\#Q_{L}(j)

with

(2.11)

|u(n)|\geq\gamma^{L}\cdot|u(j)|\ {\rm for}\ \forall n\in T.

For the case of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, a unique continuation result was first established for the harmonic equation
\Delta u=0
(i.e.,
W\equiv 0
) in
[
BLMS22
]
. Building on this work, Ding and Smart adapted the methodology of
[
BLMS22
]
to prove a probabilistic unique continuation result on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
(cf. (
2.12
)), which enabled them to establish localization for the ABM on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}

[
DS20
]
. Later, Li
[
Li22
]
refined the result of
[
DS20
]
by leveraging the Bourgain-Tzafriri restricted invertibility theory. A minor adaptation of the proofs from
[
Li22
,
DS20
]
to our hierarchical ABM framework yields the following result:

Theorem 2.4

(
A probabilistic unique continuation result on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
).

For every small
\varepsilon>0
, there exists some
C_{1}\gg 1
(depending only on
h,\varepsilon,\beta
) such that if

(1)

Q\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
a block of length
L>C_{1}
,

(2)

Q^{\prime}\subset Q
a block of length
\ell<\varepsilon^{2}L
,

(3)

\lambda_{0}\in[-1,9+\beta]
,

(4)

\mathcal{E}_{uc}^{\varepsilon,L_{0}}(Q,Q^{\prime},\lambda_{0})
denotes the event that

\begin{cases}|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|\leq\exp\{-C_{1}\cdot(L\log L)\}\\
H(\omega)u-\lambda u=0\ {\rm in}\ Q\\
\end{cases}

implies there exists a subset
T\subset Q\setminus Q^{\prime}
, such that

(2.12)

\#T\geq\varepsilon^{3}\cdot L^{2}

and

(2.13)

|u(n)|\geq\exp\{-C_{1}\cdot(L\log L)\}\cdot\|u\|_{\ell^{\infty}(Q^{\prime})}\ {\rm for}\ \forall n\in T,

then we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{uc}^{\varepsilon,L_{0}}(Q,Q^{\prime},\lambda_{0})|V_{\text{r},Q^{\prime}})\geq 1-\exp\{-\varepsilon L^{\frac{2}{3}}\}
. Here
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|\cdot)
means the condition probability on
V_{\text{r},Q^{\prime}}
with
V_{\text{r},Q^{\prime}}
denoting the random variables restricted in
Q^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{P}
being only about random variables in
Q
.

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

The proof of Theorem
2.4
follows from the argument in Chapter 5 of
[
Li22
]
, with two key adaptations. First, the upper bound in
[
Li22
, (5.13)]
is replaced by
(30+h+2\beta)^{(t-2)\vee 0}
. Second, our potential
V(\omega)
is defined by (
1.5
), and consequently the distance appearing in
[
Li22
, (5.76)]
becomes

\displaystyle\ \ \ {\rm dist}(P_{\mathcal{S}_{0}}(\vec{V}_{\text{hi},t^{\prime}}+\beta\vec{V}_{\text{r},t^{\prime}}),(M_{S_{0}}A_{S_{0}})^{-1}(\Gamma-v^{*}|_{S_{0}}))

\displaystyle={\rm dist}(P_{\mathcal{S}_{0}}(\beta\vec{V}_{\text{r},t^{\prime}}),(M_{S_{0}}A_{S_{0}})^{-1}(\Gamma-v^{*}|_{S_{0}})-P_{\mathcal{S}_{0}}(\vec{V}_{\text{hi},t^{\prime}})).

Geometrically, this means that the affine space
(M_{S_{0}}A_{S_{0}})^{-1}(\Gamma-v^{*}|_{S_{0}})
is translated by the deterministic vector
P_{\mathcal{S}0}(\vec{V}_{\text{hi},t^{\prime}})
. Since the probabilistic estimate in
[
Li22
, Lemma 1.6]
depends only on the dimension of this affine space, it remains valid.

According to the above discussion, Theorem
2.4
follows immediately once we verify that
Q^{\prime}
is
\varepsilon
-regular in
Q
(see Condition 2 of
[
Li22
, Lemma 3.5]
). For the definitions of “
\varepsilon
-sparse” and “
\varepsilon
-regular”, we refer to Definitions 3.1–3.4 in
[
DS20
]
.

Suppose that
Q^{\prime}
is not
\varepsilon
-sparse in some tilted square
R
. By definition, this implies that the side length of
R
must be less than
\varepsilon^{-1}\ell
and that
R
intersects with
Q^{\prime}
. Consequently,
R
must be contained in the
10\varepsilon^{-1}\ell
-neighborhood of
Q^{\prime}
, which is denoted by
\widetilde{Q}^{\prime}
.

Now, let
R_{1},\cdots,R_{k}\subset Q
be a collection of disjoint tilted squares, in each of which
Q^{\prime}
is not
\varepsilon
-sparse. Since they are disjoint and are all contained in
\widetilde{Q}^{\prime}
, we have

\sum_{s}\#R_{s}\leq\#\widetilde{Q}^{\prime}\leq(20\varepsilon^{-1}+1)^{2}\ell^{2}.

Using the assumption
\ell\leq\varepsilon^{2}L
and taking
\varepsilon>0
to be sufficiently small, we obtain

(20\varepsilon^{-1}+1)^{2}\ell^{2}\leq\varepsilon L^{2}=\varepsilon\cdot\#Q.

Thus,
Q^{\prime}
is
\varepsilon
-sparse in
Q
and we finish the proof.
∎

For the case of
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, Li and Zhang
[
LZ22
]
used the cone property and the geometric structure of
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
to prove the following unique continuation result.

Theorem 2.5

(
A deterministic unique continuation result on
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
, cf. Theorem 1.3 in
[
LZ22
]
)
.

There exists a constant
p>\frac{3}{2}
such that the following holds. Let
u
satisfy (
2.3
) in a block
Q_{L}(j)\subset\mathbb{Z}
. Then there is some
C_{1}>0
(depending only on
h,\beta
) such that, for all
L\geq C_{1}
, there exists a subset
T\subset Q_{L}(j)
satisfying

(2.14)

\#T\geq L^{p}

so that

(2.15)

|u(n)|\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L\}\cdot|u(j)|\ {\rm for}\ \forall n\in T.

While the aforementioned unique continuation results have been established for
d=1,2,3
, they do not ensure that the transversal set
T
in (
2.14
) is of full dimension when
d=3
. In the present work, we prove that for
d\geq 3
, transversality holds on a full-dimensional subset with high probability, where the probability in question depends on the initial data.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
d\geq 2
. Consider a solution
u
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
to the equation

H(\omega)u-\lambda u=0,

subject to the boundary condition
u\equiv u_{0}
on the union of two hyperplanes
\mathcal{P}_{0}\cup\mathcal{P}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{k}=\{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ n_{d}=k\}
.

Then there exist some
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(d)>0
and some
C_{1}=C_{1}(h,\beta,d)\gg 1
with the following property: For any
L\geq C_{1}
, we define
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{uc}}(L,u_{0},\lambda)
to be the event that there exists a subset
T\subset Q_{L}(0)
satisfying

(2.16)

\#T\geq\varepsilon\cdot L^{d}

so that

(2.17)

|u(n)|\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L\}\cdot|u(0)|\ {\rm for}\ \forall n\in T.

Then we have

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{uc}}(L,u_{0},\lambda)\big)\geq 1-\exp\{-\varepsilon L\},

where
\mathbb{P}
only depends on random variables in
Q_{L}(0)
.

The proof of Theorem
2.6
bears strong similarity to that of the high-dimensional Wegner estimate in Section
4
, as both rely on a martingale argument grounded in the cone property—an approach we deem to be of independent interest. For this reason, we defer the detailed proof of Theorem
2.6
to Appendix
A
.

We remark that, unlike Theorem
2.4
, the probabilistic estimate in Theorem
2.6
depends on the initial data
u_{0}
. However, for the special case
d=2
, Theorem
2.6
yields an exponential tail bound for the probabilistic estimate, in contrast to the sub-exponential bound furnished by Theorem
2.4
.

This observation naturally gives rise to the following open problem:

Problem 2.7
.

Can we obtain a probabilistic estimate that is independent of the initial data in Theorem
2.6
, while preserving the essential bounds given in (
2.16
) and (
2.17
)?

A positive solution to Problem
2.7
would likely pave the way for a proof of Anderson localization at the bottom of the spectrum for the standard ABM on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}\ (d\geq 4)
.

3.
Wegner estimate on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
d=1,2,3

In this section, we establish the key Wegner estimate for dimensions
1\leq d\leq 3
, —a result that plays a central role in the proof of Anderson localization. Our approach integrates the unique continuation results presented in Theorems
2.3
,
2.4
, and
2.5
with the free-sites argument originally developed in
[
Bou04
]
(see
[
BK05
,
DS20
]
for subsequent generalizations).

3.1.
Preliminary lemmas

In this part, we make no restriction on the dimension
d
. From now on and for simplicity, we omit the explicit dependence of
H(\omega)
on the randomness
\omega
when no confusion arises. For a subset
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, the corresponding Green’s function on
\Lambda
is defined by

G_{\Lambda}(E)=(H_{\Lambda}-E)^{-1},\ E\in\mathbb{R}.

We say that a finite set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is non-resonant if it is contained within the barrier region, i.e.,

\Lambda\subset\mathcal{B}=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ V_{\rm hi}(x)=h\}.

Recall that we have taken
\iota
(which essentially depends on
d,h,\beta
) to be (
2.2
).

For each non-resonant set, we have

Lemma 3.1
.

If
\Lambda
is non-resonant, then for any
E\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
, we have

(3.1)

\displaystyle\|G_{\Lambda}(E)\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{h-4d-\beta-\iota},

(3.2)

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(x,y;E)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{h-4d-\beta-\iota}\cdot\exp\{-\gamma_{0}|x-y|_{1}\}\ {\rm for}\ \forall x,y\in\Lambda,

where
\gamma_{0}=\log(1+\frac{h-4d-\beta-\iota}{2d})>0
.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

This proof is based on the Neumann series argument. Indeed,
expanding the Green’s function by the Neumann series, we get

\displaystyle G_{\Lambda}(E)

\displaystyle=(2d-E+V_{\Lambda}-\Delta_{\Lambda})^{-1}

(3.3)

\displaystyle=(V_{\Lambda}+2d-E)^{-1}\sum_{s\geq 0}\left(\Delta_{\Lambda}(V_{\Lambda}+2d-E)^{-1}\right).

Since
\Lambda
is non-resonant and
E\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
, we have

\|(V_{\Lambda}+2d-E)^{-1}\|=\|(\beta V_{\text{r},\Lambda}+h+2d-E)^{-1}\|\leq\frac{1}{h+2d-E}\leq\frac{1}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}.

Since
\|\Delta_{\Lambda}\|\leq 2d
, we obtain

\|G_{\Lambda}(E)\|\leq\frac{\|(V_{\Lambda}+2d-E)^{-1}\|}{1-2d\|(V_{\Lambda}+2d-E)^{-1}\|}\leq\frac{1}{h-4d-\beta-\iota}.

For the off-diagonal decay estimate, we have

\displaystyle G_{\Lambda}(x,y;E)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\rm path}\ g=(x_{0}=x,x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}=y)\subset\Lambda}\frac{1}{V_{x_{0}}+2d-E}\cdot\frac{1}{V_{x_{1}}+2d-E}\cdots\frac{1}{V_{x_{s}}+2d-E}.

The sum runs over all paths
g=(x_{0}=x,x_{1},\cdots,x_{s}=y)
from
x
to
y
with
x_{k-1}\sim x_{k}
. The number of such paths of length
s
is at most
(2d)^{s}
, since each site in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
has
2d
neighbors. Moreover, any such path must have length at least
|x-y|_{1}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(x,y;E)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s\geq|x-y|_{1}}(2d)^{s}\left(\frac{1}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{s+1}=\frac{1}{h-4d-\beta-\iota}\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{|x-y|_{1}},

which implies (
3.2
).
∎

Now recall the notations
\Lambda_{k},C_{k}^{s},\Lambda_{k}(j_{s})
from (
1.2
), Definition
1.1
and Definition
1.2
. Denote by
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
and
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}
the
\frac{1}{10}d_{k+1}
-neighborhood of
\Lambda_{k}
and
\Lambda_{k}(j_{s})
(for
s\geq 2
), respectively.

The following lemma gives an upper bound on the number of eigenvalues of
H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
in the low energy region.

Lemma 3.2
.

There is a scale
k^{(1)}
depending only on
d,\beta,h,\alpha,d_{0}
such that for all
k\geq k^{(1)}
, we have

\#\left({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\cap[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]\right)\leq 2N^{k-k^{(1)}}(16d_{k^{(1)}}+1)^{d}.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

Let
k\geq k^{(1)}
and let
E\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
be an eigenvalue of
H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
with a corresponding normalized eigenfunction
\psi_{E}
. Denote by
U_{k,k^{(1)}}
the union of all
\Lambda_{k^{(1)}}(j_{s})\subset\Lambda_{k}
at the
k^{(1)}
-th hierarchical level, and let
\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}}
be the
2d_{k^{(1)}}
-neighborhood of
U_{k,k^{(1)}}
.
We have

(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}-E)=(H_{U_{k,k^{(1)}}}-E)\oplus(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus U_{k,k^{(1)}}}-E)+\Gamma,

where
\Gamma
is the connecting operator
U_{k,k^{(1)}}
and
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus U_{k,k^{(1)}}
via
-\Delta
. Applying the Poisson’s formula yields

\psi_{E}=-\left(G_{U_{k,k^{(1)}}}(E)\oplus G_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus U_{k,k^{(1)}}}(E)\right)\Gamma\psi_{E}.

Clearly,
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus U_{k,k^{(1)}}
is non-resonant. Applying Lemma
3.1
, we obtain that for every
x\in\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}},

\displaystyle|\psi_{E}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{w\in\partial^{+}U_{k,k^{(1)}}\atop w^{\prime}\in\partial^{-}U_{k,k^{(1)}},\ w\sim w^{\prime}}\left|G_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus U_{k,k^{(1)}}}(x,w;E)\right|\cdot|\psi_{E}(w^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2d}{h-4d-\beta-\iota}\cdot\sum_{w\in\partial^{+}U_{k,k^{(1)}}}\exp\{-\gamma_{0}|x-w|_{1}\}\lesssim_{d,h,\beta}\sum_{s\geq{\rm dist}(x,U_{k,k^{(1)}})}s^{d-1}\exp\{-\gamma_{0}s\}

(3.4)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}\cdot{\rm dist}(x,U_{k,k^{(1)}})\}.

From (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle\|\psi_{E}\|_{\ell^{2}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}}}\exp\{-\gamma_{0}\cdot{\rm dist}(x,U_{k,k^{(1)}})\}

(3.5)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-\gamma_{0}d_{k^{(1)}}\}.

Indeed, (
3.1
) also means that

(3.6)

\|\psi_{E}\|_{\ell^{2}(\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}})}^{2}\geq 1-\exp\{-\gamma_{0}d_{k^{(1)}}\}\geq\frac{1}{2}.

The estimates (
3.1
), (
3.1
) and (
3.6
) remain valid provided
{\rm dist}(x,U_{k,k^{(1)}})\geq 2d_{k^{(1)}}
is chosen sufficiently large, or for
k^{(1)}
sufficiently large (depending only on
d,h,\beta,\alpha,d_{0}
). Combining those estimates together, we can apply the following Hilbert-Schmidt argument:

\displaystyle\#\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}}}\|\delta_{x}\|^{2}\geq\sum_{x\in\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}}}\sum_{E\in{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\cap[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]}|\psi_{E}(x)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{E\in{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\cap[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]}\|\psi_{E}\|_{\ell^{2}(\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}})}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\#\left({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\cap[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]\right),

which yields

\#\left({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\cap[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]\right)\leq 2\#\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}}\leq 2N^{k-k^{(1)}}(16d_{k^{(1)}}+1)^{d}.

Here, we used the fact that there are at most
N^{k-k^{(1)}}
-many
\Lambda_{k^{(1)}}(j_{s})\subset\Lambda_{k}
at the
k^{(1)}
-th hierarchical level, and the
2d_{k^{(1)}}
-extension of each
\Lambda_{k^{(1)}}(j_{s})
has its cardinality less than
(16d_{k^{(1)}}+1)^{d}
.

∎

Remark 3.1
.

Indeed, if
\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}
denotes the
2d_{k}
-neighborhood of
\Lambda_{k}
, then the similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
3.2
will lead to

\#\left({\rm Spec}(H_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}})\cap[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]\right)\leq 2\#\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}}\leq 2N^{k-k^{(1)}}(16d_{k^{(1)}}+1)^{d}.

3.2.
Proof of the Wegner estimate for
d=1,2,3

Now let
1\leq d\leq 3
. The remaining part of this section is devoted to proving the following Wegner estimate.

Theorem 3.3

(
Wegner estimate for
d=1,2,3
).

Let
d=1,2,3
. For any
0<\beta\leq 1
, there exist some
q>0
,
\varepsilon>0
and
k_{\text{in}}>0
(all of them depend only on
h,\beta,\alpha,d_{0},N
) such that, for all
k\geq k_{\text{in}}
and
E\in[-\frac{\iota}{2},4d+\beta+\frac{\iota}{2}]
, we have

(3.7)

\mathbb{P}\left({\rm dist}\big({\rm Spec}(H_{\Lambda}),E\big)<\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}\right)\leq d_{k+1}^{-q},

where
\Lambda
can be either
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
or
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}
(with
s\geq 2
), and the probability
\mathbb{P}
is taken only over the random variables within
\Lambda
.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

Indeed, since each
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}
shares the same hierarchical structure as
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
, it suffices to analyze the case of
\Lambda=\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
.

Let
\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}
denote the
2d_{k}
-neighborhood of
\Lambda_{k}
. We define the set of frozen sites to be

\mathcal{F}=\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}

and the complementary set of
free sites
as

\mathcal{S}=\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}.

Choose
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
and
C_{1}\gg 1
(both depending only on
h,\alpha,\beta
) such that the unique continuation Theorems
2.3
,
2.4
and
2.5
are applicable, and in addition
(1-\varepsilon)(1-2\varepsilon)>1/\alpha
. Define the length scales

L_{0}={\rm diam}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}),\ L_{1}=L_{0}^{1-2\varepsilon},\ L_{2}=L_{1}^{1-2\varepsilon}.

With the choice of
\varepsilon
, we then have

(3.8)

{\rm diam}(\Lambda_{k}^{\prime})\sim d_{k}=d_{k+1}^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\sim L_{0}^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\ll L_{2}.

For
k\gg 1
, we have the following claim about the transversality estimate.

Claim 3.4
.

Denote by
\mathcal{E}_{uc}=\mathcal{E}_{uc}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k},E)
the event that the following holds: For any eigenvalue
\lambda
with
|\lambda-E|\leq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}
and any corresponding eigenfunction
\psi_{\lambda}
of
H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
, one can find a subset
T(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})\subset\mathcal{S}
on which
\psi_{\lambda}
is transversal, i.e.,

|\psi_{\lambda}(x)|\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}\|\psi_{\lambda}\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda_{k}^{\prime})}.

Moreover,

(3.9)

\#T(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})\geq N_{uc}:=\begin{cases}\varepsilon L_{2},\ d=1\\
\varepsilon^{3}L^{2}_{2},\ d=2\\
L_{2}^{p},\ d=3,\end{cases}

where
p>\frac{3}{2}
is given in Theorem
2.5
.
Then we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{uc}|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}})\geq 1-\exp\{-\varepsilon L^{\frac{2}{3}}_{2}\}.

Proof of Claim
3.4
.

Let
k\gg 1
be such that
L_{2}>C_{1}
.

For
d=1
, assume
\psi_{\lambda}
attains its maximum within
\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}
at
x_{0}
. Observe that by (
3.8
), we have

\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}\subset Q_{L_{2}-8d_{k}}(x_{0})\subset Q_{L_{2}}(0)\subset Q_{\frac{1}{2}L_{0}}(0)=\overline{\Lambda}_{k}.

Now we can apply Theorem
2.3
to the cube
Q_{L_{2}-8d_{k}}(x_{0})
. Deterministically, there exists a
\overline{T}(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})\subset Q_{L_{2}-8d_{k}}(x_{0})
such that

\#\overline{T}(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\#Q_{L_{2}-8d_{k}}(x_{0})=L_{2}-8d_{k},

and for every
x\in\overline{T}(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})
,

|\psi_{\lambda}(x)|\geq\gamma^{L_{2}-8d_{k}}|\psi_{\lambda}(x_{0})|\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}|\psi_{\lambda}(x_{0})|.

Finally, we take
T(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})=\overline{T}(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})\setminus\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{S}
, and therefore

\#T(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})\geq L_{2}-8d_{k}-\#\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}\geq\varepsilon L_{2}.

For
d=3
, assume again that
\psi_{\lambda}
attains its maximum within
\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}
at some
x_{0}
. We first apply cone property (Proposition
2.1
) to obtain a chain
(x_{0},n_{1},n_{2},\cdots,x^{\prime}_{0})
in the direction
\mathbf{e}_{d}
starting at
x_{0}
and terminating at
x^{\prime}_{0}
. For this chain, inequality (
2.9
) holds. Consequently, we may assume without loss of generality that
|x_{0}^{\prime}|\in\{2L_{2}+8d_{k},2L_{2}+8d_{k}-1\}
. The length of the chain is then at most
2L_{2}+16d_{0}
, and therefore

|\psi_{\lambda}(x_{0}^{\prime})|\geq\gamma^{2L_{2}+16d_{k}}|\psi_{\lambda}(x_{0})|.

Notice that
Q_{L_{2}}(x_{0}^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{S}
. Apply Theorem
2.5
to the cube
Q_{L_{2}}(x_{0}^{\prime})
. Deterministically, there exists a
T(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})\subset Q_{L_{2}}(x_{0}^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{S}
such that

\#T(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})\geq L_{2}^{p},

and for every
x\in T(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})
,

\displaystyle|\psi_{\lambda}(x)|

\displaystyle\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\}|\psi_{\lambda}(x_{0}^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\}\gamma^{2L_{2}+16d_{k}}|\psi_{\lambda}(x_{0})|\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}|\psi_{\lambda}(x_{0})|.

The argument as above for
d=1
and
d=3
is deterministic, that is, it holds for all
\omega\in\Omega
and all
\lambda\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
(which contains the interval
[-\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}+E,\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}+E]
, supposing
k\gg 1
). Consequently, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{uc}|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}})=1\geq 1-\exp\{-\varepsilon L^{\frac{2}{3}}_{2}\}.

For
d=2
, we have

\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}\subset Q_{\frac{1}{2}L_{2}}(0)\subset\overline{\Lambda}_{k}.

From (
3.8
), it follows that

{\rm diam}(\Lambda_{k}^{\prime})\leq 16d_{k}\leq\varepsilon^{2}L_{2}=\varepsilon^{2}\cdot{\rm diam}(Q_{\frac{1}{2}L_{2}}(0)).

Therefore, we can apply Theorem
2.4
to the block
Q_{\frac{1}{2}L_{2}}(0)
. This yields that for all eigenvalues
\lambda
satisfying

|\lambda-E|\leq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\},

there is a set
T(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})\subset Q_{\frac{1}{2}L_{2}}(0)\setminus\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{S}
such that

\#T(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})\geq\varepsilon^{3}L_{2}^{2},

and for every
x\in T(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})
,

\displaystyle|\psi_{\lambda}(x)|\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}\|\psi_{\lambda}\|_{\ell^{2}(\Lambda_{k}^{\prime})}.

Finally, Theorem
2.4
also provides the probability estimate

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{uc}|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}})\geq 1-\exp\{-\varepsilon L^{\frac{2}{3}}_{2}\}.

Combining the above discussions, we complete the proof of the Claim.
∎

With Claim
3.4
in hand, we can proceed with the proof of the Wegner estimate. Let

\lambda_{1}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\geq\lambda_{2}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{\#\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})

denote the eigenvalues of
H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
arranged in the non-increasing order, with corresponding normalized eigenfunctions
\{\psi_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq\#\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
.
For
1\leq j_{1},j_{2}\leq\#\overline{\Lambda}_{k}=L_{0}^{d}
and an integer
0\leq\ell\leq L_{0}^{\varepsilon}
, denote by
\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell}
the event that

|\lambda_{j_{1}}-E|\vee|\lambda_{j_{2}}-E|<s_{\ell}\ {\rm and}\ |\lambda_{j_{1}-1}-E|\wedge|\lambda_{j_{2}+1}-E|\geq s_{\ell},

where

s_{\ell}=\exp\{-L_{1}+(2C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}-2\gamma_{0}d_{k}+C_{2})\ell\}

with
C_{2}\gg 1
will be specified later. By our choice of
\ell
and (
3.8
), we ensure that

(3.10)

\displaystyle s_{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-L_{1}+(2C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}-2\gamma_{0}d_{k}+C_{2})\cdot L_{0}^{\varepsilon}\}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-L_{1}+2C_{1}L_{1}^{1-2\varepsilon+\frac{\varepsilon}{1-2\varepsilon}}\log L_{2}\}\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}L_{1}\}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}.

\lambda_{j_{1}-1}
\lambda_{j_{1}}
\lambda_{j_{2}}
\lambda_{j_{2}+1}
E
E+s_{\ell}
E+s_{\ell+1}

Figure 3.
The event
\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell}
.

From now on, we take condition probability on
V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}
. This leads to a cylindrical decomposition of the probability space:

\{0,1\}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}=\bigcup_{v\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{F}}}\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{S}}\times\{V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}=v\}:=\bigcup_{v\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{F}}}\mathcal{C}_{v}.

In this representation, both
\mathcal{E}_{uc}
and
\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell}
can be viewed as subsets of
\mathcal{C}_{v}
.

Recall the definition of
N_{uc}
given by (
3.9
). For
i=0,1
, let
\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,i}
denote the event that

\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell}\ {\rm and}\ \#\left(\left\{|\psi_{j_{1}}|\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}\|\psi_{j_{1}}\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda_{k}^{\prime})}\right\}\cap\{V_{\text{r},\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}=i\}\setminus\mathcal{F}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}N_{uc}.

By the pigeonhole principle, (
3.10
) and Claim
3.4
, we have

(3.11)

\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell}\cap\mathcal{E}_{uc}\subset\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,0}\cup\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,1}.

Now if
\omega\in\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,i},x\in\mathcal{S},\omega(x)=i
and

(3.12)

|\psi_{j_{1}}(x)|\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}\|\psi_{j_{1}}\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda_{k}^{\prime})},

then we change the value of
V_{\text{r}}
at
x
, i.e., take

\omega_{x}(y)=\begin{cases}\omega(y),\ {\rm if}\ y\neq x,\\
1-\omega(x),\ {\rm if}\ y=x.\end{cases}

Claim 3.5
.

\omega_{x}\notin\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,i}.

Proof of Claim
3.5
.

We only consider the case
i=0
, as the proof of the case
i=1
is analogous. Define the shifted operator

\widetilde{H}_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}(\omega)=H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}(\omega)-E+s_{\ell},

of which all eigenvalues are
\widetilde{\lambda}j=\lambda_{j}-E+s_{\ell}
(For
i=1
, the corresponding operator is
-(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}(\omega)-E-s_{\ell})
). Set

(3.13)

r_{1}=2s_{\ell},\ r_{2}=s_{\ell}+s_{\ell+1}.

Then since
\omega\in\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell}
, the following ordering holds true:

0<\widetilde{\lambda}_{j_{1}}\leq\widetilde{\lambda}_{j_{2}}<r_{1}<r_{2}<\widetilde{\lambda}_{j_{2}+1}.

Now recall the set
\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}}\subset\Lambda_{k}\subset\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}
defined in the proof of Lemma
3.2
.
Since
E\in[-\frac{\iota}{2},4d+\beta+\frac{\iota}{2}]
, and assuming
k\gg 1
such that

|E-\lambda_{j_{1}}|\leq s_{\ell}\leq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}\ll\frac{\iota}{2},

we have

(3.14)

\lambda_{j_{1}}\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota].

This implies that the estimate (
3.6
) is valid for
\psi_{j_{1}}
, and

\displaystyle\|\psi_{j_{1}}\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda_{k}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\geq\|\psi_{j_{1}}\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}})}\geq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\#\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}}}}\geq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2N^{k-k^{(1)}}(16d_{k^{(1)}}+1)^{d}}}

\displaystyle\geq L_{0}^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}\sim d_{0}^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\cdot\alpha^{k+1}},

where in the last inequality we used
k\gg 1
. Combining the above estimates with (
3.12
) yields

(3.15)

|\psi_{j_{1}}(x)|\geq L_{0}^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}\geq\exp\{-2C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}:=r_{3}.

Next, recalling (
3.10
), we set

(3.16)

r_{5}=2\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}+s_{\ell}>r_{2}.

Consider now a
j
such that
r_{2}<\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}<r_{5}
. Then
|\lambda_{j}-E|\leq r_{5}-s_{\ell}<r_{5}
.
By similar argument leading to (
3.14
), we get
\lambda_{j}\in{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\cap[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
.
Consequently, the estimate (
3.1
) is applicable to the eigenfunctions corresponding to such
\lambda_{j}
. Simultaneously, notice that
d(x,\widehat{U}_{k,k^{(1)}})\geq 2d_{k}
by
x\notin\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}
. We thus obtain

(3.17)

\sum_{j:r_{2}<\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}<r_{5}}|\psi_{j}(x)|^{2}\leq\#\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\cdot\exp\{-\gamma_{0}d_{k}\}\leq\exp\{-2\gamma_{0}d_{k}\}:=r_{4}.

The proof will be completed after applying the following lemma concerning rank-one perturbations, which is adapted from
[
DS20
, Lemma 5.1]
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Suppose that the real symmetric matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
has eigenvalues

\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{R}

with orthonormal eigenbasis
v_{1},v_{2},\cdots,v_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then for every
\beta>0
, there is some
0<c\ll 1
(depending only on
\beta
) such that, if

(1)

0<r_{1}<r_{2}<r_{3}<r_{4},r_{5}<1
,

(2)

r_{1}\leq c\min\{r_{3}r_{5},r_{2}r_{3}/r_{4}\}
,

(3)

0<\lambda_{j}\leq\lambda_{i}<r_{1}<r_{2}<\lambda_{i-1}
,

(4)

v_{j}^{2}(x)\geq r_{3}
,

(5)

\displaystyle\sum_{r_{2}<\lambda_{s}<r_{5}}v_{s}^{2}(x)\leq r_{4}
,

then

\operatorname{trace}\mathbf{1}_{[r_{1},\infty)}(A)<\operatorname{trace}\mathbf{1}_{[r_{1},\infty)}(A+\beta e_{x}\otimes e_{x}),

where
e_{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the
x
-th standard basis element.

Remark 3.2
.

In the continuous model on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
[
BK05
]
handled the rank-one perturbations on a free site by continuing the Bernoulli variable and using eigenvalue variations. This approach seems invalid in the discrete setting, because the transversal set
T(\omega,\psi_{\lambda})
is not the whole space, but depends on
\omega
, which cannot allow to take differentiability.
Furthermore, we note that the assumption
r_{4}<r_{5}
in
[
DS20
, Lemma 5.1]
is, in fact, unnecessary as seen from the proof. We have therefore omitted it here.

Having established Lemma
3.6
, we recall the parameters
r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},r_{4},r_{5}
defined in (
3.13
), (
3.15
), (
3.17
) and (
3.16
). To verify the hypothesis of Lemma
3.6
, we compute

r_{2}r_{3}/r_{4}=r_{1}\frac{1+\exp\{2C_{2}L_{2}\log L_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{k}+C_{2}\}}{2}\cdot\exp\{-2C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}+2\gamma_{0}d_{k}\}\geq\frac{e^{C_{2}}}{2}r_{1}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\min\{r_{3}r_{5},r_{2}r_{3}/r_{4}\}

\displaystyle\geq\min\{2\exp\{-3C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\},\frac{e^{C_{2}}}{2}r_{1}\}>cr_{1}.

Here we choose
C_{2}\gg 1
and use estimate (
3.10
), which guarantees that for
k\gg 1
,

r_{1}\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}L_{1}\}\ll 2\exp\{-3C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}.

Therefore, Lemma
3.6
is applicable to the rank-one perturbation

\widetilde{H}_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}(\omega_{x})=\widetilde{H}_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}(\omega)+\beta e_{x}\otimes e_{x},

yielding

\operatorname{trace}\mathbf{1}_{[r_{1},\infty)}(\widetilde{H}_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}(\omega))<\operatorname{trace}\mathbf{1}_{[r_{1},\infty)}(\widetilde{H}_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}(\omega_{x})).

This inequality is precisely equivalent to
\lambda_{j_{2}}(\omega_{x})>s_{\ell+1}
. Hence,
\omega_{x}\notin\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell}
, and we obtain

\omega_{x}\notin\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,0}\subset\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell}.

∎

Indeed, Claim
3.5
holds in a more general sense. Recall the event
\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,i}
. Define

T^{\prime}(\omega,\psi_{j_{1}},i)=\left\{|\psi_{j_{1}}|\geq\exp\{-C_{1}L_{2}\log L_{2}\}\|\psi_{j_{1}}\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda_{k}^{\prime})}\right\}\cap\{V_{\text{r},\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}=i\}\setminus\mathcal{F}.

Now suppose
\omega\in\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,i}
and
\widetilde{\omega}
is obtained from
\omega
by modifying it only on the sites in
T^{\prime}(\omega,\psi_{j_{1}},i)
, i.e.,

\{y:\ \omega(y)\neq\widetilde{\omega}(y)\}\subset T^{\prime}(\omega,\psi_{j_{1}},i).

We still restrict our attention to the case
i=0
. Choose an arbitrary site
x\in{y:\ \omega(y)\neq\widetilde{\omega}(y)}
. The proof of Claim
3.5
shows that
\lambda_{j_{2}}(\omega_{x})>s_{\ell+1}
. Now, by the monotonicity of eigenvalues under positive perturbations, we have

\lambda_{j_{2}}(\widetilde{\omega})>\lambda_{j_{2}}(\omega_{x})>s_{\ell+1},

which again implies

(3.18)

\widetilde{\omega}\notin\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,0}.

If we identify a
\omega
with the subset of
\mathcal{S}
given by

\omega\Leftrightarrow\{x\in\mathcal{S}:\ \omega(x)=1\},

then (
3.18
) precisely means that
\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,0}
is a
\rho
-Sperner family of
\mathcal{S}
(for the definition of
\rho
-Sperner, see
[
DS20
, Definition 4.1]
), where

(3.19)

\rho=\frac{\frac{1}{2}N_{uc}}{\#\mathcal{S}}.

Thus, using
[
DS20
, Theorem 4.2]
enables us to get

(3.20)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,0}|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}})\leq\left(\#\mathcal{S}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\rho^{-1}=\frac{2\sqrt{\#\mathcal{S}}}{N_{uc}}.

For
i=1
, if we identify
\omega
with

\omega\Leftrightarrow\{x\in\mathcal{S}:\ \omega(x)=0\},

then a similar argument will yield

(3.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,1}|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}})\leq\left(\#\mathcal{S}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\rho^{-1}=\frac{2\sqrt{\#\mathcal{S}}}{N_{uc}}.

Finally, the proof of Theorem
3.3
can be completed once we established the following claim.

Claim 3.7
.

There exists a subset
\mathcal{K}\subset\{1,2,\cdots,\#\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\}
depending only on the randomness in the frozen sites set
\mathcal{F}
such that,
\#\mathcal{K}\leq L_{0}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}
and

\{\omega:\ {\rm dist}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}},E)<\exp\{-L_{1}\}\}\cap\{V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}=v\}\subset\bigcup_{j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathcal{K}\atop 0\leq\ell\leq L_{0}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell}.

Indeed, supposing Claim
3.7
holds true, then by Claim
3.4
, together with (
3.11
), (
3.19
) and (
3.21
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\rm dist}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}},E)<\exp\{-L_{1}\}|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{uc}^{c}|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}})+\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcup_{j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathcal{K}\atop 0\leq\ell\leq L_{0}^{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell}\bigg|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-\varepsilon L_{2}^{\frac{2}{3}}\}+\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathcal{K}\atop 0\leq\ell\leq L_{0}^{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell}\cap\mathcal{E}_{uc}|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-\varepsilon L_{2}^{\frac{2}{3}}\}+\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathcal{K}\atop 0\leq\ell\leq L_{0}^{\varepsilon}}\sum_{i=0,1}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell,i}|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}\right)

(3.22)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-\varepsilon L_{2}^{\frac{2}{3}}\}+(\#\mathcal{K})^{2}L_{0}^{\varepsilon}\frac{4\sqrt{\#\mathcal{S}}}{N_{uc}}.

Recall that
N_{uc}
is given in (
3.9
). Consequently,

\mathbb{P}\left({\rm dist}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}},E)<\exp\{-L_{1}\}|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}\right)\leq\begin{cases}8\varepsilon^{-1}L_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon}L_{2}^{-1},\ d=1,\\
8\varepsilon^{-3}L_{0}^{1+2\varepsilon}L_{2}^{-2},\ d=2,\\
8L_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}+2\varepsilon}L_{2}^{-p},\ d=3.\end{cases}

This implies

\mathbb{P}\left({\rm dist}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}},E)<\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}|V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}\right)\leq L_{0}^{-q^{\prime}}\leq d_{k+1}^{-q}

for some
q,q^{\prime}>0
. Finally, applying the law of total expectation can remove the conditioning, and then yield (
3.7
). The entire proof is conducted under the assumption that
k\gg 1
. We choose
k_{\text{in}}
to be large enough (depending only on
h,\beta,\alpha,d_{0},N
) to ensure the validity of the proof provided
k\geq k_{\text{in}}
.

∎

Remark 3.3
.

From (
3.2
), it is clear that for the above argument to hold in more general dimensions
d\geq 4
, one needs to prove in the unique continuation lemma a lower bound of
N_{uc}\gtrsim L^{\frac{d}{2}+}
for the number of sites where the transversality occurs. This remains unsettled.

Finally, we prove Claim
3.7
, thus finish this section.

Proof of Claim
3.7
.

The proof is based on a bootstrap estimate first introduced in
[
Bou04
]
, and subsequently employed in
[
BK05
,
DS20
,
LZ22
,
Li22
,
LSZ25b
]
.

Now, for each
v\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{F}}
, we take probability condition on the event
\{V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}=v\}
. The remaining randomness is then denoted by
\zeta\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{S}}
. We define

(3.23)

\mathcal{K}(v)=\left\{j:\ \exists\zeta\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{S}}\ {\rm such\ that}\ |\lambda_{j}(\zeta,v)-E|\leq\exp\{-L_{2}\}\right\}.

Since
V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}\equiv v
has been fixed, we omit the dependence of
\lambda_{j}
on
v
for simplicity. The function
\lambda_{j}(\zeta)
can be naturally extended to a continuous domain by allowing the variable
\zeta
to vary in
[0,1]^{\mathcal{S}}
instead of
\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{S}}
.

By the first-order eigenvalue variation formula, for every
x\in\mathcal{S}
,

(3.24)

\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\zeta(x)}\lambda_{j}(\zeta)\right|=\beta|\psi_{j}(\zeta)(x)|^{2}\leq 1.

This derivation is valid because the Kato–Rellich theorem guarantees the analyticity of
\lambda_{j}(\zeta)
with respective to each single coordinate
\zeta(x)
. Applying the mean value theorem yields

(3.25)

\left|\lambda_{j}(\zeta_{1})-\lambda(\zeta_{2})\right|\leq|\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2}|_{1}\leq\#\mathcal{S}\cdot\|\zeta_{1}-\zeta_{2}\|_{\infty}.

Now for any eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}(\zeta),\zeta\in[0,1]^{\mathcal{S}}
, if

(3.26)

|\lambda-E|\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{k}\},

then a similar argument leading to (
3.14
) will ensure
\lambda_{j}(\zeta)\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
. Then the estimate (
3.1
) will be valid, implying

(3.27)

|\psi_{j}(\zeta)(x)|\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}\cdot{\rm dist}(x,U_{k,k^{(1)}})\}\leq\exp\{-\gamma_{0}d_{k}\}

for every
x\in\mathcal{S}
.

For any
j\in\mathcal{K}
, by our definition (
3.23
), there exists a
\zeta_{0}\in[0,1]^{\mathcal{S}}
such that

|\lambda_{j}(\zeta_{0})-E|\leq\exp\{-L_{2}\}.

Pave
[0,1]^{\mathcal{S}}
with
e^{-100L_{2}}
-size cubes (in the supremum norm) and assume
\zeta_{0}\in B
(
B
is of
\exp\{-100L_{2}\}
-size). Thus for all
\zeta\in B,
we can apply (
3.25
) to control

\displaystyle|\lambda_{j}(\zeta)-E|

\displaystyle\leq|E-\lambda_{j}(\zeta_{0})|+|\lambda_{j}(\zeta_{0})-\lambda_{j}(\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-L_{2}\}+L_{0}^{d}\cdot\|\zeta-\zeta_{0}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-L_{2}\}+L_{0}^{d}\exp\{-100L_{2}\}\ll\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{k}\}.

This implies (
3.26
) holds for all
\zeta\in B
, and therefore (
3.27
) is valid in the whole
B
, which gives us the refined estimate on the derivative

(3.28)

\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\zeta(x)}\lambda_{j}(\zeta)\right|=\beta|\psi_{j}(\zeta)(x)|^{2}\leq\exp\{-2\gamma_{0}d_{k}\}.

Apply (
3.28
), instead of (
3.24
), in the mean value theorem. We obtain

\displaystyle|\lambda_{j}(\zeta)-E|\leq\exp\{-L_{2}\}+L_{0}^{d}\exp\{-2\gamma_{0}d_{k}\}\cdot\|\zeta-\zeta_{0}\|_{\infty}

holds for any
\zeta\in B
. We can propagate the above estimate iteratively over the entire
[0,1]^{\mathcal{S}}
via the
\exp\{-100L_{2}\}
-size pavements, and finally deduce that

\displaystyle|\lambda_{j}(\zeta)-E|

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-L_{2}\}+L_{0}^{d}\exp\{-2\gamma_{0}d_{k}\}\cdot\|\zeta-\zeta_{0}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-L_{2}\}+L_{0}^{d}\exp\{-2\gamma_{0}d_{k}\}\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{k}\}

holds for any
\zeta\in[0,1]^{\mathcal{S}}
. Again, by a similar argument leading to (
3.14
), we obtain

\lambda_{j}(\zeta)\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]\ {\rm for}\ \forall j\in\mathcal{K}(v),\ \forall\zeta\in[0,1]^{\mathcal{S}}.

Therefore, we can apply Lemma
3.2
to deduce that

\#\mathcal{K}(v)\leq\#\left({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\cap[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]\right)\leq 2N^{k-k^{(1)}}(16d_{k^{(1)}}+1)^{d}\ll L_{0}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}},

supposing that
k\gg 1
.

Finally, suppose
\zeta\in\{\omega:\ {\rm dist}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}},E)<\exp\{-L_{1}\}\}\cap\{V_{\text{r},\mathcal{F}}=v\}
. Then there exists an eigenvalue
\lambda_{j_{0}}(\zeta)
satisfying

|\lambda_{j_{0}}(\zeta)-E|<d\exp\{-L_{1}\}\leq\exp\{-L_{2}\}.

Therefore, by our definition of
\mathcal{K}(v)
, we have
j\in\mathcal{K}(v)
. Recall that we let
0\leq\ell\leq L_{0}^{\varepsilon}
and
s_{0}=\exp\{-L_{1}\}
. Thus the interval
(E-s_{0},E+s_{0})
contains
\lambda_{j_{0}}(\zeta)
. Moreover, since

\#\mathcal{K}(v)\leq L_{0}^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\ll L_{0}^{\varepsilon},

there must be some
0\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq L_{0}^{\varepsilon}
such that

\big((E-s_{\ell^{\prime}+1},E-s_{\ell^{\prime}}]\cup[E+s_{\ell^{\prime}},E+s_{\ell^{\prime}+1})\big)\cap\{\lambda_{j}(\zeta):\ j-1\ {\rm or}\ j\ {\rm or}\ j+1\in\mathcal{K}(v)\}=\emptyset.

Now from
\lambda_{j_{0}}(\zeta)\in(E-s_{0},E+s_{0})
, it follows that

\{j\in\mathcal{K}(v):\ E-s_{\ell^{\prime}}<\lambda_{j}(\zeta)<E+s_{\ell^{\prime}}\}\neq\emptyset.

Therefore, we can define

\displaystyle j_{1}=\min\{j\in\mathcal{K}(v):\ E-s_{\ell^{\prime}}<\lambda_{j}(\zeta)<E+s_{\ell^{\prime}}\},

\displaystyle j_{2}=\max\{j\in\mathcal{K}(v):\ E-s_{\ell^{\prime}}<\lambda_{j}(\zeta)<E+s_{\ell^{\prime}}\},

and then
\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell^{\prime}}
happens. Hence
\zeta\in\mathcal{E}_{j_{1},j_{2},\ell^{\prime}}
, which completes the proof.

∎

4.
Wegner estimate on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for
d\geq 4

In this section, we establish a Wegner estimate in higher dimensions (
d\geq 4
). For this higher-dimensional setting, we do not resort to results pertaining to unique continuation arguments; instead, we rely solely on the transversality estimate obtained on one-dimensional subsets, as furnished by the cone property (Lemma
2.2
). Although the transversality in this framework is weakened—manifesting specifically in the relatively small number of points where transversality holds—we can suppress the quantum tunneling effect of particles by tuning the height and width of the hierarchical potential barriers. This adjustment enhances the transversality at individual points, ultimately offsetting the substantial loss of transversality inherent to the higher-dimensional lattice.

The proof of the higher-dimensional Wegner estimate draws substantial inspiration from the eigenvalue shift analysis presented in
[
Imb21
]
. The methodology in
[
Imb21
]
demonstrates that transversality at a single point can displace at least one eigenvalue far from the target energy
E
, with such a shift occurring over a dyadic scale. However, in the multi-scale analysis employed previously, we have established that the scales typically encountered are Newtonian scales. A key insight underpinning the present proof of the higher-dimensional Wegner estimate is therefore the interpolation of
a
-adic scales between consecutive Newtonian scales, which enables us to derive estimates of higher precision. At the final stage of establishing the probabilistic bounds, we uncover an important martingale structure. In summary, the derivation of the Wegner estimate on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for
d\geq 4
constitutes the most critical novel contribution of this work.

The proof of the higher-dimensional Wegner estimate is largely inspired by the analysis of eigenvalue movement presented in
[
Imb21
]
. The technique of
[
Imb21
]
demonstrates that the transversality at a single point can move at least one eigenvalue far away from the energy
E
, and such movement happens under a dyadic scale. However, in the multi-scale analysis employed earlier, we have shown that the scales that we typically encounter are
Newtonian scales
. Therefore, a key idea in the present proof for higher dimensional Wegner estimate is to interpolate
a
-adic scales between two Newtonian scales, thereby obtaining estimates of higher precision. At the final stage of proving the probabilistic estimate, we discover an important martingales structure. In a word, the proof of Wegner estimate on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for
d\geq 4
is the most important new ingredient of our work.

We still use the notation
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
and
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}
to stand for the
\frac{1}{10}d_{k+1}
-neighborhood of
\Lambda_{k}
and
\Lambda_{k}(j_{s})
(for
s\geq 2
), respectively. The Wegner estimate in higher dimensions is

Theorem 4.1

(
Wegner estimate for
d\geq 4
).

Let
d\geq 4
. For any
0<\beta\leq 1
, there is some
h_{0}=h_{0}(d)\gg 1
such that, if
h>h_{0},\alpha>N
, then the following holds ture: There exist some
q>0
,
\varepsilon>0
and
k_{\text{in}}
(all of them only depending on
h,\beta,\alpha,d_{0},N
) such that, for all
k\geq k_{\text{in}}
and all
E\in[-\frac{\iota}{2},4d+\beta+\frac{\iota}{2}]
, we have

(4.1)

\mathbb{P}\left({\rm dist}\big({\rm Spec}(H_{\Lambda}),E\big)<\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}\right)\leq d_{k+1}^{-q},

where
{\Lambda}
can be either
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
or
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}
(with
s\geq 2
), and the probability
\mathbb{P}
is taken only over the random variables within
\Lambda
.

Again, because the sets
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}
share the same hierarchical structure as
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
, we only focus on the case of
\Lambda=\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
.

4.1.
Refinement of the scales

We still let
\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}
denote the
2d_{k}
-neighborhood of
\Lambda_{k}
, so that
\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}\subset\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
and

{\rm diam}(\Lambda_{k}^{\prime})\approx 16d_{k},\ {\rm diam}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k})\sim d_{k+1}.

Set
L_{0}={\rm diam}(\Lambda_{k}^{\prime})
and introduce the following
a
-adic scales
for some integer
a\geq 2
:

(4.2)

L_{j+1}=a\cdot L_{j},\ j\geq 0.

Let
P
be the unique integer satisfying
L_{P}\leq d_{k+1}^{1/\sqrt{\alpha}}=d_{k}^{\sqrt{\alpha}}<L_{P+1}
. Consequently,

(4.3)

P\sim\log_{a}\left(\frac{d_{k}^{\sqrt{\alpha}}}{16d_{k}}\right)\sim\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}-1}{\log a}\cdot\log d_{k}\sim(\sqrt{\alpha}-1)\frac{\log d_{0}}{\log a}\cdot\alpha^{k}.

For
0\leq j\leq P
, we define
B_{j}\equiv\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}
and let
\overline{B}_{j}
be the
aL_{j}
-neighborhood of
B_{j}
. In this way, we obtain a nested block structure interpolating between
\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}
and
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
:

\Lambda_{k}^{\prime}\subset\overline{B}_{0}\subset\overline{B}_{1}\cdots\subset\overline{B}_{P}\subset\overline{\Lambda}_{k}

with corresponding scales

(4.4)

{\rm diam}(\Lambda_{k}^{\prime})=L_{0}<L_{1}<L_{2}<\cdots<L_{P}\sim d_{k}^{\sqrt{\alpha}}\ll d_{k+1}\sim{\rm diam}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}).

The value of
a
will be chosen at the end of the proof (it suffices to take
a\geq 5
) and will then remain fixed throughout.

4.2.
Schur complements and the smallness in different scales

In this part, we will study the movement of eigenvalues under the
a
-adic scale, using the iterative Schur complement method in
[
IM16
,
Imb21
]
. The following fundamental lemma is adapted from
[
IM16
, Lemma 1.4]
, and we omit its proof.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
K
be a
(p+q)\times(p+q)
symmetric matrix in block form

K=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}

with
A
a
p\times p
matrix,
D
a
q\times q
matrix, and
C=B^{\top}
(
B^{\top}
the denotes adjoint matrix of
B
).
Assume that

\|(D-E)^{-1}\|\leq\epsilon^{-1},\ \|B\|\leq\delta,\ \|C\|\leq\delta,\ E\in\mathbb{R}.

Define the Schur complement with respect to
\lambda
:

S_{\lambda}=A-B(D-\lambda)^{-1}C.

Let
\epsilon
and
\delta/\epsilon
be small enough and
|\lambda-E|\leq\epsilon/2
. Then

(i)

If
\varphi
is an eigenvector of
S_{\lambda}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
, then

\langle\varphi,-(D-\lambda)^{-1}C\varphi\rangle

is an eigenvector of
K
with eigenvalue
\lambda
, and all eigenvectors of
K
with eigenvalue
\lambda
are of this form.

(ii)

\|S_{\lambda}-S_{E}\|\leq 2\left(\frac{\delta}{\epsilon}\right)^{2}|\lambda-E|.

(iii)

The spectrum of
K
in
[E-\epsilon/2,E+\epsilon/2]
is in close agreement with that of
S_{E}
in the following sense.
If
\lambda
is an eigenvalues of
K
in
[E-\epsilon/2,E+\epsilon/2]
,
then there exists a corresponding eigenvalue
\tilde{\lambda}
of
S_{E}
, such that

|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}|\leq 2(\frac{\delta}{\epsilon})^{2}|\lambda-E|.

Remark 4.1
.

Let
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{m}
be the eigenvalues of
K
lying in
[E-\epsilon/2,E+\epsilon/2]
.
Then by the above lemma, for each
\lambda_{i}
one can find a corresponding eigenvalue
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}
of
S_{E}
to approximate it, with the ordered list

\tilde{\lambda}_{1}\leq\tilde{\lambda}_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\tilde{\lambda}_{m}.

However, such list does not necessarily coincide with the actual ordering (with multiplicity) of the eigenvalues of
S_{E}
. Hence, Lemma
4.2

does not
imply

(4.5)

\#\left({\rm Spec}(K)\cap[E-\frac{\epsilon}{2},E+\frac{\epsilon}{2}]\right)\leq\#\left({\rm Spec}(S_{E})\cap[E-\frac{\epsilon}{2}-\frac{\delta^{2}}{\epsilon},E+\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\frac{\delta^{2}}{\epsilon}]\right).

Therefore, to obtain (
4.5
), some extra efforts are required.

Now for each
0\leq j\leq P
, we write the matrix
H_{\overline{B}_{j}}
in the block form

H_{\overline{B}_{j}}=\begin{pmatrix}H_{B_{j}}&\Gamma_{j}^{\top}\\
\Gamma_{j}&H_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}\end{pmatrix},

where the connecting matrix
\Gamma_{j}:\ \ell^{2}(B_{j})\rightarrow\ell^{2}(\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j})
comes from
-\Delta
. The Schur complement in scale
L_{j}
with respect to
\mathcal{E}\in\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(4.6)

\widetilde{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}(B_{j}):=H_{B_{j}}-\Gamma_{j}^{\top}(H_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}-\mathcal{E})^{-1}\Gamma_{j}.

We will study the movement of eigenvalues of the Schur complements from scale
L_{j}
to scale
L_{j+1}
. Throughout this procedure, we define the following smallness: Recall
\gamma=\frac{1}{4d+h+\beta}
in Lemma
2.2
, and we set

•

(Spectral distance)

\varepsilon_{j}=\gamma^{(a+1+0.2)L_{j}}
,

•

(Transversality)

t_{j}=\gamma^{(a+1+0.1)L_{j}}
,

•

(The smallness generated by out-and-in random walks)

g_{j}=\gamma^{(2a-0.3)L_{j}}
.

(Indeed, the factor
0.1
can be replaced by any sufficiently small quantity.) The reason for this definition of smallness will be explained later.
Certainly,

(4.7)

\varepsilon_{j+1}\ll t_{j+1}\ll g_{j}\ll\varepsilon_{j}\ll t_{j}.

Keeping (
4.7
) in mind will help us better understand the eigenvalue behavior discussed in the following subsection.

4.3.
Movement of eigenvalues

In this part, we discuss the movement of eigenvalues. Let
E\in[-\frac{\iota}{2},4d+\beta+\frac{\iota}{2}]
. Denote

(4.8)

\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})=\#\left({\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(j)}_{E}(B_{j}))\cap I_{\varepsilon_{j}}(E)\right),

where
I_{\delta}(E):=[E-\delta,E+\delta]
. Obviously, by our definition,
\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})
only depends on the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j}
.

Inspired by
[
Imb21
, Proposition 3.2]
, we will show that, with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
, the quantity (
4.8
) has strict monotonicity property. More precisely, the following lemma holds for
j\geq 1
if we take
k
sufficiently large.

Lemma 4.3

(
Probabilistic strict monotonicity).

Assume
a\geq 5
, the height of the barrier
h\gg 1
and the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j-1}
(i.e.,
V_{{\rm r},\overline{B}_{j-1}}
