) has been fixed. Then

(1)

Deterministically, we have
\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})\leq\widehat{n}_{j-1}(B_{j-1})
.

(2)

There exists a site,
\bar{y}=\bar{y}(V_{{\rm r},\overline{B}_{j-1}})\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}
, depending only on the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j-1}
such that, after further fixing the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j}\setminus(\overline{B}_{j-1}\cup\{\bar{y}\})
, we have

(4.9)

\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})<\widehat{n}_{j-1}(B_{j-1})\ {\rm or}\ \widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})=\widehat{n}_{j-1}(B_{j-1})=0\ \bigg|\ V_{{\rm r},\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\{\bar{y}\}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}.

The above
\mathbb{P}
is only about the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j}
. In other words, the above event fails for at most one value of the random variable
V_{\text{r}}(\bar{y})=\omega(\bar{y})\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

We denote by
v\in\{0,1\}^{\overline{B}_{j-1}}
the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j-1}
, and by
\zeta\in\{0,1\}^{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}
the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}
. After fixing the random variables
V_{\text{r},\overline{B}_{j-1}}=v
, we may assume without loss of generality that there is at least one realization

V_{\text{r},\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}=\zeta_{0}

such that
\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})>0
. Otherwise

\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})=0\big|V_{\text{r},\overline{B}_{j}}=v\right)=1

and Lemma
4.3
holds trivially.

We first introduce the following difference of Schur complements at the same energy
\mathcal{E}
but on two successive scales:

(4.10)

\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}:=\widetilde{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}(B_{j})-\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\mathcal{E}}(B_{j-1}).

Claim 4.4
.

Assume
\mathcal{E}\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
. Then

\|\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}\|\leq g_{j-1}=\gamma^{(2a-0.3)L_{j-1}}.

Proof of Claim
4.4
.

Simple computation shows that

\displaystyle\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{j-1}^{\top}(H_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}-\mathcal{E})^{-1}\Gamma_{j-1}-\Gamma_{j}^{\top}(H_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}-\mathcal{E})^{-1}\Gamma_{j}.

Indeed, noticing that
B_{j}=B_{j-1}
and both
\Gamma_{j}
and
\Gamma_{j-1}
essentially connect site
w\in\partial^{-}B_{j}
with one of its neighbor
w^{\prime}\in\partial^{+}B_{j}
, we can cursorily view
\Gamma_{j}
and
\Gamma_{j-1}
the same. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{j}^{\top}[G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})]\Gamma_{j}-\Gamma_{j}^{\top}G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(\mathcal{E})\Gamma_{j}

(4.11)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{j}^{\top}\bigg([G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})]-G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(\mathcal{E})\bigg)\Gamma_{j}.

We denote
\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}
the connecting matrix between
\partial^{-}\overline{B}_{j-1}
and
\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}
via
-\Delta
.
By the resolvent identity, we get

\displaystyle G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})-G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(\mathcal{E})

\displaystyle=[G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})]\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(\mathcal{E})

(4.12)

\displaystyle=[G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})]\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}[G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})]

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-[G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})]\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(\mathcal{E})\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}[G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})].

Notice that if both
w,w^{\prime}\in\partial^{+}B_{j}
, then the element of the first term in (
4.3
)

[G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})]\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}[G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\overline{B}_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})](w,w^{\prime})

will vanish. Putting the remaining terms in (
4.3
) yields

(4.13)

\displaystyle\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\text{out,in}}(x,y)}G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j}}(w,x_{1};\mathcal{E})G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime};\mathcal{E})G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j}}(x_{2},w^{\prime};\mathcal{E}).

Here,
\mathcal{G}_{\text{out,in}}(x,y)
contains all the random walks (which lie in
\overline{B}_{j}
)

g:\ x\sim w\rightarrow x_{1}\sim x_{1}^{\prime}\rightarrow x_{2}^{\prime}\sim x_{2}\rightarrow w^{\prime}\sim y

starting at
x
and terminating at
y
with
x,y\in\partial^{-}B_{j};w,w^{\prime}\in\partial^{+}B_{j};x_{1},x_{2}\in\partial^{-}\overline{B}_{j-1}
and
x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}
. (Indeed the summation in (
4.13
) is only about the sites
w,w^{\prime},x_{1},x_{2},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}
in
g
, and the summation about the remaining sites in
g
is actually hidden in each Green’s function via the Neumann series expansion.) Such walks first start in
B_{j}
, then go out of
\overline{B}_{j-1}
, and finally turn back to
B_{j}
(see Figure
4
). Therefore, we must have

(4.14)

{\rm length}(g)\geq 2\ {\rm dist}(\partial^{+}B_{j},\partial^{-}\overline{B}_{j-1})+4\geq 2aL_{j-1}+2,

where
{\rm length}(\cdot)
denotes the length of a random walk.

B_{j}
\overline{B}_{j-1}
\overline{B}_{j}
g
x_{1}^{\prime}
x_{1}
w
x_{2}^{\prime}
x_{2}
w^{\prime}
transversality path

Figure 4.
Smallness generates from random walks.

Now, noticing that the annulus
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}
is non-resonant and
\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j},\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}
are both subset of
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}
(therefore are both non-resonant), we can apply Lemma
3.1
to the expansion (
4.13
). Recall that

\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}<1.

We have

\displaystyle|\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\text{out,in}(x,y)}}\left(\frac{1}{h-4d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{3}\cdot\exp\{-\gamma_{0}(|w-x_{1}|_{1}+|x_{1}^{\prime}-x_{2}^{\prime}|_{1}+|x_{2}-w^{\prime}|_{1})\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\text{out,in}(x,y)}}\left(\frac{1}{h-4d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{3}\cdot\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{2aL_{j-1}+2-4}

\displaystyle\leq(\#\partial^{+}B_{j})^{2}\cdot(\#\partial^{-}\overline{B}_{j-1})^{2}\cdot(\#\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1})^{2}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{h-4d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{3}\cdot\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{2aL_{j-1}-2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{(2a-0.1)L_{j-1}}

supposing that
k\gg 1
(to ensure
L_{j-1}\geq d_{k}\gg 1
). Therefore, by the Schur’s test,

\displaystyle\|\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in B_{j}}\sum_{y\in B_{j}}|\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}(x,y)|\leq\#B_{j}\cdot\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{(2a-0.1)L_{j-1}}

(4.15)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{(2a-0.2)L_{j-1}}\leq\gamma^{(2a-0.3)L_{j-1}}.

The final inequality in (
4.3
) requires

\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{2a-0.2}\leq\left(\frac{1}{4d+h+\beta}\right)^{2a-0.3}=\gamma^{2a-0.3},

which holds for sufficiently large
h
. Recall that
0<\beta\leq 1
and (
2.2
), the above inequality can be ensured by

(4.16)

\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-2}\right)^{2a-0.2}\leq\left(\frac{1}{4d+h+1}\right)^{2a-0.3}.

Since
a
will be selected later, the required largeness of
h
depends essentially only on the dimension
d
.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

The discussion above allows us to interpret the smallness parameter
g_{j-1}
in the following way. If the expansion (
4.13
) is carried out more fully by applying the Neumann series argument to the Green’s function, each site visited by the random walk contributes a factor of order
\gamma
. The length estimate (
4.14
) then yields a total smallness of about
\gamma^{(2a-)L_{j-1}}
, which we denote by
g_{j-1}
.
Moreover, the proof of Lemma
2.2
shows that the chain obtained via the cone property (recall (
2.8
)) originates in
B_{j}
and extends to
\partial^{+}B_{j-1}
(see the blue path in Figure
4
). Hence the transversality smallness
t_{j-1}
is significantly larger than
g_{j-1}
, because the latter corresponds to a walk that returns to
B_{j}
and then proceeds an additional distance of order
aL_{j-1}
-length. We therefore expect

(4.17)

t_{j-1}/g_{j-1}\sim\gamma^{-(a-)L_{j-1}}\gg 1.

The comparison in (
4.17
) can be made even stronger by increasing the barrier height
h
, which reduces the value of
\gamma
. This can be interpreted as that the larger
h
strengthens the transversality at a single site.

Recall that we fix
E\in[-\frac{\iota}{2},4d+\beta+\frac{\iota}{2}]
. Thus using Lemma
4.4
yields

(4.18)

\|\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{E}\|<g_{j-1}.

For any eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
of
\widetilde{S}^{(j)}_{E}(B_{j})
lying in
I_{\varepsilon_{j}}(E)
, we use (
4.18
) and the spectral stability lemma (Corollary
B.1
) to claim that, there exist a
\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}\in{\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{E}(B_{j-1}))
such that

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}-E|

\displaystyle\leq|\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}-\lambda_{0}|+|\lambda_{0}-E|\leq\|\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{E}\|+\varepsilon_{j}\leq\gamma^{(2a-0.3)L_{j-1}}+\gamma^{a(a+1+0.2)L_{j-1}}

(4.19)

\displaystyle\leq 2\gamma^{(2a-0.3)L_{j-1}}\ll\varepsilon_{j-1}.

Moreover, in Corollary
B.1
, this correspondence preserves the ordering of the eigenvalues (counting multiplicities). Hence,

\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})\leq\#\left({\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{E}(B_{j-1}))\cap I_{\varepsilon_{j-1}}(E)\right)=\widehat{n}_{j-1}(B_{j-1})

happens deterministically.

Under the configuration
(v,\zeta_{0})
,
\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})>0
implies that the
\lambda_{0}
exists, and thus if we take
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{1}(v)
as the eigenvalue of
\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{E}(B_{j-1})
closest to
E
, it will satisfy (
4.3
) and

(4.20)

|\lambda_{1}-E|\leq 2\gamma^{(2a-0.3)L_{j-1}}.

Obviously, by our choice of
\lambda_{1}
, it only depends on the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j-1}
.

Next, we introduce the following difference of Schur complements on the same scale but at two distinct energies:

(4.21)

\partial{S}^{(j-1)}_{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}=\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\mathcal{E}}(B_{j-1})-\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}(B_{j-1}).

Claim 4.5
.

Assume both
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
are in
[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
. Then

\|\partial{S}^{(j-1)}_{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}\|\leq\gamma^{1.9}|\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{E}^{\prime}|.

Proof of Claim
4.5
.

Simple computation shows that

\displaystyle\partial{S}^{(j-1)}_{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{j-1}^{\top}(H_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}-\mathcal{E})^{-1}\Gamma_{j-1}-\Gamma_{j-1}^{\top}(H_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}-\mathcal{E}^{\prime})^{-1}\Gamma_{j-1}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\Gamma_{j-1}^{\top}G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\Gamma_{j-1}.

Clearly,
\|\Gamma_{j-1}\|\leq\|-\Delta\|=2d
. Since
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
and
\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}
is non-resonant, we can apply Lemma
3.1
to obtain

\displaystyle\|\partial{S}^{(j-1)}_{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}\|

\displaystyle\leq(2d)^{2}|\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{E}^{\prime}|\cdot\|G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E})\|\cdot\|G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{2d}{h-4d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{2}|\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{E}^{\prime}|

(4.22)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{1.9}|\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{E}^{\prime}|.

Inequality (
4.3
) requires

\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{2}\leq\left(\frac{1}{4d+h+\beta}\right)^{1.9}=\gamma^{1.9},

which holds for a sufficiently large choice of
h
. Again, the above inequality can be ensured by

(4.23)

\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-2}\right)^{2}\leq\left(\frac{1}{4d+h+1}\right)^{1.9},

and the required largeness of
h
depends essentially only on the dimension
d
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

In the Wegner estimate, we fix
E\in[-\frac{\iota}{2},4d+\beta+\frac{\iota}{2}]
. Therefore Lemma
3.1
, Claim
4.4
and Claim
4.5
are applicable for all energy in
I_{\frac{\iota}{2}}(E)\subset[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
.

Now for our fixed
V_{\text{r},\overline{B}_{j-1}}=v\in\{0,1\}^{\overline{B}_{j-1}}
such that
\zeta_{0}
exists, we have proven that (in (
4.20
)) there must be a
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{1}(v)\in{\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{E}(B_{j-1}))
satisfying
|\lambda_{1}-E|\leq 2g_{j-1}
. Therefore, applying Claim
4.5
yields

\|\partial{S}^{(j-1)}_{E,\lambda_{1}}\|\leq\gamma^{1.9}\cdot 2g_{j-1}.

Then using Corollary
B.1
, we obtain that, there exists a
\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{2}(v)\in{\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda_{1}}(B_{j-1}))
such that

|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\leq\|\partial{S}^{(j-1)}_{E,\lambda_{1}}\|\leq\gamma^{1.9}\cdot 2g_{j-1}.

Thus, again applying Claim
4.5
yields

\|\partial{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\|\leq\gamma^{1.9\times 2}\cdot 2g_{j-1}.

Iterating such a fixed-point argument, we can obtain that for
s\geq 2
, there exists a
\lambda_{s+1}=\lambda_{s+1}(v)\in{\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda_{s}}(B_{j-1}))
such that

|\lambda_{s}-\lambda_{s+1}|\leq\|\partial{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda_{s-1},\lambda_{s}}\|\leq\gamma^{1.9s}\cdot 2g_{j-1}

and

\|\partial{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda_{s},\lambda_{s+1}}\|\leq\gamma^{1.9(s+1)}\cdot 2g_{j-1}.

Obviously, one can ensure all the
\lambda_{s}\in I_{\frac{\iota}{2}}(E)
by letting
d_{k}
sufficiently large, and by Remark
4.3
, Claim
4.5
is always applicable during each step of the iterations. The limit of
\lambda_{s},s\rightarrow\infty
is then denoted by
\mu
, which solves the fixed-point equation

(4.24)

\mu\in{\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\mu}(B_{j-1}))

in the region

|\mu-E|\leq|E-\lambda_{1}|+\sum_{s\geq 1}|\lambda_{s+1}-\lambda_{s}|\leq 2g_{j-1}\sum_{s\geq 0}\gamma^{1.9s}<3g_{j-1}.

Therefore, we can choose
\lambda
as the solution of (
4.24
), which is closest to
E
, and thus

(4.25)

\lambda\in{\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}(B_{j-1})),\ |\lambda-E|<3g_{j-1}.

Clearly, such
\lambda=\lambda(v)
only depends on the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j-1}
.

We will use the spectral information of
\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}(B_{j-1})
as an intermediate term. Denote

(4.26)

\widehat{n}=\#\left({\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}(B_{j-1}))\cap I_{\frac{\varepsilon_{j-1}}{2}}(E)\right).

Claim 4.6
.

\widehat{n}\leq\widehat{n}_{j-1}(B_{j-1})
.

Proof of Claim
4.6
.

By Claim
4.5
,

\|\partial{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda,E}\|\leq\gamma^{1.9}|E-\lambda|<3\gamma^{1.9}g_{j-1}.

Therefore, by the spectral stability lemma (Corollary
B.1
), each eigenvalue of
\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}
is approximated by an eigenvalue of
\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{E}
within an error of at most
3\gamma^{1.9}g_{j-1}
. This correspondence preserves the ordering of the eigenvalues (counting multiplicities). Hence,

\widehat{n}\leq\#\left({\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{E}(B_{j-1}))\cap I_{\frac{\varepsilon_{j-1}}{2}+3\gamma^{1.9}g_{j-1}}(E)\right).

Finally, notice that

3\gamma^{1.9}g_{j-1}=3\gamma^{1.9+(2a-0.3)L_{j-1}}\ll\frac{\varepsilon_{j-1}}{2}.

We have

\widehat{n}\leq\#\left({\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{E}(B_{j-1}))\cap I_{\varepsilon_{j-1}}(E)\right)=\widehat{n}_{j-1}(B_{j-1}).

∎

By our construction of
\lambda=\lambda(v)
, (
4.25
) holds. Hence, Lemma
4.2
implies that

(4.27)

\lambda\in{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{B}_{j-1}}).

Moreover, if
\psi
is a corresponding eigenvector of
H_{\overline{B}_{j-1}}
satisfying

H_{\overline{B}_{j-1}}\psi=\lambda\psi,

then
\varphi=R_{B_{j-1}}\psi
is an eigenvector of
\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}(B_{j-1})
for the eigenvalue
\lambda
, and

(4.28)

\psi=\langle\varphi,-G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\lambda)\Gamma_{j-1}\varphi\rangle.

This correspondence is one to one. For simplicity, we denote

\widetilde{G}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}=-G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\lambda)\Gamma_{j-1}.

Recall that we can compute
\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda}
as in Claim
4.4
by (
4.3
) and (
4.3
). That is to say

\displaystyle\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda}(B_{j})-\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}(B_{j-1})

\displaystyle=-\Gamma_{j-1}^{\top}G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\lambda)\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(\lambda)\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\lambda)\Gamma_{j-1}

\displaystyle=-(\widetilde{G}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda})^{\top}\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(\lambda)\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}\widetilde{G}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}

We choose the orthonormal eigenbasis of
\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}(B_{j-1})
in
\ell^{2}(B_{j-1})=\ell^{2}(B_{j})
as

\{\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\cdots,\varphi_{\widehat{n}},\varphi_{\widehat{n}+1},\cdots,\varphi_{\#B_{j}}\},

which have their corresponding eigenvalues
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}\cdots,\mathcal{E}_{\#B_{j}}
with

\lambda=\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\cdots,\mathcal{E}_{\widehat{n}}\in I_{\frac{\varepsilon_{j-1}}{2}}(E)

and other eigenvalues are not in
I_{\frac{\varepsilon_{j-1}}{2}}(E)
. Under such basis, the elements of the matrix
\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda}
can be written as

(4.29)

\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda,m,m^{\prime}}=-\left\langle\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}\widetilde{G}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}\varphi_{m},G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(\lambda)\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}\widetilde{G}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}\varphi_{m^{\prime}}\right\rangle.

Since
\varphi_{1}
is an eigenvector of
\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}(B_{j-1})
for the eigenvalue
\lambda
, (
4.28
) tells us that
\psi_{1}=\langle\varphi_{1},\widetilde{G}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}\varphi_{1}\rangle
is a solution of the Dirichlet boundary problem

(4.30)

H_{\overline{B}_{j-1}}\psi_{1}=\lambda\psi_{1}.

This allows us to apply Lemma
2.2
to obtain

\max_{y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\mathcal{I}_{\psi_{1}}(y)\geq\gamma^{aL_{j-1}+L_{0}}\cdot\max_{n\in B_{j-1}}|\psi_{1}(n)|.

Since
\varphi_{1}=R_{B_{j-1}}\psi_{1}
, we have

\max_{y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\mathcal{I}_{\psi_{1}}(y)\geq\gamma^{(a+1)L_{j-1}}\|\varphi_{1}\|_{\ell^{\infty}(B_{j-1})}.

Trivially, one has

\|\varphi_{1}\|_{\ell^{\infty}(B_{j-1})}\geq\sqrt{\frac{1}{\#B_{j-1}}}\|\varphi_{1}\|=L_{0}^{-\frac{d}{2}}.

We have

(4.31)

\max_{y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\mathcal{I}_{\psi_{1}}(y)\geq\gamma^{(a+1)L_{j-1}}L_{0}^{-\frac{d}{2}}\geq\gamma^{(a+1+0.1)L_{j-1}}=t_{j-1}.

Building on (
4.29
), we define for each
y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}
,

(4.32)

a_{m}(y):=\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}\widetilde{G}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}\varphi_{m}(y)

and the vector in
\mathbb{R}^{\widehat{n}}

(4.33)

\mathbf{a}(y)=(a_{1}(y),a_{2}(y),\cdots,a_{\widehat{n}}(y)).

Clearly, (
4.32
) and (
4.33
) also depend only on the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j-1}
. In particular, by our definition,

(4.34)

|a_{1}(y)|=\mathcal{I}_{\psi_{1}}(y).

We choose
\bar{y}=\bar{y}(v)\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}
to be a point so that

|\mathbf{a}(y)|=\left(\sum_{1\leq m\leq\widehat{n}}|a_{m}(y)|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

attains its maximum. This maximum depends only on
\lambda
and the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j-1}
. Since
\lambda
itself is taken as the eigenvalue closest to
E
, it is determined solely by the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j-1}
. Hence
\bar{y}
also depends essentially only on the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j-1}
. If the maximum is attained at more than one point, we select any one
\bar{y}
among them.
Hence, by (
4.31
) and (
4.34
), we have

(4.35)

|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|\geq\max_{y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\mathcal{I}_{\psi_{1}}(y)\geq\gamma^{(a+1+0.1)L_{j-1}}=t_{j-1}.

Having chosen
\bar{y}
, we further take condition probability on the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j}\setminus(\overline{B}_{j-1}\cup\{\bar{y}\})
, that is, we fix the configuration

V_{\text{r},\overline{B}_{j}\setminus(\overline{B}_{j-1}\cup\{\bar{y}\})}=\zeta^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus(\overline{B}_{j-1}\cup\{\bar{y}\})}.

Now we use the eigenbasis
\{\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{\#B_{j}}\}
to do further Schur complement argument. At the energy
\mathcal{E}
, we write
\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\mathcal{E}}(B_{j-1})
and
\widetilde{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}(B_{j})
in the following block forms (notice that the eigenbasis diagonalizes
\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}(B_{j-1})
):

(4.36)

\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\mathcal{E}}(B_{j-1})=\begin{pmatrix}q(\mathcal{E})&r(\mathcal{E})\\
s(\mathcal{E})&t(\mathcal{E})\end{pmatrix},\ \widetilde{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}(B_{j})=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{q}(\mathcal{E})&\tilde{r}(\mathcal{E})\\
\tilde{s}(\mathcal{E})&\tilde{t}(\mathcal{E})\end{pmatrix}.

Here
q,\tilde{q}
are restricted to the subspace spanned by
\{\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{\widehat{n}}\}
, and
t,\tilde{t}
to the subspace spanned by
\{\varphi_{\widehat{n}+1,}\cdots,\varphi_{\#B_{j}}\}
. We define the following Schur complements:

(4.37)

s^{(j-1)}_{\mathcal{E}}=q(\mathcal{E})-r(\mathcal{E})(t(\mathcal{E})-\mathcal{E})^{-1}s(\mathcal{E}),\ s^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}=\tilde{q}(\mathcal{E})-\tilde{r}(\mathcal{E})(\tilde{t}(\mathcal{E})-\lambda)^{-1}\tilde{s}(\mathcal{E}).

In particular, since
\{\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{\#B_{j}}\}
diagonalizes
\widetilde{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}(B_{j-1})
, we have

r(\lambda)=0,\ s(\lambda)=0.

Since we have already conditioned on the randomness in region
\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\{\bar{y}\}
(i.e.,
(v,\zeta^{\prime})
), we next wish to decompose
s^{(j)}_{E}
in order to extract the principal part depending only on the random variable
\omega(\bar{y})
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Assume that we choose the
a
-adic scale with
a\geq 5
. After conditioning on the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\{\bar{y}\}
, we can decompose

\displaystyle s^{(j)}_{E}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}\left(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})+\sum_{z\sim\bar{y}\atop z\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(z)-\lambda}\cdot(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(z)+\mathbf{a}(z)\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y}))\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\mathcal{D}+\mathcal{R}(\omega(\bar{y})).

Here
\mathcal{D}
is a deterministic term, and the random term
\mathcal{R}(\omega(\bar{y}))
satisfies

(4.38)

\|\mathcal{R}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|\leq\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2.5}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{0.5L_{j-1}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

Proof of Lemma
4.7
.

Consider

(4.39)

s^{(j)}_{E}=s^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}+(s^{(j)}_{\lambda}-s^{(j-1)}_{\lambda})+(s^{(j)}_{E}-s^{(j)}_{\lambda}).

We analyze the above terms separately.

(Term
s^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}
)

We set

(4.40)

\mathcal{D}_{1}=s^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}.

Obviously,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is deterministic, as it only depends on the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j-1}
.

(Term
(s^{(j)}_{\lambda}-s^{(j-1)}_{\lambda})
)

By (
4.36
) and (
4.37
), direct computation leads to

(4.41)

s^{(j)}_{\lambda}-s^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}=(\tilde{q}(\lambda)-q(\lambda))-\tilde{r}(\lambda)(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}\tilde{s}(\lambda).

Since
r(\lambda)=0
and
s(\lambda)=0
, it is easy to see that
\tilde{q}(\lambda)-q(\lambda),\tilde{r}(\lambda),\tilde{s}(\lambda)
and
\tilde{t}(\lambda)-t(\lambda)
contain elements of
\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda}
under the eigenbasis, i.e., (
4.29
).
Now under the notation (
4.32
) and (
4.33
), (
4.29
) becomes

(4.42)

\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda,m,m^{\prime}}=-\sum_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}a_{m}(x)G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(x,y;\lambda)a_{m^{\prime}}(y).

We expand
G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(\lambda)
via the Neumann series similar to (
3.1
), leading to the following random walk expansion:

(4.43)

\displaystyle G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(x,y;\lambda)=\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y)}\frac{1}{V_{x}+2d-\lambda}\cdot\frac{1}{V_{x_{1}}+2d-\lambda}\cdots\frac{1}{V_{x_{s-1}}+2d-\lambda}\cdot\frac{1}{V_{y}+2d-\lambda}.

Here for
x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}
, the set
\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y)
contains all random walks
g
contained in
\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}
with

g:\ x\sim x_{1}\sim x_{2}\cdots\sim x_{s-1}\sim y,\ s={\rm length}(g).

We decompose
\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y)
into disjoint unions

\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y)=\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(0)}(x,y)\cup\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}(x,y)\cup\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(2)}(x,y),

with

\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(0)}(x,y)=\left\{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y):\ \bar{y}\notin g\right\},

\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}(x,y)=\left\{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y):\ \bar{y}\in g,\ {\rm length}(g)=0\ {\rm or}\ 1\right\},

\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(2)}(x,y)=\left\{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y):\ \bar{y}\in g,\ {\rm length}(g)\geq 2\right\}.

Clearly,
\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}
is not empty if and only if
x=\bar{y}
or
y=\bar{y}
. Therefore, (
4.43
) can be decomposed into

(4.44)

K(x,y):=G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(x,y;\lambda)=K_{0}(x,y)+K_{1}(x,y)+K_{2}(x,y),

where

(4.45)

K_{i}(x,y)=\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y)}\frac{1}{V_{x}+2d-\lambda}\cdot\frac{1}{V_{x_{1}}+2d-\lambda}\cdots\frac{1}{V_{y}+2d-\lambda},\ i=1,2,3.

Clearly, our decomposition ensures that
K_{1}(x,y),K_{2}(x,y)
depend only on
V_{\text{r}}(\bar{y})=\omega(\bar{y})
, whereas
K_{0}(x,y)
does not. Since
\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}
is non-resonant, we have

(4.46)

K_{1}(\bar{y},\bar{y})=\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}

and

(4.47)

K_{1}(\bar{y},z)=K_{1}(z,\bar{y})=\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}\cdot\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(z)-\lambda}\ {\rm for}\ \forall z\sim\bar{y},z\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}.

Moreover, the random walks in
\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(2)}
have length larger than
2
.
We use
\lambda\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
to estimate

\displaystyle|K_{2}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t\geq(|x-\bar{y}|_{1}+|\bar{y}-y|_{1})\vee 2\atop r+s=t,r>|x-\bar{y}|_{1},s>|\bar{y}-y|_{1}}\sum_{g_{1}=(x_{0}=x,x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}=\bar{y})\atop g_{1}\in\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}\sum_{g_{2}=(x_{r}=\bar{y},x_{r+1},\cdots,x_{r}=\bar{y})\atop g_{2}\in\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}\frac{1}{V_{x}+2d-\lambda}\cdots\frac{1}{V_{y}+2d-\lambda}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t\geq(|x-\bar{y}|_{1}+|\bar{y}-y|_{1})\vee 2}(t+1)(2d)^{t}\left(\frac{1}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{t+1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d}\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{(|x-\bar{y}|_{1}+|\bar{y}-y|_{1})\vee 2+1}

(4.48)

\displaystyle<\gamma^{0.9[(|x-\bar{y}|_{1}+|\bar{y}-y|_{1})\vee 3]}.

Here the validity of (
4.3
) also requires

(4.49)

\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\leq\frac{2d}{h-2d-2}\lesssim_{d}\left(\frac{1}{4d+h+1}\right)^{1.9}\leq\gamma^{0.9},

which will hold when
h
is sufficiently large (depending only on
d
).
We first discuss the term
\tilde{q}(\lambda)-q(\lambda)
in (
4.41
). For
m,m^{\prime}\in\{1,\cdots,\widehat{n}\}
, using (
4.42
) and the decomposition (
4.44
) yields

\displaystyle(\tilde{q}(\lambda)-q(\lambda))_{m,m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda,m,m^{\prime}}=-\sum_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}a_{m}(x)K(x,y)a_{m^{\prime}}(y)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}a_{m}(x)\left(K_{0}(x,y)+K_{1}(x,y)+K_{2}(x,y)\right)a_{m^{\prime}}(y).

We set

(4.50)

\mathcal{D}_{2,m,m^{\prime}}=-\sum_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}a_{m}(x)K_{0}(x,y)a_{m^{\prime}}(y),

which is deterministic since
\mathbf{a}(x),\mathbf{a}(y)
and
K_{0}(x,y)
are independent of
\omega(\bar{y})
.
Moreover, recalling t (
4.46
) and (
4.47
), a direct computation yields

\displaystyle\sum_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}a_{m}(x)K_{1}(x,y)a_{m^{\prime}}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}a_{m}(\bar{y})a_{m^{\prime}}(\bar{y})+\sum_{z\sim\bar{y}\atop z\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\frac{a_{m}(\bar{y})a_{m^{\prime}}(z)+a_{m}(z)a_{m^{\prime}}(\bar{y})}{(h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda)\cdot(h+2d+\beta\omega(z)-\lambda)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}\left(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})+\sum_{z\sim\bar{y}\atop z\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(z)-\lambda}\cdot(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(z)+\mathbf{a}(z)\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y}))\right)_{m,m^{\prime}}.

Next, we set

(4.51)

\mathcal{R}_{1,m,m^{\prime}}(\omega(\bar{y}))=-\sum_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}a_{m}(x)K_{2}(x,y)a_{m^{\prime}}(y).

Claim 4.8
.

We have

\|\mathcal{R}_{1}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|\leq\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2.5}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

Proof of Claim
4.8
.

By our definition,

\mathcal{R}_{1}(\omega(\bar{y}))=-\sum_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}K_{2}(x,y)\cdot\mathbf{a}(x)\otimes\mathbf{a}(y).

Hence, using the Minkowski inequality and (
4.3
) yields

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{1}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}|K_{2}(x,y)|\cdot\|\mathbf{a}(x)\otimes\mathbf{a}(y)\|=\sum_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}|K_{2}(x,y)|\cdot|\mathbf{a}(x)|\cdot|\mathbf{a}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}\sum_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\gamma^{0.9[(|x-\bar{y}|_{1}+|\bar{y}-y|_{1})\vee 3]}\leq|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}\sum_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\gamma^{0.9[(|x-\bar{y}|_{1}+|\bar{y}-y|_{1})\vee 3]}

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}\sum_{r,s\geq 0}(2d)^{r+s}\gamma^{0.9[(r+s)\vee 3]}\lesssim_{d}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}\gamma^{0.9\times 3}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2.5}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}

supposing
h
is large enough (depending only on
d
) to make sure
0<\gamma\ll 1
. In the above argument, we also use the fact that
\bar{y}
is chosen to maximize
|\mathbf{a}(y)|
.

∎

Summarize the above discussions, we have decomposed

(4.52)

\displaystyle\tilde{q}(\lambda)-q(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{2}+\mathcal{R}_{1}(\omega(\bar{y}))

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}\left(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})+\sum_{z\sim\bar{y}\atop z\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(z)-\lambda}\cdot(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(z)+\mathbf{a}(z)\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y}))\right).

For the remaining term
\tilde{r}(\lambda)(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}\tilde{s}(\lambda)
in (
4.41
), we denote it by
\mathcal{R}_{2}(\omega(\bar{y}))
. It can be estimated as follows:

Claim 4.9
.

For

(4.53)

\mathcal{R}_{2}(\omega(\bar{y}))=-\tilde{r}(\lambda)(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}\tilde{s}(\lambda),

we have

\|\mathcal{R}_{2}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|\leq\frac{1}{6}\gamma^{(a-2)L_{j-1}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

Proof of Claim
4.9
.

For
m,m^{\prime}\in\{1,\cdots,\widehat{n}\}
, we expand elements in
\mathcal{R}_{2}(\omega(\bar{y}))
into

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2,m,m^{\prime}}(\omega(\bar{y}))=-\sum_{\widehat{n}+1\leq\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}\leq\#B_{j}}\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda,m,\widetilde{m}}\cdot[(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}]_{\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}}\cdot\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda,\widetilde{m}^{\prime},m^{\prime}}.

By (
4.42
), this can be further expanded into

(4.54)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2,m,m^{\prime}}(\omega(\bar{y}))

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\sum_{\widehat{n}+1\leq\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}\leq\#B_{j}}a_{m}(x)K(x,x^{\prime})a_{\widetilde{m}}(x^{\prime})\cdot[(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}]_{\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}}\cdot a_{\widetilde{m}^{\prime}}(y^{\prime})K(y^{\prime},y)a_{m^{\prime}}(y).

Now, recall that we choose the eigenbasis such that
\mathcal{E}_{m}\notin I_{\frac{\varepsilon_{j-1}}{2}}(E)
for
\widehat{n}\leq m\leq\#B_{j}
, and we have (
4.25
). Thus

{\rm dist}({\rm Spec}(t(\lambda)),\lambda)>\frac{\varepsilon_{j-1}}{2}-3g_{j-1}.

Moreover, since
\tilde{t}(\lambda)-t(\lambda)
is the restriction of
\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda}
on the subspace spanned by
{\varphi_{\widehat{n}+1},\cdots,\varphi_{\#B_{j}}}
, applying Claim
4.4
yields

\|\tilde{t}(\lambda)-t(\lambda)\|\leq\|\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda}\|\leq g_{j-1}.

Therefore, using Corollary
B.1
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm dist}({\rm Spec}(\tilde{t}(\lambda)),\lambda)

\displaystyle>{\rm dist}({\rm Spec}(t(\lambda)),\lambda)-g_{j-1}>\frac{\varepsilon_{j-1}}{2}-4g_{j-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{(a+1+0.2)L_{j-1}}-4\gamma^{(2a-0.3)L_{j-1}}>\frac{\varepsilon_{j-1}}{3},

which implies

(4.55)

\|(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}\|<\frac{3}{\varepsilon_{j-1}}.

On the other hand, the definition (
4.32
) ensures that

\displaystyle|a_{m}(y)|

\displaystyle=|\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}\widetilde{G}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}\varphi_{m}(y)|=|\Gamma_{\partial\overline{B}_{j-1}}G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\lambda)\Gamma_{j-1}\varphi_{m}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{w\in\partial^{-}\overline{B}_{j-1}\atop w\sim y}\sum_{w^{\prime}\in\partial^{+}B_{j-1}\atop y^{\prime}\in\partial^{-}B_{j-1},w^{\prime}\sim y^{\prime}}|G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(w,w^{\prime};\lambda)|\cdot|\varphi_{m}(y^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\#\partial^{+}B_{j-1}\cdot\frac{(2d)^{2}}{h-4d-\beta-\iota}\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{{\rm dist}(\partial^{+}B_{j-1},\partial^{-}\overline{B}_{j-1})}.

Here we used Lemma
3.1
to estimate
G_{\overline{B}_{j-1}\setminus B_{j-1}}(\lambda)
. Now by the fact that

{\rm dist}(\partial^{+}B_{j-1},\partial^{-}\overline{B}_{j-1})=aL_{j-1}-1

and taking
h
large enough (depending on
d,\beta
), we obtain

(4.56)

|a_{m}(y)|<\gamma^{(a-0.1)L_{j-1}}\ {\rm for}\ \forall 1\leq m\leq\#B_{j},\ \forall y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}.

We can use (
4.55
) and (
4.56
) to bound

\displaystyle\sum_{\widehat{n}+1\leq\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}\leq\#B_{j}}\bigg|a_{\widetilde{m}}(x^{\prime})\cdot[(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}]_{\widetilde{m},\widetilde{m}^{\prime}}\cdot a_{\widetilde{m}^{\prime}}(y^{\prime})\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq(\#B_{j})^{2}\gamma^{2(a-0.1)L_{j-1}}\cdot\frac{3}{\gamma^{(a+1+0.2)L_{j-1}}}

\displaystyle<\gamma^{(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}.

Plugging this estimate into (
4.54
) gives

\displaystyle|\mathcal{R}_{2,m,m^{\prime}}(\omega(\bar{y}))|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}\bigg|\sum_{x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}a_{m}(x)K(x,x^{\prime})K(y^{\prime},y)a_{m^{\prime}}(y)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}\cdot(\#\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1})^{4}\|K\|^{2}.

In the above argument, we again used the fact that
\bar{y}
maximizes
|\mathbf{a}(y)|
. Finally, recall that
K
is the Green’s function
G_{\overline{B}_{j}\setminus B_{j}}(x,y;\lambda)
. Applying Claim
3.1
and the Schur’s test yields

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{2}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma^{(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}}{(h-4d-\beta-\iota)^{2}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}\cdot(\#\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1})^{4}\cdot(\#B_{j})^{2}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{1}{6}\gamma^{(a-2)L_{j-1}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

The absorption of terms such as
\#B_{j}
is possible because we can take
k
sufficiently large, thereby making
L_{j-1}
large enough.
∎

It follows from (
4.41
), (
4.52
) and (
4.53
) that

(4.57)

\displaystyle s^{(j)}_{\lambda}-s^{(j-1)}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{2}+\mathcal{R}_{1}(\omega(\bar{y}))+\mathcal{R}_{2}(\omega(\bar{y}))

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}\left(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})+\sum_{z\sim\bar{y}\atop z\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(z)-\lambda}\cdot(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(z)+\mathbf{a}(z)\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y}))\right).

(Term
(s^{(j)}_{E}-s^{(j)}_{\lambda})
)

For this term, we compute it as

\displaystyle s^{(j)}_{E}-s^{(j)}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{q}(E)-\tilde{q}(\lambda))+\tilde{r}(\lambda)(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}\tilde{s}(\lambda)-\tilde{r}(E)(\tilde{t}(E)-E)^{-1}\tilde{s}(E)

(4.58)

\displaystyle=(q(E)-q(\lambda))

\displaystyle\ \ \ +[(\tilde{q}(E)-q(E))-(\tilde{q}(\lambda)-q(\lambda))]

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\tilde{r}(\lambda)(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}\tilde{s}(\lambda)-\tilde{r}(E)(\tilde{t}(E)-E)^{-1}\tilde{s}(E).

We set

(4.59)

\mathcal{D}_{3}=(q(E)-q(\lambda)),

which is deterministic, since by our construction,
q(\mathcal{E})
and
\lambda
are independent of
\omega(\bar{y})
.

Next, for the second line in (
4.3
), we set

(4.60)

\mathcal{R}_{3}(\omega(\bar{y}))=(\tilde{q}(E)-q(E))-(\tilde{q}(\lambda)-q(\lambda)).

Claim 4.10
.

We have

\|\mathcal{R}_{3}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|\leq\frac{1}{6}\gamma^{(a-2)L_{j-1}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

Proof of Claim
4.10
.

Indeed,
\mathcal{R}_{3}(\omega(\bar{y}))
can be viewed as the matrix

\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{E}-\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda}

restricted to the subspace spanned by
\{\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{\widehat{n}}\}
. Therefore,

\|\mathcal{R}_{3}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|\leq\|\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{E}-\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda}\|.

Expanding the Green’s function in (
4.13
) further by a Neumann series yields

(4.61)

\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}(x,y)=-\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\text{out,in}}(x,y)}\frac{1}{V_{x_{1}}+2d-\mathcal{E}}\cdot\frac{1}{V_{x_{2}}+2d-\mathcal{E}}\cdots\frac{1}{V_{x_{s-1}}+2d-\mathcal{E}}

with the random walk

g=(x_{0}=x,x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{s-1},x_{s}=y),x_{i-1}\sim x_{i}

in
\mathcal{G}_{\text{out,in}}(x,y)
. (For the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{\text{out,in}}(x,y)
, see the proof of Claim
4.10
.)

Taking the derivative about
\mathcal{E}
on (
4.61
) and applying (
4.14
) yield that

\displaystyle|\frac{d}{d\mathcal{E}}(\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\mathcal{E}}(x,y))|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\text{out,in}}(x,y)}\left(\frac{1}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{{\rm length}(g)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s\geq 2aL_{j-1}+2}\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{s}<\gamma^{(2a-0.1)L_{j-1}}

holds for all
\mathcal{E}\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
and
x,y\in\partial^{-}B_{j}
, provided

\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{2a}<\gamma^{(2a-0.1)}.

This can be ensured by

(4.62)

\left(\frac{2d}{h-2d-2}\right)^{2a}<\left(\frac{1}{4d+h+1}\right)^{(2a-0.1)},

which requires
h
to be sufficiently large (depending only on
d
). Thus by the mean value theorem and (
4.25
),

|\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{E}(x,y)-\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda}(x,y)|<\gamma^{(2a-0.1)L_{j-1}}|\lambda-E|<3g_{j-1}\gamma^{(2a-0.1)L_{j-1}}.

Apply the Schur’s test, we get

\displaystyle\|\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{E}-\Delta{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda}\|

\displaystyle\leq(\#\partial^{-}B_{j})^{2}\cdot 3g_{j-1}\gamma^{(2a-0.1)L_{j-1}}\leq\gamma^{(4a-0.5)L_{j-1}}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{(2a-2-0.7)L_{j-1}}t^{2}_{j-1}\leq\frac{1}{6}\gamma^{(a-2)L_{j-1}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

In the above estimate, we used (
4.35
). Therefore, we have proven

(4.63)

\|\mathcal{R}_{3}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|\leq\frac{1}{6}\gamma^{(a-2)L_{j-1}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

∎

Finally, for the third line in (
4.3
), we set

(4.64)

\mathcal{R}_{4}(\omega(\bar{y}))=\tilde{r}(\lambda)(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}\tilde{s}(\lambda)-\tilde{r}(E)(\tilde{t}(E)-E)^{-1}\tilde{s}(E).

Claim 4.11
.

Assume that we choose the
a
-adic scale with
a\geq 5
. Then we have

\|\mathcal{R}_{4}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|\leq\frac{1}{6}\gamma^{0.5L_{j-1}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

Proof of Claim
4.11
.

Compute
\mathcal{R}_{4}(\omega(\bar{y}))
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{4}(\omega(\bar{y}))

\displaystyle=(\tilde{r}(\lambda)-\tilde{r}(E))(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}\tilde{s}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\qquad+\tilde{r}(E)(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}(\tilde{s}(\lambda)-\tilde{s}(E))

\displaystyle\qquad+\tilde{r}(E)(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}\cdot[(\tilde{t}(E)-\tilde{t}(\lambda))-(E-\lambda)]\cdot(\tilde{t}(E)-E)^{-1}\tilde{s}(E).

We have the following available estimates:

•

By
r(\lambda)=0,s(\lambda)=0
,
|\lambda-E|<3g_{j-1}
and Claim
4.5
, we have

\|s(E)\|,\ \|r(E)\|\leq\|\partial{S}^{(j-1)}_{\lambda,E}\|\leq\gamma^{1.9}\cdot 3g_{j-1}

and

\|\tilde{s}(\lambda)-\tilde{s}(E)\|,\ \|\tilde{r}(\lambda)-\tilde{r}(E)\|\leq\|\partial{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda,E}\|\leq\gamma^{1.9}\cdot 3g_{j-1}.

•

By
r(\lambda)=0,s(\lambda)=0
and Claim
4.10
, we have

\|\tilde{r}(\lambda)\|,\|\tilde{s}(\lambda)\|,\ \|\tilde{r}(E)-r(E)\|,\ \|\tilde{s}(E)-s(E)\|,\ \|\tilde{t}(E)-t(E)\|\leq g_{j-1}.

Therefore, we also have

\|\tilde{s}(E)\|,\ \|\tilde{r}(E)\|\leq g_{j-1}+\gamma^{1.9}\cdot 3g_{j-1}\leq 2g_{j-1}.

•

By similar argument leading to (
4.55
), and the fact that

\|\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\tilde{t}(E)\|\leq\|\partial{S}^{(j)}_{\lambda,E}\|\leq\gamma^{1.9}g_{j-1},

we have

\|(\tilde{t}(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}\|,\ \|(\tilde{t}(E)-E)^{-1}\|<\frac{4}{\varepsilon_{j-1}}.

Use the estimates listed above, we can control the norm of
\mathcal{R}_{4}(\omega(\bar{y}))
as

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}_{4}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{g_{j-1}^{2}}{\varepsilon_{j-1}}+\frac{g_{j-1}^{3}}{\varepsilon_{j-1}^{2}}=\gamma^{(3a-1-0.8)L_{j-1}}+\gamma^{(4a-2-1.4)L_{j-1}}

(4.65)

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma^{[3a-1-0.8-2(a+1+0.1)]L_{j-1}}t^{2}_{j-1}\leq\frac{1}{6}\gamma^{0.5L_{j-1}}t^{2}_{j-1}.

The validity of (
4.3
) requires

(4.66)

3a-1-0.8-2(a+1+0.1)>0.5,

which means we need to take
a\geq 5
. Thus, using (
4.35
) and (
4.3
) yields

\|\mathcal{R}_{4}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|\leq\frac{1}{6}\gamma^{0.5L_{j-1}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

∎

Combining (
4.3
), (
4.59
), (
4.60
) and (
4.64
) together yields the decomposition

(4.67)

s^{(j)}_{E}-s^{(j)}_{\lambda}=\mathcal{D}_{3}+\mathcal{R}_{3}(\omega(\bar{y}))+\mathcal{R}_{4}(\omega(\bar{y})).

(Summarize all terms)

Finally, by (
4.39
), (
4.40
), (
4.57
) and (
4.67
), we take

\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{1}+\mathcal{D}_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{3}

and

\mathcal{R}(\omega(\bar{y}))=\mathcal{R}_{1}(\omega(\bar{y}))+\mathcal{R}_{2}(\omega(\bar{y}))+\mathcal{R}_{3}(\omega(\bar{y}))+\mathcal{R}_{4}(\omega(\bar{y})).

Then the decomposition in Lemma
4.7
is obtained. Using Claim
4.8
, Claim
4.9
, Claim
4.10
and Claim
4.11
on the random parts yield (
4.38
). We have finished the proof of Lemma
4.7
.
∎

At this stage, we are approaching the final step of proving Lemma
4.3
. Define

\widehat{n}_{s}=\#\left({\rm Spec}(s^{(j)}_{E})\cap I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)\right).

Now we choose
a\geq 5
. We will show that, after conditioning on the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\{\bar{y}\}
, the event

(4.68)

\widehat{n}_{s}<\widehat{n}

fails for at most one value of
\omega(\bar{y})
. By construction, the matrix
s^{(j)}_{E}
has order exactly equaling to
\widehat{n}
(which is also deterministic now). Thus deterministically we have

\widehat{n}_{s}\leq\widehat{n}.

For simplicity, we denote the principal part in Lemma
4.7
by

\mathcal{M}=\left(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})+\sum_{z\sim\bar{y}\atop z\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(z)-\lambda}\cdot(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(z)+\mathbf{a}(z)\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y}))\right),

which is deterministic and independent of
\omega(\bar{y})
.

(Case 1:
\widehat{n}=1
.)

When
\widehat{n}=1
, then
s^{(j)}_{E}
is a scalar. Since we have the influence estimate

(4.69)

\left|\ \frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}\bigg|_{\omega(\bar{y})=0}^{\omega(\bar{y})=1}\ \right|\geq\frac{\beta}{(h-2d-\beta-\iota)^{2}}\geq\gamma^{2}\beta,

using the decomposition in Lemma
4.7
yields

\displaystyle\left|\ s^{(j)}_{E}\big|_{\omega(\bar{y})=0}^{\omega(\bar{y})=1}\ \right|

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{2}\beta|\mathcal{M}|-|\mathcal{R}(1)-\mathcal{R}(0)|

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{2}\beta|\mathcal{M}|-2\left(\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2.5}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{0.5L_{j-1}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{2}\beta|\mathcal{M}|-2\gamma^{2.5}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

Now, the second part in
\mathcal{M}
can be bounded by

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{z\sim\bar{y}\atop z\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(z)-\lambda}\cdot(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(z)+\mathbf{a}(z)\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y}))\right|\leq\frac{4d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2},

where we used the fact that
\bar{y}
is chosen to maximize
|\mathbf{a}(y)|
. Thus if we choose
h
large (depending only on
d
) such that

(4.70)

\frac{4d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\leq\frac{4d}{h-2d-2}<\frac{1}{2},

then we have

|\mathcal{M}|\geq\left(1-\frac{4d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

Therefore, combining
a\geq 5
and (
4.35
) yields

\displaystyle\left|\ s^{(j)}_{E}\big|_{\omega(\bar{y})=0}^{\omega(\bar{y})=1}\ \right|

\displaystyle\geq(\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\beta-2\gamma^{2.5})\cdot|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}

(4.71)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\beta}{4}\gamma^{2}t^{2}_{j-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\beta}{4}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{2(a+1+0.1)L_{j-1}}\gg 4\gamma^{\frac{a}{2}(a+1+0.2)L_{j-1}}=4\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}.

Here, the validity of (
4.3
) requires

4\gamma^{0.5}\leq\beta,

which requires
h

to be sufficiently large (depending on
\beta
).
However, this
\beta
-dependence can be removed by a technical argument, which we will present in the next subsection.
The estimate above on the influence of
s^{(j)}_{E}
implies that for at most one value of
\omega(\bar{y})
do we have
|s^{(j)}_{E}-E|\leq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}
. Consequently, inequality (
4.68
) fails for at most one value of
\omega(\bar{y})\in\{0,1\}
.

(Case 2:
\widehat{n}\geq 2
.)

When
\widehat{n}\geq 2
, then the spread of matrix
s^{(j)}_{E}
is well-defined (see (
B.2
) for the definition). Since the matrix
\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})
is of rank-one, we have

{\rm Spread}(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y}))=|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

Moreover, clearly we have

\displaystyle{\rm Spread}\left(\sum_{z\sim\bar{y}\atop z\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(z)-\lambda}\cdot(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(z)+\mathbf{a}(z)\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y}))\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\cdot\left\|\sum_{z\sim\bar{y}\atop z\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(z)-\lambda}\cdot(\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(z)+\mathbf{a}(z)\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y}))\right\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2},

where we used that
\bar{y}
maximizes
|\mathbf{a}(y)|
and
\|\mathbf{v}\otimes\mathbf{w}\|=|\mathbf{v}|\cdot|\mathbf{w}|
for any vectors
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}
. Again, choose
h
large enough (depending only on
d
) and we can ensure

(4.72)

\frac{8d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\leq\frac{8d}{h-2d-2}<\frac{1}{2}.

Therefore, applying spread estimate argument, Corollary
B.2
, yields

(4.73)

{\rm Spread}(\mathcal{M})\geq\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{a}(\omega(\bar{y}))|^{2}.

Now set

M_{1}=s^{(j)}_{E}\big|_{\omega(\bar{y})=0},\ M_{2}=s^{(j)}_{E}\big|_{\omega(\bar{y})=0}^{\omega(\bar{y})=1}.

Then

M_{2}=\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}\bigg|_{\omega(\bar{y})=0}^{\omega(\bar{y})=1}\cdot\mathcal{M}+(\mathcal{R}(1)-\mathcal{R}(0)).

Applying Corollary
B.2
, we can obtain

\displaystyle{\rm Spread}(M_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\left|\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}\bigg|_{\omega(\bar{y})=0}^{\omega(\bar{y})=1}\right|\cdot{\rm Spread}(\mathcal{M})-{\rm Spread}(\mathcal{R}(1)-\mathcal{R}(0))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\beta|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}-2\|\mathcal{R}(1)-\mathcal{R}(0)\|\geq(\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\beta-2\gamma^{2.5})\cdot|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}

(4.74)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\beta}{4}\gamma^{2}t^{2}_{j-1}\gg 10\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}.

Here, we again require
h
to be
large enough (depending on
\beta
)
.

Now, without loss of generality, we assume that at the value
\omega(\bar{y})=0
,
\widehat{n}_{s}=\widehat{n}
. This implies

{\rm Spec}(M_{1})\subset I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E),

and therefore

{\rm Spread}(M_{1})\leq 4\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}.

Thus, for the another value
\omega(\bar{y})=1
, we apply Corollary
B.2
to obtain

\displaystyle{\rm Spread}(s^{(j)}_{E}\big|_{\omega(\bar{y})=1})

\displaystyle={\rm Spread}(M_{1}+M_{2})\geq{\rm Spread}(M_{2})-{\rm Spread}(M_{1})

\displaystyle\geq 10\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}-4\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}=6\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}.

This means that at least one eigenvalue of
s^{(j)}_{E}
at
\omega(\bar{y})=1
is out of
I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)
. Thus, (
4.68
) holds for
\omega(\bar{y})=1
. The above discussion implies that (
4.68
) fails for at most one value of
\omega(\bar{y})\in\{0,1\}
.

Now, recalling the Claim
4.6
, the following claim will conclude the proof of Lemma
4.3
.

Claim 4.12
.

\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})\leq\widehat{n}_{s}
.

Proof of Claim
4.12
.

Since
s^{(j)}_{E}
is the Schur complement of

\widetilde{S}^{(j)}_{E}(B_{j})=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{q}(E)&\tilde{r}(E)\\
\tilde{s}(E)&\tilde{t}(E)\end{pmatrix}.

In the previous content, we already show that
\|\tilde{r}(E)\|,\|\tilde{s}(E)\|\leq 2g_{j-1}
and
\|(\tilde{t}(E)-E)^{-1}\|\leq\frac{4}{\varepsilon_{j-1}}
.
Assume
\lambda_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})}
are the all eigenvalues of
\widetilde{S}^{(j)}_{E}(B_{j})
in
I_{\varepsilon_{j}}(E)
(counting the multiplicities).
Since
\varepsilon_{j}\ll\frac{\varepsilon_{j-1}}{8}
, Lemma
4.2
ensures that for each
\lambda_{i}
, there is an eigenvalue
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}
of
s^{(j)}_{E}
such that

|\lambda_{i}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}|\leq(\frac{g_{j-1}}{2\varepsilon_{j-1}})^{2}|\lambda_{i}-E|\leq\gamma^{2(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}|\lambda_{i}-E|.

Therefore,

|\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}-E|\leq|\lambda_{i}-E|+|\lambda_{i}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}|\leq 2\varepsilon_{j}.

Thus,
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}\in I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)
.

However, the Remark
4.1
reveals that the correspondence of
\lambda_{i}
and
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i}
does not necessarily coincide with the actual ordering (with multiplicities) in the spectrum (just like Corollary
B.1
). Thus, we require a further analysis centered on approximate orthogonality. By Lemma
4.2
, we can set

\mathbf{v}_{i}=\langle\mathbf{w}_{i},-(\tilde{t}(E)-\lambda_{i})^{-1}\tilde{s}(E)\mathbf{w}_{i}\rangle,\ \|\mathbf{w}_{i}\|=1,\ 1\leq i\leq\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})

to be the eigenvector of
\widetilde{S}^{(j)}_{E}(B_{j})
with respect to
\lambda_{i}
. Since
\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}},i\neq i^{\prime}
are multually orthogonal, we have

|\langle\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{w}_{i^{\prime}}\rangle|=|\langle\mathbf{w}_{i},\tilde{r}(E)(\tilde{t}(E)-\lambda_{i})^{-1}(\tilde{t}(E)-\lambda_{i^{\prime}})^{-1}\tilde{s}(E)\mathbf{w}_{i^{\prime}}\rangle|,\ i\neq i^{\prime}.

Since

|\lambda_{i}-E|,\ |\lambda_{i^{\prime}}-E|\leq\varepsilon_{j}\ll\varepsilon_{j-1},

we have
\|(\tilde{t}(E)-\lambda_{i})^{-1}\|,\ \|(\tilde{t}(E)-\lambda_{i^{\prime}})^{-1}\|\leq\frac{5}{\varepsilon_{j-1}}
. Therefore, for
i\neq i^{\prime}
, we have

(4.75)

|\langle\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{w}_{i^{\prime}}\rangle|\lesssim(\frac{g_{j-1}}{\varepsilon_{j-1}})^{2}=\gamma^{2(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}.

Moreover, by Lemma
4.2
,
\mathbf{w}_{i}
is the eigenvector of the Schur complement

\mathcal{S}_{i}=\tilde{q}(E)-\tilde{r}(E)(\tilde{t}(E)-\lambda_{i})^{-1}\tilde{s}(E).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|(s^{(j)}_{E}-E)\mathbf{w}_{i}\|

\displaystyle=\|(s^{(j)}_{E}-E)\mathbf{w}_{i}-(\mathcal{S}_{i}-\lambda_{i})\mathbf{w}_{i}\|

\displaystyle\leq(1+\|\tilde{r}(E)(\tilde{t}(E)-\lambda_{i})^{-1}(\tilde{t}(E)-E)^{-1}\tilde{s}(E)\|)\cdot|E-\lambda_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon_{j}.

Now denote the spectral projection of
s^{(j)}_{E}
on
I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)
by
P_{I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)}
, and the spectral projection on
\mathbb{R}\setminus I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)
by
P_{I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)^{c}}
. We have

\|(s^{(j)}_{E}-E)\mathbf{w}_{i}\|\geq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}\|P_{I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)^{c}}\mathbf{w}_{i}\|.

Hence,

(4.76)

\|P_{I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)^{c}}\mathbf{w}_{i}\|\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}.

Denote
P_{I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)}\mathbf{w}_{i}
by
\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}
. Then

(4.77)

\|\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i}\|^{2}=1-\|P_{I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)^{c}}\mathbf{w}_{i}\|^{2}=1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{j}).

By (
4.75
) and (
4.76
), for
i\neq i^{\prime}
, we also have

(4.78)

\displaystyle|\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i^{\prime}}\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle\mathbf{w}_{i}-P_{I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)^{c}}\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{w}_{i^{\prime}}-P_{I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)^{c}}\mathbf{w}_{i^{\prime}}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{2(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}+3\gamma^{\frac{a}{2}(a+1+0.2)L_{j-1}}=\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{2(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}).

Then combining (
4.77
) and (
4.78
) implies

(4.79)

|\langle\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i},\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i^{\prime}}\rangle|=\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{2(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}),\ 1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j}).

Moreover, we have
\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})\leq\#B_{j}
and

\#B_{j}\cdot\gamma^{2(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}\leq L_{0}^{d}\gamma^{2(a-1-0.5)L_{j-1}}\ll 1

supposing
k\gg 1
. Thus, we can apply Lemma
B.2
to conclude that
\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}_{i},1\leq i\leq\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})
are linearly independent in the subspace
{\rm Range}(P_{I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)})
(the range of the operator
P_{I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)}
). Therefore,

\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})\leq{\rm dim}\ {\rm Range}(P_{I_{2\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}}(E)})=\widehat{n}_{s}.

∎

Finally, combining Claim
4.6
, (
4.68
) and Claim
4.12
together, we have proven that, after conditioning on the randomness in
\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\{\bar{y}\}
, the event that

\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})\leq\widehat{n}_{s}<\widehat{n}\leq\widehat{n}_{j-1}(B_{j-1})

occurs for at least one value of
V_{\text{r},\bar{y}}=\omega(\bar{y})\in\{0,1\}
. Therefore, (
4.9
) is proved.

∎

4.4.
Removing the
\beta
-dependence of the barrier height

In the proof above, we observe that the required barrier height
h
depends on the coupling strength
\beta
, and as
\beta\to 0
, the height
h
tends to infinity (see (
4.3
) and (
4.3
)). This is definitely not satisfactory if one tries to extend the present method to handle the ABM on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for
d\geq 4
. However, using the trick described below, we can remove this
\beta
-dependence of
h
, thereby obtain a non‑perturbative lower bound on the barrier height with respect to the coupling strength.

From (
4.3
) and (
4.3
), where the
\beta
-dependence of
h
originates from, it is clear that the dependence is due primarily to the quantity

\gamma^{2.5}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2},

which arises from estimating the remainder term
\mathcal{R}_{1}(\omega(\bar{y}))
(See Claim
4.8
). Since the remainder term
\mathcal{R}_{1}(\omega(\bar{y}))
comes from the
K_{2}
in decomposition (
4.44
), consequently, to improve the estimate of this remainder term, it needs to expand the random leading term
K_{1}(x,y)
into higher orders terms. So we redefine

\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y)=\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(0)}(x,y)\cup\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}(x,y)\cup\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(2)}(x,y)

with

\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(0)}(x,y)=\left\{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y):\ \bar{y}\notin g\right\},

\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}(x,y)=\left\{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y):\ \bar{y}\in g,\ {\rm length}(g)\leq\mathcal{L}\right\},

\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(2)}(x,y)=\left\{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}(x,y):\ \bar{y}\in g,\ {\rm length}(g)\geq\mathcal{L}+1\right\}.

Here,
\mathcal{L}
is a large length parameter that will be selected later in the argument. The decomposition
K_{0},K_{1},K_{2}
will still be given by (
4.45
). In this setup, applying the argument used in the proof of Claim
4.8
directly yields that the upper bound on
\mathcal{R}_{1}(\omega(\bar{y}))
will become

(4.80)

\|\mathcal{R}_{1}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|\leq\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{0.85(\mathcal{L}+2)}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

However, the leading term (which comes from graphs in
\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}(x,y)
and
K_{1}
) in Lemma
4.7
will contain more terms, and will become

(4.81)

-\sum_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}K_{1}(x,y){\bf a}(x)\otimes{\bf a}(y).

If we denote

\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}=\bigcup_{x,y\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}(x,y),

then (
4.81
) becomes

(4.82)

-\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}}\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(x_{0})-\lambda}\cdot\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(x_{1})-\lambda}\cdots\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(x_{s})-\lambda}\mathbf{a}(x_{1})\otimes\mathbf{a}(x_{s}),

where
\bar{y}\in g=(x_{0},\cdots,x_{s}),x_{i}\sim x_{i+1}
and
x_{0},x_{s}\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}
. We denote by

\mathfrak{t}(g)=\#\{0\leq i\leq s:\ x_{s}=\bar{y}\}

the time of the random walk
g
arriving at
\bar{y}
.
We do a further decomposition on
\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}
based on the length of random walks

\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}=\bigcup_{0\leq s\leq\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1),s},

where

\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1),s}=\left\{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1)}:\ {\rm length}(g)=s\right\}.

Using the notation introduced above, equation (
4.82
) becomes

(4.83)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}(\omega(\bar{y})):=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})

\displaystyle-\sum_{1\leq s\leq\mathcal{L}}\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1),s}}\left(\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}\right)^{\mathfrak{t}(g)}\prod_{x_{i}\in g\atop x_{i}\neq\bar{y}}\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(x_{i})-\lambda}\cdot\mathbf{a}(x_{0})\otimes\mathbf{a}(x_{s})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})\otimes\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})+\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(\omega(\bar{y})).

Clearly, the estimates in Claim
4.9
, Claim
4.10
and Claim
4.9
remain valid, and thus the Lemma
4.7
will become

\displaystyle s^{(j)}_{E}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{h+2d+\beta\omega(\bar{y})-\lambda}=\mathfrak{L}(\omega(\bar{y}))+\mathcal{D}+\mathcal{R}(\omega(\bar{y})).

Here
\mathcal{D}
is a deterministic term, and the random term
\mathcal{R}(\omega(\bar{y}))
satisfies

\|\mathcal{R}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|\leq\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{0.85(\mathcal{L}+1)}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{0.5L_{j-1}}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

Choose
k
sufficiently large to ensure
L_{j-1}\geq L_{0}\sim d_{k}\geq 10\mathcal{L}
. Then this will yield

(4.84)

\|\mathcal{R}(\omega(\bar{y}))\|\leq\gamma^{0.85(\mathcal{L}+1)}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

Moreover, for the higher order leading term
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(\omega(\bar{y}))
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\omega(\bar{y})\in(0,1)}\left\|\frac{d}{d\omega(\bar{y})}\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(\omega(\bar{y}))\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\beta\cdot\sum_{1\leq s\leq\mathcal{L}}\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1),s}}\mathfrak{t}(g)\left(\frac{1}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{s+2}|\mathbf{a}(x_{0})|\cdot|\mathbf{a}(x_{s})|

\displaystyle\leq\beta|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}\sum_{1\leq s\leq\mathcal{L}}(s+1)\cdot\left(\#\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1),s}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{s+2}.

In the above estimate, we used that
\bar{y}
maximizes
|\mathbf{a}(y)|
, and that
\mathfrak{t}(g)\leq{\rm length}(g)+1
. Since the random walks in
\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1),s}
must pass through the fixed site
\bar{y}
, we have
\#\mathcal{G}_{\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{j-1}}^{(1),s}\leq(s+1)(2d)^{s}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{\omega(\bar{y})\in(0,1)}\left\|\frac{d}{d\omega(\bar{y})}\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(\omega(\bar{y}))\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\beta|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}\sum_{1\leq s\leq\mathcal{L}}(s+1)^{2}\cdot(2d)^{s}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{h-2d-\beta-\iota}\right)^{s+2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d}\beta(\frac{2d}{h-2d-\beta-\iota})^{3}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}

(4.85)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{10}\beta\gamma^{2}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2},

which is uniform about
\mathcal{L}\geq 1
.
The validity of (
4.4
) also needs the largeness of
h
, which only depends on
d
. Hence, applying the mean value theorem, we have

(4.86)

\|\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(0)-\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(1)\|\leq\frac{1}{5}\beta\gamma^{2}|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

We have the following cases:

(Case 1:
\widehat{n}=1
.)

By (
4.69
) and (
4.86
), we have

\|\mathfrak{L}(1)-\mathfrak{L}(0)\|\geq\gamma^{2}\beta|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}-\|\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(0)-\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(1)\|\geq\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\beta|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2},

and thus by (
4.84
), the estimate (
4.3
) becomes

\displaystyle\left|\ s^{(j)}_{E}\big|_{\omega(\bar{y})=0}^{\omega(\bar{y})=1}\ \right|

\displaystyle\geq(\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\beta-2\gamma^{0.85(\mathcal{L}+2)})\cdot|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}

(4.87)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\beta}{4}\gamma^{2}t^{2}_{j-1}\gg 4\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}.

Now, (
4.4
) only requires us to choose
\mathcal{L}
large enough so that

\gamma^{0.85(\mathcal{L}+2)-2}\ll\beta\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{L}\gg\log\beta/\log\gamma.

So we remove the
\beta
-dependence of
h
in this case.

(Case 2:
\widehat{n}\geq 2
.)

The case
\widehat{n}\geq 2
can be handled in a similar manner. By (
4.69
), (
4.86
) and Corollary
B.2
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm Spread}(\mathfrak{L}(1)-\mathfrak{L}(0))

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{2}\beta|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}-{\rm Spread}(\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(1)-\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(0))

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{2}\beta|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}-2\|\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(1)-\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(0)\|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\beta|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}.

Therefore, by (
4.84
), the estimate (
4.3
) becomes

\displaystyle{\rm Spread}(M_{2})

\displaystyle\geq{\rm Spread}(\mathfrak{L}(1)-\mathfrak{L}(0))-{\rm Spread}(\mathcal{R}(1)-\mathcal{R}(0))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\beta|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}-2\|\mathcal{R}(1)-\mathcal{R}(0)\|\geq(\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\beta-2\gamma^{0.85(\mathcal{L}+2)})\cdot|\mathbf{a}(\bar{y})|^{2}

(4.88)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\beta}{4}\gamma^{2}t^{2}_{j-1}\gg 10\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}

provided
\mathcal{L}
large enough. We remove the
\beta
-dependence of
h
again.

Finally, since the largeness requirements for the barrier height
h
arising elsewhere depend only on the dimension
d
(see (
4.16
), (
4.23
), (
4.49
), (
4.62
), (
4.70
), (
4.72
) and (
4.4
)), the final choice of
h_{0}
depends solely on
d
as well.
Moreover, observing the requirement (
4.16
), if we assume
h_{0}(d)\sim d^{n}
grows polynomially in
d
, we must have

d^{-(2a-0.2)(n-1)}\lesssim d^{-(2a-0.3)n},

which gives
n>20a-2
. Consequently, with the choice of
a\geq 5
, we obtain at least that
h_{0}(d)\gg d^{50}\gg 4d+1
. This is the condition we imposed on the barrier height in Theorem
1.3
.

4.5.
A martingale type argument

Based on the discussions above, we have shown that there exists a large constant
h_{0}(d)
such that, if we choose
h>h_{0}(d)
, then for all sufficiently large scales
k
, Lemma
4.3
holds true. We denote by
k^{(2)}
(depending only on
h,\beta,d_{0}
) the smallest
k
for which the above argument remains valid.

We now proceed to prove the Wegner estimate, i.e., Theorem
4.1
. Lemma
4.3
indicates that the transversality at some point on the boundary of
\overline{B}_{j-1}
can, with high probability, move at least one eigenvalue away from the energy
E
. Therefore, we want to select a sufficiently large collection of points so that all eigenvalues closed to
E
are successively shifted away. This procedure will generate a martingale with a particular structure, which then allows us to complete the proof of Theorem
4.1
via a large deviation estimate.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Throughout the proof, we let
k\geq k_{\text{in}}>\max\{k^{(1)},k^{(2)}\}
, where
k^{(1)}
is the lower bound of scales such that Lemma
3.2
holds true.

Recall that we take the
a
-adic scales as in (
4.2
) and (
4.4
). Moreover, recall that we set
B_{j}\equiv\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}
and
\overline{B}_{j}
the
aL_{j-1}
-neighborhood of
B_{j}
for
0\leq j\leq P
. In this proof, we just let
a=5
.

We construct a filtration of the probability space
\{0,1\}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
as follows.

First, we set

(4.89)

\mathcal{F}_{0}=\sigma(\overline{B}_{0})

to be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the randomness in
\overline{B}_{0}
. Letting
\overline{\omega}_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{\overline{B}_{0}}
denote the configuration of this randomness,
\mathcal{F}_{0}
induces the cylindrical decomposition

\{0,1\}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0}}\{\overline{\omega}_{0}\}\times\{0,1\}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\overline{B}_{0}}:=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0}}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\omega}_{0}}.

Now we can apply Lemma
4.3
. If
\overline{\omega}_{0}
is fixed, then we can find a point
\bar{y}_{1}=\bar{y}_{1}(\overline{\omega}_{0})\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{0}
(that is to say
\bar{y}_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable) satisfying the conclusions of Lemma
4.3
.
Next, for each fixed
\overline{\omega}_{0}
(and then
\bar{y}_{1}
is fixed), we set the configuration of the randomness in
\overline{B}_{1}\setminus\{\bar{y}_{1}\}
to be
\widehat{\omega}_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{\overline{B}_{1}\setminus(\overline{B}_{0}\cup\{\bar{y}_{1}\})}
. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra matching with the following cylindrical decomposition

\{0,1\}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0}}\{(\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0})\}\times\{0,1\}^{(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\overline{B}_{1})\cup\{\bar{y}_{1}\}}:=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0}}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0}}.

Then, clearly
\mathcal{F}_{0}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{0}
.

If we condition on
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
, i.e., we just consider the randomness in each cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0}}
, the randomness of
V_{\text{r},\bar{y}_{1}}=\omega(\bar{y}_{1})
is still free and an application of the Lemma
4.3
yields

(4.90)

\mathbb{P}_{\omega(\bar{y}_{1})}\left(\widehat{n}_{1}(B_{1})<\widehat{n}_{0}(B_{0})\ {\rm or}\ \widehat{n}_{1}(B_{1})=\widehat{n}_{0}(B_{0})=0\ \bigg|\ V_{\text{r},\overline{B}_{j}\setminus\{\bar{y}\}}=(\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0})\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Here
\mathbb{P}_{\omega(\bar{y}_{1})}
means that indeed the probability is only about the randomness of
\omega(\bar{y}_{1})
. We denote the above event by

{\rm Event}_{1}:\ \widehat{n}_{1}(B_{1})<\widehat{n}_{0}(B_{0})\ {\rm or}\ \widehat{n}_{1}(B_{1})=\widehat{n}_{0}(B_{0})=0\,

and let
\mathcal{Z}_{1}=\mathbf{1}_{{\rm Event}_{1}}(\omega(\bar{y}_{1}))
. Then
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-measurable, where

\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma(\overline{B}_{1})

is the the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the randomness in
\overline{B}_{1}
. Clearly,
\mathcal{Q}_{0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1}
, and if we set the configuration of
\omega(\bar{y}_{1})
to be
\overline{\omega}_{1}
, then
\mathcal{F}_{1}
will match with the following cylindrical decomposition

\{0,1\}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1}}\{(\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1})\}\times\{0,1\}^{(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\overline{B}_{1})}:=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1}}.

Moreover, by (
4.90
), we have

(4.91)

\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Z}_{1})=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}\ |\ \mathcal{C}_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0}}))\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Now, since
\mathcal{F}_{1}
realizes the randomness in
\overline{B}_{1}
, applying Lemma
4.3
again will yield a
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-measurable
\bar{y}_{2}=\bar{y}_{2}(\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1})\in\partial^{+}\overline{B}_{1}
. If we condition on
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, then the value of
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
and the choice of
\bar{y}_{2}
will be fixed. We then define
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
as the
\sigma
-algebra matching with the following cylindrical decomposition

\{0,1\}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{1}}\{(\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{1})\}\times\{0,1\}^{(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\overline{B}_{2})\cup\{\bar{y}_{2}\}}:=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{1}},

where
\widehat{\omega}_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{\overline{B}_{2}\setminus(\overline{B}_{1}\cup\{\bar{y}_{2}\})}
is the configuration of the randomness in
{\overline{B}_{2}\setminus(\overline{B}_{1}\cup\{\bar{y}_{2}\})}
. Then, clearly
\mathcal{F}_{1}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{1}
. If we denote the event

{\rm Event}_{2}:\ \ \widehat{n}_{2}(B_{2})<\widehat{n}_{1}(B_{1})\ {\rm or}\ \widehat{n}_{2}(B_{2})=\widehat{n}_{1}(B_{1})=0,

then using Lemma
4.3
yields that in each cylinder
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{1}}
,

(4.92)

\mathbb{P}_{\omega(\bar{y}_{2})}\left({\rm Event}_{2}\ \bigg|\ \mathcal{C}_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{1}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Therefore, we can define
\mathcal{Z}_{2}=\mathbf{1}_{{\rm Event}_{2}}(\omega(\bar{y}_{2}))
, which is measurable in the
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{F}_{2}=\sigma(\overline{B}_{2}).

Moreover, since
\mathcal{Z}_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-measurable,
\mathcal{F}_{1}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{1}\subset\mathcal{F}_{2}
and (
4.92
), we have

(4.93)

\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}\ |\ \mathcal{Z}_{1})=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}\ |\ \mathcal{C}_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\overline{\omega}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{1}})|\mathcal{Z}_{1})\geq\frac{1}{2}.

By iterating the above construction, we obtain the following filtration

\mathcal{F}_{0}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{F}_{P-1}\subset\mathcal{Q}_{P-1}\subset\mathcal{F}_{P}:=\sigma(\overline{\Lambda}_{k})

together with a (site-mixed) martingale
(\bar{y}_{j+1},\mathcal{Z}_{j}),0\leq j\leq P
satisfying:

•

We set
\mathcal{Z}_{0}=0
. We have
(\bar{y}_{j+1},\mathcal{Z}_{j})
is
\mathcal{F}_{j}
-measurable. The realization of
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
happens between the two filtration elements
\mathcal{Q}_{j-1}\subset\mathcal{F}_{j}
. Moreover,

(4.94)

\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Z}_{j}\ |\ \mathcal{Z}_{j-1})\geq\frac{1}{2},\ 1\leq j\leq P.

•

The
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{j}
match with the cylindrical decomposition

\{0,1\}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\cdots,\overline{\omega}_{j}}\{(\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\cdots,\overline{\omega}_{j})\}\times\{0,1\}^{(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\overline{B}_{j})}:=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\cdots,\overline{\omega}_{j}}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\cdots,\overline{\omega}_{j}}

and the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
match with the cylindrical decomposition

\{0,1\}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\cdots,\overline{\omega}_{j},\widehat{\omega}_{j}}\{(\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\cdots,\overline{\omega}_{j},\widehat{\omega}_{j})\}\times\{0,1\}^{(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\overline{B}_{j+1})\cup\{\bar{y}_{j+1}\}}:=\bigcup_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\cdots,\overline{\omega}_{j},\widehat{\omega}_{j}}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\omega}_{0},\widehat{\omega}_{0},\cdots,\overline{\omega}_{j},\widehat{\omega}_{j}}.

Here we say the martingale is
“site-mixed”
, because during the process the site
\bar{y}_{j}
itself is a random variable, and the filtration
\mathcal{Q}_{j-1}
is constructed based on the realization of
\bar{y}_{j}
. (For a visual illustration of this construction, see Figure
5
.)

\cdots
\bar{y}_{1}
\bar{y}_{2}
\bar{y}_{3}
\bar{y}_{P}
\overline{B}_{0}
\overline{B}_{1}
\overline{B}_{2}
\overline{B}_{P-1}
\overline{B}_{P}
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
L_{P}\sim d_{k}^{\sqrt{\alpha}}
\sim d_{k+1}

Figure 5.
The construction of the chain of sites in the martingale.

Now, we let

(4.95)

\mathcal{X}_{j}=\mathcal{Z}_{1}+\mathcal{Z}_{2}+\cdots+\mathcal{Z}_{j},\ 1\leq j\leq P.

Moreover, we let
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{j}=\mathcal{Z}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}
and

(4.96)

\mathcal{Y}_{j}=\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}+\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}+\cdots+\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{j}=\mathcal{X}_{j}-\frac{j}{2},\ 1\leq j\leq P.

Then applying (
4.94
) yields

\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{j+1}\ |\ \widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{j})\geq 0

and therefore

\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Y}_{j+1}\ |\ \mathcal{Y}_{j})\geq\mathcal{Y}_{j}.

This means
(\mathcal{Y}_{j})_{1\leq j\leq P}
is a submartingale. Moreover, by our construction,

|\mathcal{Y}_{j+1}-\mathcal{Y}_{j}|=|\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{j+1}|\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Hence we can apply the Azuma’s inequality and (
4.3
) to obtain

(4.97)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{X}_{P}\leq\frac{1}{10}P\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{Y}_{P}\leq-(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{10})P\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left\{-2\frac{\big((\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{10})P\big)^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{P}(\frac{1}{2})^{2}}\right\}\leq\exp\{-P\}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}-1}{\alpha\cdot\log 5}\cdot\log d_{k+1}\right\}=d_{k+1}^{-q}

for some constant
q>0
.

Clearly, the event
\mathcal{X}_{P}>\frac{1}{10}P
represents that the strict monotonicity in (
4.9
) happens more than
\frac{1}{10}P
times, and therefore (Lemma
4.3
tells us that deterministically
\widehat{n}_{j}(B_{j})\leq\widehat{n}_{j-1}(B_{j-1})
)

(4.98)

\widehat{n}_{P}(B_{P})\leq\max\left\{\ \widehat{n}_{0}(B_{0})-\frac{1}{10}P,\ 0\ \right\}.

Now, recall that the
\widehat{n}_{0}(B_{0})
is the number of eigenvalues (counting multiplicities) of the Schur complement

(4.99)

\widetilde{S}^{(0)}_{E}(B_{0})=H_{B_{0}}-\Gamma_{0}^{\top}(H_{\overline{B}_{0}\setminus B_{0}}-E)^{-1}\Gamma_{0}

in the interval
I_{\varepsilon_{0}}(E)
for
\varepsilon_{0}=\gamma^{6.2L_{0}}
. Since
\overline{B}_{0}\setminus B_{0}
is non-resonant, by Lemma
3.1
, we get

\|\widetilde{S}^{(0)}_{E}(B_{0})-H_{B_{0}}\|\leq\|\Gamma_{0}\|^{2}\cdot\|G_{\overline{B}_{0}\setminus B_{0}}(E)\|\leq\frac{4d^{2}}{h-4d-\beta-\iota}\leq\frac{\iota}{4}.

The last inequality follows from our choice
h\geq h_{0}(d)\gg d^{50}
with
d\geq 4
(so that
\iota=\frac{1}{100}
by (
2.2
)). Therefore, applying Corollary
B.1
will yield

(4.100)

\widehat{n}_{0}(B_{0})\leq\#\left({\rm Spec}(H_{B_{0}})\cap I_{\varepsilon_{0}+\frac{\iota}{4}}(E)\right).

Recall that we fix
E\in[-\frac{\iota}{2},4d+\beta+\frac{\iota}{2}]
and
\varepsilon_{0}\ll\frac{\iota}{4}
if we choose
k
large. So

I_{\varepsilon_{0}+\frac{\iota}{4}}(E)\subset[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota].

By (
4.100
),
B_{0}=\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}
and Remark
3.1
, we obtain

(4.101)

\widehat{n}_{0}(B_{0})\leq\#\left({\rm Spec}(H_{\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}})\cap[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]\right)\leq 2N^{k-k^{(1)}}(16d_{k^{(1)}}+1)^{d}.

Therefore, by (
4.98
) and (
4.3
), the event
\mathcal{X}_{P}>\frac{1}{10}P
implies

(4.102)

\displaystyle\widehat{n}_{P}(B_{P})

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{2N^{k-k^{(1)}}(16d_{k^{(1)}}+1)^{d}-C\cdot(\sqrt{\alpha}-1)\frac{\log d_{0}}{\log a}\cdot\alpha^{k},\ 0\right\}=0.

The validity of (
4.102
) can be ensured by taking sufficiently large
k
(depending only on
h,\beta,d_{0},\alpha,N
), since
we assume
\alpha>N
and
k^{(1)}
depends only on
h,\beta,d_{0},\alpha,N
. Thus, by (
4.97
), we have

(4.103)

\mathbb{P}\left(\widehat{n}_{P}(B_{P})=0\right)\geq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{X}_{P}>\frac{1}{10}P)\geq 1-d_{k+1}^{-q}.

Finally, we return the control of
\widehat{n}_{P}(B_{P})
back to counting the number of eigenvalues of
H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
. Assume there exists a
\lambda
lying in
{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{B}_{P}})\cap I_{\varepsilon_{P}/2}(E)
. Then by Lemma
4.2
,
\lambda
is also an eigenvalue of the Schur complement
\widetilde{S}^{(P)}_{\lambda}(B_{P})
. Moreover, since we choose
h>h_{0}(d)
, we can use Claim
4.5
to deduce that

\|\widetilde{S}^{(P)}_{\lambda}(B_{P})-\widetilde{S}^{(P)}_{E}(B_{P})\|\leq\gamma^{1.9}|\lambda-E|\leq\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{1.9}\varepsilon_{P}.

Therefore, by Corollary
B.1
, there must be an eigenvalue
\widetilde{\lambda}
of
\widetilde{S}^{(P)}_{E}(B_{P})
such that

|\widetilde{\lambda}-E|\leq|\widetilde{\lambda}-\lambda|+|\lambda-E|\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{1.9}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\varepsilon_{P}<\varepsilon_{P},

which means
\widehat{n}_{P}(B_{P})>0
. Thus, (
4.102
) tells us

(4.104)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\rm dist}({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{B}_{P}}),E)<\varepsilon_{P}=\gamma^{6.2L_{P}}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}(\widehat{n}_{P}(B_{P})>0)\leq d_{k+1}^{-q}.

However, (
4.104
) only ensures an exponential decay upper bound with respect the block length
{\rm diam}(\overline{B}_{P})=L_{P}
. But we need a sub-exponential decay upper bound in (
4.1
). So we need the following additional bootstrap argument.

Denote the Schur complement of
H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}-\mathcal{E}
about the block matrix restricted in
B_{P}=\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}
by

\widetilde{S}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}_{\mathcal{E}}(B_{P})=H_{B_{P}}-\Gamma_{P}^{\top}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus B_{P}}-E)^{-1}\Gamma_{P}.

For
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\in[-\iota,4d+\beta+\iota]
, the argument used in the proof of Claim
4.10
can be applied analogously to yield

(4.105)

\|\widetilde{S}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}_{\mathcal{E}}(B_{P})-\widetilde{S}^{(P)}_{\mathcal{E}}(B_{P})\|\leq\gamma^{(2a-0.3)L_{P}}=\gamma^{9.7L_{P}}.

Now assume there is a
\lambda_{0}
lying in
{\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}_{E}(B_{P}))\cap I_{\varepsilon_{P}/10}(E)
. By (
4.105
), we have

\|\widetilde{S}^{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}_{E}(B_{P})-\widetilde{S}^{(P)}_{E}(B_{P})\|\leq\gamma^{(2a-0.3)L_{P}}=\gamma^{9.7L_{P}}=g_{P},

and thus Corollary
B.1
implies there is an eigenvalue
\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}
of
\widetilde{S}^{(P)}_{E}(B_{P})
such that

|E-\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}|\leq|\lambda_{0}-E|+|\lambda_{0}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}|<g_{P}+\frac{\varepsilon_{P}}{10}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{P}}{5}.

Using Claim
4.5
, we also have

\|\widetilde{S}^{(P)}_{E}(B_{P})-\widetilde{S}^{(P)}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}}(B_{P})\|\leq\gamma^{1.9}|E-\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}|,

and thus Corollary
B.1
implies there is an eigenvalue
\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}
of
\widetilde{S}^{(P)}_{\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}}(B_{P})
such that
