|\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}|\leq\gamma^{1.9}|E-\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}|.

Therefore, the fixed-point argument leading to (
4.25
) will help us to find a
\widetilde{\lambda}
such that

(4.106)

\widetilde{\lambda}\in{\rm Spec}(\widetilde{S}^{(P)}_{\widetilde{\lambda}}(B_{P})),\ |\widetilde{\lambda}-E|<2|E-\widetilde{\lambda}_{0}|<\frac{2}{5}\varepsilon_{P}.

By Lemma
4.2
and (
4.106
), we yield
\widetilde{\lambda}\in{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{B}_{P}})
, and therefore

{\rm dist}({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{B}_{P}}),E)<\frac{2}{5}\varepsilon_{P}<\varepsilon_{P}.

That is to say, by (
4.104
),

(4.107)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\rm dist}({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}),E)<\frac{1}{10}\varepsilon_{P}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left({\rm dist}({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{B}_{P}}),E)<\varepsilon_{P}\right)\leq d_{k+1}^{-q}.

Finally, noticing that

\frac{1}{10}\varepsilon_{P}\sim\exp\{-C\cdot d_{k+1}^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}}\}\ll\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}

if we choose
\varepsilon
to be smaller than
1-\alpha^{-1/2}
, we finish the proof of the Wegner estimate (
4.1
).

The entire proof is conducted under the assumption that
k
is sufficiently large. We choose
k_{\text{in}}
to be large enough (depending only on
h,\beta,\alpha,d_{0},N
) to ensure the validity of the proof when
k\geq k_{\text{in}}
.

∎

Remark 4.4
.

The final bootstrap argument relies on the existence of an annular non-resonant region surrounding
\overline{B}_{P}
, in which a similar structure also presents in the important Bourgain’s geometric lemma
[
Bou07
]
in the quasi-periodic Schrödinger operators case.

5.
Elimination of the energy and the proof of the localization

In this section, we will use the Wegner estimates Theorem
3.3
and Theorem
4.1
to prove the our main theorems on the localization.

From now on, for every
(k,N)
-hierarchical structure we simply write
2\leq s\leq N
instead of
2\leq s\leq N_{k}\leq N
. This simplified notation does not affect any of the estimates, since we always have the bound
N_{k}\leq N
.

5.1.
Eliminating the energy

Recall that we use the notation
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
and
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}
to stand for the
\frac{1}{10}d_{k+1}
-neighborhood of
\Lambda_{k}
and
\Lambda_{k}(j_{s})
(for
s\geq 2
).

To prove the Anderson localization, it’s sufficient to establish exponential decay of all generalized eigenfunctions in the corresponding spectral interval. Since we have (
1.8
), the well-known Shnol’s lemma tells us that the spectral interval
[0,4d+\beta]
contains the following generalized eigenvalues (of full spectral measure):

(5.1)

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}(\omega)\in[0,4d+\beta]\ {\rm with\ generalized\ eigenfunction\ }\psi.

Here the generalized eigenfunction satisfies

(5.2)

|\psi(m_{\mathcal{E}})|\geq 1,\ |\psi(x)|\leq C_{1}(d)(|m_{\mathcal{E}}|+1)^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\cdot(|x|+1)^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\ {\rm for\ all\ }x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

We emphasize that both the generalized eigenvalue
\mathcal{E}
and the site
m_{\mathcal{E}}=m_{\mathcal{E}}(\omega)
depend on the random potential
V_{\text{r}}=\omega
, while the constant
C_{1}(d)
only depends on
d
(For the statement and proof of Shnol’s lemma, see e.g.
[
Kir08
, Proposition 7.4]
).

Now we choose an arbitrary generalized eigenvalue
\mathcal{E}\in[0,4d+\beta]
. Then we have

Lemma 5.1
.

For any
\omega\in\Omega
, there is a scale
K_{1}=K_{1}(\mathcal{E},\omega,h,\beta,d)>0
such that, for
\forall k\geq K_{1}
, we have

(5.3)

{\rm dist}\left(\mathcal{E},{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\cap[-\frac{\iota}{2},4d+\beta+\frac{\iota}{2}]\right)<\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{50}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\right\}.

Proof.

We decompose

H-\mathcal{E}=(H_{\mathcal{B}}-\mathcal{E})\oplus(H_{\mathcal{W}}-\mathcal{E})+\Gamma,

where
\Gamma
is the connecting matrix between the potential barrier
\mathcal{B}
and the potential well
\mathcal{W}
. Since
\mathcal{B}
is non-resonant, Lemma
3.1
, (
5.2
) and

(H-\mathcal{E})\psi=0,

we use the Poisson’s formula to get for all
x\in\mathcal{B}
,

(5.4)

\displaystyle|\psi(x)|

\displaystyle=\left|-\sum_{w\in\partial^{-}\mathcal{B}\atop w^{\prime}\in\partial^{+}\mathcal{B}}G_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{E})(x,w)\cdot\psi(w^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2dC_{1}(d)(|m_{\mathcal{E}}|+1)^{\frac{d}{2}+1}}{h-4d-\beta-\iota}\sum_{w\in\partial^{-}\mathcal{B}}\exp\{-\gamma_{0}|x-w|_{1}\}(2+|w|)^{\frac{d}{2}+1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,h,\beta,\omega,\mathcal{E}}\sum_{w\in\partial^{-}\mathcal{B}}\exp\{-\gamma_{0}|x-w|_{1}\}(2+|x|+|w-x|)^{\frac{d}{2}+1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,h,\beta,\omega,\mathcal{E}}(2+|x|)^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\sum_{k\geq{\rm dist}(x,\partial^{-}\mathcal{B})}k^{d+\frac{d}{2}+1}\exp\{-\gamma_{0}\cdot k\}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,h,\beta,\omega,\mathcal{E}}(2+|x|)^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\exp\{-\gamma_{0}{\rm dist}(x,\partial^{-}\mathcal{B})\}.

Therefore, if
x\in\partial^{\pm}\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
, then we have

{\rm dist}(x,\partial^{-}\mathcal{B})\geq\frac{1}{10}d_{k+1},

which combined with (
5.4
) implies

(5.5)

|\psi(x)|\lesssim_{d,h,\beta,\omega,\mathcal{E}}(2+\frac{1}{10}d_{k+1})^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\exp\{-\frac{1}{10}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}.

Take
\psi_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}=R_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}\psi
. It’s easy to check that
(H-\mathcal{E})\psi_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
supports only in
\partial^{\pm}\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
with

\displaystyle\|(H-\mathcal{E})\psi_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}\|

\displaystyle\leq 2d\cdot\sqrt{\#\partial^{\pm}\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}\cdot\|\psi\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\partial^{\pm}\overline{\Lambda}_{k})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,h,\beta,\omega,\mathcal{E}}(d_{k+1})^{{d}+1}\exp\{-\frac{1}{10}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}

(5.6)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{11}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}.

The validity of (
5.1
) requires
k
to be large enough (depending only on
d,h,\beta,\omega,\mathcal{E}
).

Now let
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\cdots,\mathcal{E}_{\#\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
denote all the eigenvalues of
H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
with corresponding normalized eigenvectors
\varphi_{1},\cdots,\varphi_{\#\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
. Since
\{\varphi_{s}\}_{1\leq s\leq\#\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
forms an eigenbasis of
\ell^{2}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k})
, we have

\psi_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}=\sum_{s}c_{s}\varphi_{s}.

Under this representation, using (
5.1
) yields

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{s:\ |\mathcal{E}_{s}-\mathcal{E}|\geq\exp\{-\frac{1}{50}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}}c_{s}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\exp\{-\frac{1}{50}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{s}|\mathcal{E}_{s}-\mathcal{E}|^{2}c_{s}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\|(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}-\mathcal{E})\psi_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|(H-\mathcal{E})\psi_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}\|\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{11}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}.

Therefore,

(5.7)

\sum_{s:\ |\mathcal{E}_{s}-\mathcal{E}|\geq\exp\{-\frac{1}{50}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}}c_{s}^{2}\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{11}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}.

Moreover, if we let
k
be sufficiently large so that
m_{\mathcal{E}}\in\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
(thus the largeness here depends on
\omega,\mathcal{E}
), we will obtain

(5.8)

\|\psi_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}\|^{2}=\sum_{s}c_{s}^{2}\geq|\psi(m_{\mathcal{E}})|^{2}\geq 1.

Combining (
5.7
) and (
5.8
) yields

\sum_{s:\ |\mathcal{E}_{s}-\mathcal{E}|<\exp\{-\frac{1}{50}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}}c_{s}^{2}\geq 1-\exp\{-\frac{1}{11}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}>0,

which means that there is some
\mathcal{E}_{s^{\prime}}\in{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})
such that

|\mathcal{E}_{s^{\prime}}-\mathcal{E}|<\exp\{-\frac{1}{50}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}\ll\frac{\iota}{2}.

Since we have chosen
\mathcal{E}\in[0,4d+\beta]
, we obtain
\mathcal{E}_{s^{\prime}}\in[-\frac{\iota}{2},4d+\beta+\frac{\iota}{2}]
. This proves (
5.3
).

∎

The next lemma concerns the separation of the spectrum, which is useful for eliminating the energy dependence in the Wegner estimate.

Lemma 5.2

(
Separation of the spectrum).

For almost every
\omega
, there exists a scale
K_{2}=K_{2}(\omega,h,d_{0},\beta,N,\alpha)>0
such that, for
\forall k\geq K_{2}
, we have

(5.9)

{\rm dist}\left({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}),{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}^{s}_{k}})\right)\geq\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

We take condition probability on
V_{\text{r},\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}
, i.e., the randomness in
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
. Then the spectrum

{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\cap[-\frac{\iota}{2},4d+\beta+\frac{\iota}{2}]:=\{E_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq M}

is now fixed, where we denote

M=\#\left({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}})\cap[-\frac{\iota}{2},4d+\beta+\frac{\iota}{2}]\right).

By Lemma
3.2
, we have
M\leq 2N^{k-k^{(1)}}(16d_{k^{(1)}}+1)^{d}
.

Now we can apply the Wegner estimates, Theorem
3.3
and Theorem
4.1
. We let
k\geq k_{\text{in}}
. For each pair
(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s},E_{i})
with
2\leq s\leq N
and
1\leq i\leq M
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left({\rm dist}\big({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}}),E_{i}\big)<\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}\ \bigg|\ V_{\text{r},\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}\right)\leq d_{k+1}^{-q}

and therefore,

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\rm dist}\big({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}),{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}^{s}_{k}})\big)<\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}\ \bigg|\ V_{\text{r},\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\exists 2\leq s\leq N,\ 1\leq i\leq M\ {\rm s.t.,}\ {\rm dist}\big({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}}),E_{i}\big)<\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}\ \bigg|\ V_{\text{r},\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle N\cdot M\cdot d_{k+1}^{-q}\leq 2N^{1-k^{(1)}}(16d_{k^{(1)}}+1)^{d}\cdot N^{k}d_{0}^{-q\cdot\alpha^{k}}.

Clearly,
\sum\limits_{k\geq 0}N^{k}d_{0}^{-q\cdot\alpha^{k}}<\infty
. Hence, by the Borel-Cantelli lemma, the event in (
5.10
) almost surely does not occur for all sufficiently large
k
. This means almost surely there will be a
K_{2}>0
(depending on
\omega
,
h,d_{0},\beta,N,\alpha
) such that, for any
k\geq K_{2}
, (
5.9
) happens.

∎

Since the parameters
h,d_{0},\beta,N,\alpha
have been fixed, in the remainder of the proof we will suppress their dependences of quantities in the notation.

5.2.
Proof of the Anderson localization

For almost every
\omega\in\Omega
, we obtain the following scales:

•

k\geq k_{\text{in}}\ \Rightarrow
The Wegner estimates hold true,

•

k\geq K_{1}(\omega,\mathcal{E})\ \Rightarrow
Lemma
5.3
holds true,

•

k\geq K_{2}(\omega)\ \Rightarrow
Lemma
5.2
holds true.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
.

For almost every
\omega
(fixed), we arbitrarily choose a generalized eigenvalue
\mathcal{E}(\omega)
in
[0,4d+\beta]
with the generalized eigenfunction
\psi
.

We take

k\geq\max\{k_{\text{in}},K_{1}(\omega,\mathcal{E}),K_{2}(\omega)\}:=k_{\text{AL}}(\omega).

Then, we can apply Lemma
5.3
and Lemma
5.2
to obtain

\displaystyle{\rm dist}(\mathcal{E},{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}}))\geq\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}-\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{50}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\right\}\geq\frac{1}{2}\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}

for any
2\leq s\leq N
. Therefore,

(5.11)

\|G_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}}(\mathcal{E})\|\leq 2\exp\{d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}\ {\rm for}\ \forall 2\leq s\leq N.

Let
D_{k}
denote the
2d_{k}
-neighborhood of
\Lambda_{k}
, and
D_{k}^{s}
the
2d_{k}
-neighborhood of
\Lambda_{k}(j_{s})
. Denote the annular region
D_{k+1}\setminus\Lambda_{k}
by
\mathcal{A}_{k}
. Then we have the following geometric structure:

•

\mathcal{A}_{k}
and
\mathcal{A}_{k+1}
have an overlap of size
2d_{k+1}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{k-1}\cap\mathcal{A}_{k+1}=\emptyset
,

•

\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}\subset\mathcal{A}_{k}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{k}\setminus(\cup_{2\leq s\leq N}\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s})
is non-resonant. Moreover,
{\rm dist}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s},D_{k})\geq\frac{9}{5}d_{k+1}
and
{\rm dist}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s},\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s^{\prime}})\geq\frac{17}{10}d_{k+1},s\neq s^{\prime}
.

Now take
L=d_{k},L_{1}={\rm diam}(\mathcal{A}_{k})\approx 16d_{k+1}\sim d_{k}^{\alpha}
and
L^{\prime}={\rm diam}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s})\approx\frac{1}{5}d_{k+1}
. Then
1\ll L\ll L^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2}L_{1}
. Moreover, we have

{\rm diam}(D_{k}^{s})\sim L,\ {\rm the\ number\ of\ }D_{k}^{s}\ {\rm in}\ \mathcal{A}_{k}\ {\rm is\ less\ than\ }N\ \Rightarrow{\rm denote}\ \mathfrak{S}=\cup_{s}D_{k}^{s},

{\rm diam}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s})\sim L^{\prime},\ \overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}\ {\rm contains\ a}\ \frac{L^{\prime}}{10}{\rm-neighborhood\ of\ }D_{k}^{s}\ \Rightarrow{\rm denote}\ \mathfrak{S}^{\prime}=\cup_{s}\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}.

Thus, we can construct a class of
L
-size blocks (denoted by
\mathfrak{F}
) to cover
\mathcal{A}_{k}\setminus\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
such that, every element of
\mathfrak{F}
is contained in
\mathcal{A}_{k}\setminus(\cup_{s}\Lambda_{k}(j_{s}))
. Moreover, we can ensure that for each
n\in\mathcal{A}_{k}\setminus\mathfrak{S}
, there is a
\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{F}
such that

Q_{\frac{L}{5}}(n)\subset\Lambda^{\prime}.

We denote
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k}=\cup_{\mathfrak{F}}\Lambda^{\prime}
.
Therefore, in
\mathcal{A}_{k}
, the geometric structure condition of Lemma
C.1
is satisfied. So applying Lemma
C.1
combined with (
5.11
) implies

(5.12)

|G_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}(x,y;\mathcal{E})|\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}|x-y|\}\ {\rm if}\ |x-y|\geq\frac{1}{10}d_{k+1}\geq\frac{1}{200}L_{1}.

Moreover, we also have

(5.13)

|G_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k}}(x,y;\mathcal{E})|\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}|x-y|\}\ {\rm if}\ |x-y|\geq L=d_{k}.

We additionally denote by
\widetilde{D}_{k}
the
d_{k}
-neighborhood of
\Lambda_{k}
, which is an intermediate block satisfying
\Lambda_{k}\subset\widetilde{D}_{k}\subset D_{k}
. Now for any
x\notin\widetilde{D}_{k_{\text{AL}}}
, by our construction, there will be a
k\geq k_{\text{AL}}
such that
x\in\widetilde{D}_{k+1}\setminus\widetilde{D}_{k}
. We distinguish the following cases:

If
{\rm dist}(x,\partial^{+}\Lambda_{k})\geq\frac{1}{10}d_{k+1}
, then we see
x\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
and
{\rm dist}(x,\mathcal{A}_{k})\geq\frac{1}{10}d_{k+1}\geq\frac{1}{200}{\rm diam}(\mathcal{A}_{k})\geq\frac{|x|}{200}
.
Applying the Poisson’s formula together with (
5.2
), (
5.12
) will lead to

(5.14)

\displaystyle|\psi(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{w\in\partial^{-}\mathcal{A}_{k}\atop w^{\prime}\sim w,w^{\prime}\in\partial^{+}\mathcal{A}_{k}}|G_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}(x,w;\mathcal{E})|\cdot|\psi(w^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d}(|m_{\mathcal{E}}|+1)^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\cdot(\#\partial^{-}\mathcal{A}_{k})\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}\cdot\frac{1}{20}d_{k+2}\}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,\omega,\mathcal{E}}\exp\{-\frac{1}{500}\gamma_{0}|x|\}.

If
{\rm dist}(x,\partial^{+}\Lambda_{k})\leq\frac{1}{10}d_{k+1}
, we see
x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
. In this case, since

{\rm dist}(x,\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s})\geq{\rm dist}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s},\Lambda_{k})-{\rm dist}(x,\Lambda_{k})\geq\frac{9}{5}d_{k+1}-\frac{1}{10}d_{k+1}\geq{\rm dist}(x,\Lambda_{k}),

we must have
{\rm dist}(x,\widetilde{A}_{k})={\rm dist}(x,\partial^{+}\Lambda_{k})
. Moreover, recalling
x\notin\widetilde{D}_{k}
, we have
|x|\geq 7d_{k}
and
{\rm dist}(x,\partial^{+}\Lambda_{k})\geq d_{k}
, which also leads to

{\rm dist}(x,\widetilde{A}_{k})={\rm dist}(x,\partial^{+}\Lambda_{k})\geq|x|-6d_{k}\geq\frac{1}{7}|x|.

Applying the Poisson’s formula together with (
5.2
) and (
5.13
) will lead to

(5.15)

\displaystyle|\psi(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{w\in\partial^{-}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\atop w^{\prime}\sim w,w^{\prime}\in\partial^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k}}|G_{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k}}(x,w;\mathcal{E})|\cdot|\psi(w^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d}(|m_{\mathcal{E}}|+1)^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\cdot(\#\partial^{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k})\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}\cdot{\rm dist}(x,\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{k})\}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d,\omega,\mathcal{E}}\exp\{-\frac{1}{14}\gamma_{0}|x|\}.

Combining (
5.14
) and (
5.15
), we have

(5.16)

|\psi(x)|\lesssim_{d,\omega,\mathcal{E}}\exp\{-\frac{1}{500}\gamma_{0}|x|\}\ {\rm for}\ \forall x\notin\widetilde{D}_{k_{\text{AL}}}.

Hence,
\psi
exhibits exponential decay, and consequently
\mathcal{E}
is an eigenvalue. This completes the proof of Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
.

∎

5.3.
Proof of the dynamical localization

Having established Anderson localization, we now proceed to prove the dynamical localization.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Since the Anderson localization (Theorem
1.2
, Theorem
1.3
) holds almost surely, we may assume (i.e., choose
\omega
such that Anderson localization happens)

{\rm Spec}(H)\cap[0,h)=\{E_{r}\}_{r\in\mathfrak{r}}

consists of eigenvalues of
H
in the spectral interval
[0,4d+\beta]
, indexed by a countable set
\mathfrak{r}
(counting multiplicities). Let
\varphi_{r}
denote the corresponding normalized eigenfunction of
E_{r}
. Then we have

P_{[0,h)}(H)\delta_{0}=\sum_{r}\varphi_{r}(0)\varphi_{r},

and therefore,

(5.17)

\displaystyle r^{2}(t,\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}\cdot\left|\sum_{r}e^{-itE_{r}}\varphi_{r}(0)\varphi_{r}(n)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}\left(\sum_{r}|\varphi_{r}(0)|\cdot|\varphi_{r}(n)|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{r}\sum_{t}|\varphi_{r}(0)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(0)|\cdot|n|^{2}\cdot|\varphi_{r}(n)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(n)|.

Now for
j\geq 1
, let

I_{j}=\left\{r:\ |\varphi_{r}(0)|\geq\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{j+1}\}\right\}.

Claim 5.3
.

If
r\in I_{j}
, then for each
k\geq j
, we must have

(5.18)

{\rm dist}(E_{r},{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}))\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}.

Proof.

Assume (
5.18
) fails. Then we have

(5.19)

\|G_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}(E_{r})\|\leq 2\exp\{d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}.

Let
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}
be the
d_{k+1}
-neighborhood of
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
, and recall that
\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}
is the
2d_{k}
-neighborhood of
\Lambda_{k}
. We have

\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}=(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}_{k})\cup\overline{\Lambda}_{k}.

Now set
L=d_{k},L^{\prime}={\rm diam}(\overline{\Lambda}_{k})\approx\frac{1}{5}d_{k+1},L_{1}={\rm diam}(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k})\approx\frac{11}{5}d_{k+1}\sim L^{\alpha}
. Then
1\ll L\ll L^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2}L_{1}
, and

{\rm diam}(\Lambda^{\prime}_{k})\sim L.

Moreover,
\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
contains the
\frac{L^{\prime}}{10}
-neighborhood of
\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}
. Since
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\Lambda_{k}
is non-resonant, we can construct a class of
L
-size blocks (denoted by
\mathfrak{F}
) to cover
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
such that, every element of
\mathfrak{F}
is contained in
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\Lambda_{k}
. Moreover, we can ensure that for each
n\in\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}\setminus\Lambda^{\prime}_{k}
, there is a
\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{F}
such that

Q_{\frac{L}{5}}(n)\subset\Lambda^{\prime}.

By above geometric structure and (
5.19
), we can apply Lemma
C.1
(with
\mathfrak{S}=\Lambda^{\prime}_{k},\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}=\overline{\Lambda}_{k}
) to conclude

(5.20)

|G_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}}(x,y;E_{r})|\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}|x-y|\}\ {\rm if}\ |x-y|\geq\frac{1}{100}d_{k+1}\geq\frac{1}{200}L_{1}.

Thus, using (
5.20
) together with the Poisson’s formula, we obtain

\displaystyle|\varphi_{r}(0)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{w\in\partial^{-}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}\atop w^{\prime}\sim w,w^{\prime}\in\partial^{+}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}}|G_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}}(0,w;E_{r})|\cdot|\varphi_{r}(w^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq 2d(\#\partial^{-}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k})\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}\cdot{\rm dist}(0,\partial^{-}\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k})\}

\displaystyle<\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{k+1}\}\leq\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{j+1}\},

which contradicts the condition
r\in I_{j}
.

∎

Based on Claim
5.3
, we can decompose (
5.17
) into (we set
I_{0}=\emptyset
)

(5.21)

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{r\in I_{i}\setminus I_{i-1}}\sum_{t\in I_{j}\setminus I_{j-1}}\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}|\varphi_{r}(n)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(n)|\right)|\varphi_{r}(0)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(0)|.

Recall that
K_{2}=K_{2}(\omega)
denotes the smallest scale so that Lemma
5.2
holds. Therefore, if
r\in I_{j}
, by Claim
5.3
,

\displaystyle{\rm dist}(E_{r},{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}}))

\displaystyle\geq{\rm dist}({\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}),{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}}))-{\rm dist}(E_{r},{\rm Spec}(H_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}}))

\displaystyle\geq\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}-\frac{1}{2}\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}\geq\frac{1}{2}\exp\{-d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}.

Thus, we have

(5.22)

\|G_{\overline{\Lambda}_{k}^{s}}(E_{r})\|\leq 2\exp\{d_{k+1}^{1-\varepsilon}\}\ {\rm for}\ \forall k\geq\max\{j,K_{2}\},\ \forall 2\leq s\leq N.

Using (
5.22
) together with the previous argument (that leads to (
5.16
)), we can prove that

(5.23)

|\varphi_{r}(x)|\lesssim_{d}\exp\{-\frac{1}{500}\gamma_{0}|x|\}\ {\rm for}\ \forall|x|>2\max\{d_{j+1},d_{K_{2}+1}\}.

(Here we note that, because
\varphi_{r}
has already been normalized, we may replace the bound (
5.2
) in Poisson’s formulas (
5.14
) and (
5.15
) by the uniform estimate
|\varphi_{r}(w^{\prime})|\leq 1
. Consequently, the constant appearing in (
5.23
) is independent of the index
r\in I_{j}
.) Thus, applying Hilbert-Schmidit argument yields

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{r}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus Q_{2\max\{d_{j+1},d_{K_{2}+1}\}}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{|x|\geq 2\max\{d_{j+1},d_{K_{2}+1}\}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{250}\gamma_{0}|x|\right\}\leq\frac{1}{2},

and

\displaystyle\#Q_{2\max\{d_{j+1},d_{K_{2}+1}\}}(0)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{|x|\leq Q_{2\max\{d_{j+1},d_{K_{2}+1}\}}(0)}\sum_{r}|\varphi_{r}(n)|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{r\in I_{j}}(1-\frac{1}{2})\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\#I_{j}.

This means

(5.24)

\#I_{j}\lesssim\left(d_{\max\{j,K_{2}\}+1}\right)^{d}\ {\rm for}\ \forall j\geq 1.

Based on (
5.24
), we further decompose (
5.21
) into

\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq K_{2}}\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{2}}+\sum_{i>K_{2}}\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{2}}+\sum_{1\leq i\leq K_{2}}\sum_{j>K_{2}}+\sum_{i>K_{2}}\sum_{j>K_{2}}\right)\sum_{r\in I_{i}\setminus I_{i-1}}\sum_{t\in I_{j}\setminus I_{j-1}}\cdots.

We discuss:

•

For the summation over
{1\leq i\leq K_{2}},{1\leq j\leq K_{2}}
, we can estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i\leq K_{2}}\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{2}}\sum_{r\in I_{i}\setminus I_{i-1}}\sum_{t\in I_{j}\setminus I_{j-1}}\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}|\varphi_{r}(n)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(n)|\right)|\varphi_{r}(0)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(0)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{r\in I_{K_{2}}}\sum_{t\in I_{K_{2}}}\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}|\varphi_{r}(n)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(n)|\right)|\varphi_{r}(0)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(0)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\#I_{K_{2}})^{2}\cdot\sup_{r,t\in I_{K_{2}}}\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}|\varphi_{r}(n)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(n)|\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\eqref{exp decay 4}}}{\lesssim_{d}}

\displaystyle(d_{K_{2}+1})^{2d}\left(\sum_{|n|\leq 2d_{K_{2}+1}}|n|^{2}+\sum_{|n|>2d_{K_{2}+1}}|n|^{2}\exp\{-\frac{1}{250}\gamma_{0}|n|\}\right)<\infty.

•

For the summation over
{i>K_{2}},{1\leq j\leq K_{2}}
and
{1\leq i\leq K_{2}},{j>K_{2}}
, we can estimate, for example,

\displaystyle\sum_{i>K_{2}}\sum_{1\leq j\leq K_{2}}\sum_{r\in I_{i}\setminus I_{i-1}}\sum_{t\in I_{j}\setminus I_{j-1}}\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}|\varphi_{r}(n)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(n)|\right)|\varphi_{r}(0)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(0)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{t\in I_{K_{2}}}\sum_{i>K_{2}}\sum_{r\in I_{i}\setminus I_{i-1}}\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}|\varphi_{r}(n)|\right)|\varphi_{r}(0)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\#I_{K_{2}})\cdot\sum_{i>K_{2}}\#I_{i}\cdot\left(\sup_{r\in I_{i}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}|\varphi_{r}(n)|\right)\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{i}\}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{\eqref{exp decay 4}}}{\lesssim_{d}}

\displaystyle(d_{K_{2}+1})^{d}\sum_{i>K_{2}}(d_{i+1})^{d}\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{i}\}\cdot\left(\sum_{|n|\leq 2d_{i+1}}|n|^{2}+\sum_{|n|>2d_{i+1}}|n|^{2}\exp\{-\frac{1}{500}\gamma_{0}|n|\}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{d}

\displaystyle(d_{K_{2}+1})^{d}\sum_{i>K_{2}}(d_{i+1})^{2d+2}\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{i}\}<\infty.

•

For the summation over
{i>K_{2}},{j>K_{2}}
, we can estimate

\displaystyle\ \ \ \sum_{i>K_{2}}\sum_{j>K_{2}}\sum_{r\in I_{i}\setminus I_{i-1}}\sum_{t\in I_{j}\setminus I_{j-1}}\left(\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}|\varphi_{r}(n)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(n)|\right)|\varphi_{r}(0)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(0)|

(5.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i>K_{2}}\sum_{j>K_{2}}\left(\sum_{r\in I_{i}\setminus I_{i-1}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}|\varphi_{r}(n)|\cdot|\varphi_{r}(0)|\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{t\in I_{j}\setminus I_{j-1}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}|\varphi_{t}(n)|\cdot|\varphi_{t}(0)|\right).

By the same argument used in the second case, we have

\left(\sum_{r\in I_{i}\setminus I_{i-1}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|n|^{2}|\varphi_{r}(n)|\cdot|\varphi_{r}(0)|\right)\lesssim(d_{i+1})^{2d+2}\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{i}\}.

Hence,

\text{\eqref{third case term, dynamical}}\lesssim_{d}\sum_{i>K_{2}}\sum_{j>K_{2}}(d_{i+1})^{2d+2}\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{i}\}\cdot(d_{j+1})^{2d+2}\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}d_{j}\}<\infty.

Summarizing all the estimates above, we obtain

r^{2}(t,\omega)<\infty

for almost every
\omega
, which completes the proof of Theorem
1.4
.

∎

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
2.6

In this section, we present the proof of Theorem
2.6
. The core idea of the proof bears strong similarity to that of the higher-dimensional Wegner estimate in Section
4
: we leverage the cone property to construct a one-dimensional subset that exhibits transversality, and then use the arguments developed in Section
4.5
to build a martingale on this subset. We emphasize that the martingale structure employed here is strikingly analogous to the one introduced in Section
4.5
. In particular, during the construction of this martingale, the sites we select form a martingale themselves, one that depends on the preceding randomness—a feature we refer to as the
”site-mixed” property
.

Proof of Theorem
2.6
.

To get better insight, we first consider the two-dimensional case.

Definition A.1
.

For a fixed length
L\gg 1
,
the elementary propagating region
of length
L
in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is defined by

\mathcal{I}_{L}:=\left\{(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:\ n_{2}\geq 0,\ n_{1}+n_{2}\leq L+1,\ n_{2}-n_{1}\leq L+1\right\}.

Remark A.1
.

The region
\mathcal{I}_{L}
can be interpreted as follows. Given initial data
u_{0}
on
(\mathcal{P}_{0}\cup\mathcal{P}_{1})\cap\mathcal{I}_{L}
, the solution
u
of the Dirichlet problem

\begin{cases}\Delta u=Wu,\\
u\equiv u_{0}\ \text{on}\ (\mathcal{P}_{0}\cup\mathcal{P}_{1})\cap\mathcal{I}_{L},\end{cases}

is uniquely determined precisely on
\mathcal{I}_{L}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{I}_{L}
is the maximal region on which the values of
u
can be determined.

Now we consider the tilted line

\mathcal{T}_{k}:=\{(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:\ n_{2}-n_{1}=k\}.

Since the equation
H(\omega)u-\lambda u=0
locally reads as

(A.1)

\displaystyle u(n_{1},n_{2})

\displaystyle=-u(n_{1}-1,n_{2}-1)

(A.2)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +(2d+V(n_{1},n_{2}-1)-\lambda)\cdot u(n_{1},n_{2}-1)

(A.3)

\displaystyle\ \ \ -u(n_{1}+1,n_{2}-1)-u(n_{1},n_{2}-2).

Clearly, (
A.1
) is about the information of
u
on
\mathcal{T}_{n_{2}-n_{1}}
, (
A.2
) on
\mathcal{T}_{n_{2}-n_{1}-1}
and (
A.3
) on
\mathcal{T}_{n_{2}-n_{1}-2}
. When
n_{2}\gg 1
, we can iterate the relationships along
\mathcal{T}_{n_{2}-n_{1}}
to obtain

\displaystyle u(n_{1},n_{2})

\displaystyle=-\big(-u(n_{1}-2,n_{2}-2)+(2d+V(n_{1}-1,n_{2}-2)-\lambda)\cdot u(n_{1}-1,n_{2}-2)-u(n_{1},n_{2}-2)-u(n_{1}-1,n_{2}-3)\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt+(2d+V(n_{1},n_{2}-1)-\lambda)\cdot u(n_{1},n_{2}-1)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt-u(n_{1}+1,n_{2}-1)-u(n_{1},n_{2}-2)

\displaystyle=u(n_{1}-2,n_{2}-2)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt+(2d+V(n_{1},n_{2}-1)-\lambda)\cdot u(n_{1},n_{2}-1)-(2d+V(n_{1}-1,n_{2}-2)-\lambda)\cdot u(n_{1}-1,n_{2}-2)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt-u(n_{1}+1,n_{2}-1)+u(n_{1}-1,n_{2}-3)

\displaystyle=\cdots

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{T}_{n_{2}-n_{1}-1}}a_{x}\cdot(2d+V(x)-\lambda)\cdot u(x)+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{T}_{n_{2}-n_{1}-2}}b_{y}\cdot u(y)+{\rm Remains}(u_{0}).

Here, we have
|a_{x}|=1
,
a_{x},b_{y}
are coefficients independent of the randomness, and
{\rm Remains}(u_{0})
is a term only involving the initial data
u_{0}
(see Figure
6
).

\cdots
\mathcal{P}_{0}\cup\mathcal{P}_{1}
iterate
u_{0}
\mathcal{T}_{n_{2}-n_{1}}
\mathcal{T}_{n_{2}-n_{1}-1}
\mathcal{T}_{n_{2}-n_{1}-2}
u(n_{1},n_{2})
u(n_{1}-1,n_{2}-1)
u(n_{1}+1,n_{2}-1)
u(n_{1},n_{2}-1)
involves potential
V
\cdots

Figure 6.
The iteration of the solution till the initial data.

This fact also has an analogue for the tilted line

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{k}:=\{(n_{1},n_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:\ n_{1}+n_{2}=k\}.

Based on the above discussions, we can construct the martingale in
\mathcal{I}_{L}
as follows.

Choose the site
X_{0}=0\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and take the
\sigma
-algebra with

\mathcal{B}_{0}=\{\Omega,\emptyset\}\subset\mathcal{F}_{0}=\sigma(\{0\}).

Now, we already know the initial data

u\equiv u_{0}\ {\rm on}\ \mathcal{P}_{0}\cup\mathcal{P}_{1}.

(Step 1)

By the cone property (i.e., Proposition
2.1
) and (
2.6
), there must be a site

X_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}(0,\mathbf{e}_{2})=\{(0,1),(1,1),(-1,1),(0,2)\}

such that
|u(X_{1})|\geq\gamma|u(0)|
. Since we already know the value of
u=u_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, we can judge that, if
X_{1}
belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, we choose
X_{1}
to be the site with the smallest first coordinate; else if

|u_{0}(x)|<\gamma|u(0)|\ {\rm for}\ \forall x\in\mathcal{P}_{1},

then
X_{1}
is chosen to be
(0,2)
. Thus, by our construction,
X_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable (deterministic).

(Step 2)

Define the
\sigma
-algebra

(A.4)

\mathcal{B}_{1}=\sigma\left(\mathfrak{B}_{1}\right),\ \mathfrak{B}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap\{0\leq n_{2}\leq(X_{1})_{2}\}\setminus\{X_{1}\}.

Now if the randomness in
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
is fixed, then the value of
u
in
(\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap\{0\leq n_{2}\leq(X_{1})_{2}+1\})\setminus\{X_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}\}
will be determined. See Figure
7
. In particular, the value
|u(X_{1})|
will be determined, which means
|u(X_{1})|
is
\mathcal{B}_{1}
-measurable.

Using again the cone property, there must be a site

X_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}(X_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2})=\{A_{1},B_{1},C_{1},D_{1}\}

with

A_{1}=X_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}-\mathbf{e}_{1},B_{1}=X_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}+\mathbf{e}_{1},C_{1}=X_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2},D_{1}=X_{1}+2\mathbf{e}_{2},

such that
|u(X_{2})|\geq\gamma|u(X_{1})|
. We can judge that, if
X_{2}
can be
A_{1}
or
B_{1}
, we choose the one with smallest first coordinate; else if

|u(A_{1})|<\gamma|u(X_{1})|,|u(B_{1})|<\gamma|u(X_{1})|,

then we must have
X_{2}\in\{C_{1},D_{1}\}
. (This means, although conditioning on
\mathcal{B}_{1}
cannot determine
X_{2}
, but can determine the relevant place where
X_{2}
may occur.)

By the preceding discussion, we can define the tilted line as follows. If
X_{2}=A_{1}
or
X_{2}\in\{C_{1},D_{1}\}
, we take

\mathfrak{L}_{1}=\{n_{2}-n_{1}=(X_{1})_{2}-(X_{1})_{1}\}=\mathcal{T}_{(X_{1})_{2}-(X_{1})_{1}},

which contains
X_{1}
, but does not pass through
X_{2}
; else if
X_{2}=B_{1}
, we take

\mathfrak{L}_{1}=\{n_{2}+n_{1}=(X_{1})_{1}+(X_{1})_{2}\}=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{(X_{1})_{1}+(X_{1})_{2}},

which also contains
X_{1}
but does not pass through
X_{2}
. See Figure
7
.

\mathcal{I}_{L}
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
X_{1}
A_{1}
D_{1}
B_{1}
\mathcal{T}_{(X_{1})_{2}-(X_{1})_{1}}
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{(X_{1})_{1}+(X_{1})_{2}}
\cdots
\cdots

Figure 7.
Construction of
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
.

Therefore, the tilted line
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
is determined, and it uniquely determines the site

\mathfrak{e}_{1}\in(\mathfrak{L}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2})\cap\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L},

where

\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L}:=\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap(\mathcal{T}_{L+1}\cup\mathcal{T}_{L}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{L+1}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{L})

is the side boundary of
\mathcal{I}_{L}
.
Thus both
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{1}
are
\mathcal{B}_{1}
-measurable.

Next, we denote the region under the tilted line
\mathcal{T}_{k}
(resp.
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{k}
) by

\mathcal{T}_{\leq k}=\{n_{2}-n_{1}\leq k\},\ \mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\leq k}=\{n_{1}+n_{2}\leq k\}.

With this notation, we denote the region under
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
by
\mathfrak{R}_{1}
, i.e.,

\mathfrak{R}_{1}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{T}_{\leq(X_{1})_{2}-(X_{1})_{1}},\ {\rm if}\ \mathfrak{L}_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{(X_{1})_{2}-(X_{1})_{1}},\\
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\leq(X_{1})_{1}+(X_{1})_{2}},\ {\rm if}\ \mathfrak{L}_{1}=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{(X_{1})_{1}+(X_{1})_{2}}.\end{cases}

We construct the next filtration element by

(A.5)

\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma(\mathfrak{F}_{1}),\ \mathfrak{F}_{1}:=(\mathfrak{R}_{1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{L})\cup\mathfrak{B}_{1}.

Our construction ensures that

(A.6)

\mathfrak{B}_{1}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{1},\ X_{1}\notin\mathfrak{B}_{1},\ X_{1}\in\mathfrak{F}_{1},\ X_{2}\notin\mathfrak{F}_{1}.

Therefore,
\mathcal{B}_{1}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1}
.

If we further condition on
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, clearly the value of
u
in the region

\{x\in\mathcal{I}_{L}:\ x\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}\ {\rm or}\ x-\mathbf{e}_{2}\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}\}

will be determined, which contains the sites
C_{1}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{1}
. Hence, we can do further discussions under the case
X_{2}\in\{C_{1},D_{1}\}
: If
|u(C_{1})|\geq\gamma|u(X_{1})|
, then we let
X_{2}=C_{1}
; else, we let
X_{2}=D_{1}
. Thus the site
X_{2}
is determined.

Moreover, the value
u(\mathfrak{e}_{1})
is now determined. By our previous discussion, we can express it in the following form

(A.7)

u(\mathfrak{e}_{1})=\sum_{x\in\mathfrak{L}_{1}}a_{x}\cdot(2d+V(x)-\lambda)\cdot u(x)+\sum_{y\in\mathfrak{L}_{1}-e_{2}}b_{y}\cdot u(y)+{\rm Remains}(u_{0}).

Observing that the value of
u
in the right hand side of (
A.7
) will be determined if we condition on the randomness in
\mathfrak{F}_{1}\setminus\{X_{1}\}
, which means

u(\mathfrak{e}_{1})=a_{X_{1}}\cdot V(X_{1})u(X_{1})+{\rm a\ deterministic\ value},\ |a_{X_{1}}|=1.

Since
V(X_{1})=V_{\text{hi}}(X_{1})+\beta\omega(X_{1})
and we have transversality estimate at
X_{1}

|u(X_{1})|\geq\gamma|u(X_{0})|=\gamma|u(0)|,

we can conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|u(\mathfrak{e}_{1})|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{1})|\ \bigg|\ \mathcal{B}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\omega(X_{1})}\left(|u(\mathfrak{e}_{1})|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{1})|\ \bigg|\ \sigma(\mathfrak{F}_{1}\setminus\{X_{1}\})\right)\bigg|\mathcal{B}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Therefore, we define

(A.8)

\mathcal{Z}_{1}=\mathbf{1}_{\{|u(\mathfrak{e}_{1})|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{1})|\}}(\omega(X_{1})).

Then
X_{2},\mathcal{Z}_{1}
are
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-measurable, and

\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}|\mathcal{F}_{0})\geq\frac{1}{2}.

(Step 3)

Analogously, we take

(A.9)

\mathcal{B}_{2}=\sigma\left(\mathfrak{B}_{2}\right),\ \mathfrak{B}_{2}=(\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap\{0\leq n_{2}\leq(X_{2})_{2}\}\setminus\{X_{2}\})\cup\mathfrak{F}_{1}.

If the randomness in
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
is fixed, then the value of
u
in
(\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap\{0\leq n_{2}\leq(X_{2})_{2}+1\})\setminus\{X_{2}+e_{2}\}
will be determined. In particular, the value
|u(X_{2})|
will be determined, which means
|u(X_{2})|
is
\mathcal{B}_{2}
-measurable.

By similar argument in
Step 2
, we can determine the relevant place of
X_{3}
, and then construct the tilted line
\mathfrak{L}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{2}
. Such construction satisfies

X_{2}\in\mathfrak{L}_{2},\ X_{3}\notin{\mathfrak{L}_{2}},\ \mathfrak{e}_{k}\in(\mathfrak{L}_{2}+\mathbf{e}_{2})\cap\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L}.

We also have
\mathfrak{L}_{2},\mathfrak{e}_{2}
are
\mathcal{B}_{2}
-measurable.

Next, with
\mathfrak{L}_{2}
determined, the
\sigma
-algebra

(A.10)

\mathcal{F}_{2}=\sigma(\mathfrak{F}_{2}),\ \mathfrak{F}_{2}:=(\mathfrak{R}_{2}\cap\mathcal{I}_{L})\cup\mathfrak{B}_{2}

will be obtained, and we have

(A.11)

\mathfrak{B}_{2}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{2},\ X_{2}\notin\mathfrak{B}_{2},\ X_{2}\in\mathfrak{F}_{2},\ X_{3}\notin\mathfrak{F}_{2}.

Therefore,
\mathcal{B}_{2}\subset\mathcal{F}_{2}
.

If we further condition on
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, using similar discussions as in
Step 2
concludes that
X_{3}
will be determined, and that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|u(\mathfrak{e}_{2})|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{2})|\ \bigg|\ \mathcal{B}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\omega(X_{2})}\left(|u(\mathfrak{e}_{2})|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{2})|\ \bigg|\ \sigma(\mathfrak{F}_{2}\setminus\{X_{2}\})\right)\bigg|\mathcal{B}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Therefore, we define

(A.12)

\mathcal{Z}_{2}=\mathbf{1}_{\{|u(\mathfrak{e}_{2})|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{2})|\}}(\omega(X_{2})).

Hence
X_{3},\mathcal{Z}_{2}
are
\mathcal{F}_{2}
-measurable, and

\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}|\mathcal{Z}_{1})=\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Z}_{2}|\mathcal{F}_{1})\geq\frac{1}{2}.

(Step 4)
\cdots

By iterating the above constructions, we obtain the following filtration

\mathcal{B}_{0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{0}\subset\mathcal{B}_{1}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1}\subset\mathcal{B}_{2}\subset\mathcal{F}_{2}\cdots\subset\mathcal{B}_{T}\subset\mathcal{F}_{T},

together with a (site-mixed) martingale
(|u(X_{j})|,\mathfrak{L}_{j},\mathfrak{e}_{j},X_{j+1},\mathcal{Z}_{j}),1\leq j\leq T
satisfying:

•

Denote the region under
\mathfrak{L}_{j}
by
\mathfrak{R}_{j}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{j}=\sigma\left(\mathfrak{B}_{j}\right),

\displaystyle\ \mathfrak{B}_{j}=(\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap\{0\leq n_{2}\leq(X_{j})_{2}\}\setminus\{X_{j}\})\cup\mathfrak{F}_{j-1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{j}=\sigma(\mathfrak{F}_{j}),

\displaystyle\ \mathfrak{F}_{j}=(\mathfrak{R}_{j}\cap\mathcal{I}_{L})\cup\mathfrak{B}_{j}.

•

We have
X_{j}\notin\mathfrak{B}_{j},X_{j}\in\mathfrak{L}_{j}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{j},X_{j+1}\notin\mathfrak{F}_{j}
. Moreover,
X_{j+1}\in\mathfrak{C}(X_{j},\mathbf{e}_{2})
has transversality:

|u(X_{j+1})|\geq\gamma|u(X_{j})|\geq\gamma^{j+1}|u(0)|.

We also have
\mathfrak{e}_{j}\in\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap(\mathfrak{L}_{j}+\mathbf{e}_{2})
, and
\mathfrak{e}_{j},1\leq j\leq T
are distinct.

•

(|u(X_{j})|,\mathfrak{L}_{j},\mathfrak{e}_{j})
are
\mathcal{B}_{j}
-measurable, and
(X_{j+1},\mathcal{Z}_{j})
are
\mathcal{F}_{j}
-measurable. Moreover,

\mathcal{Z}_{j}=\mathbf{1}_{\{|u(\mathfrak{e}_{j})|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{j})|\}}(\omega(X_{j})),\ \mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Z}_{j+1}|\mathcal{Z}_{j})\geq\frac{1}{2}.

For a visual illustration, see Figure
8
.

\mathcal{I}_{L}
\cdots
\cdots
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
X_{0}
\mathfrak{B}_{2}
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
\mathfrak{L}_{2}
\mathfrak{e}_{1}
\mathfrak{e}_{2}
\mathfrak{F}_{1}
X_{1}
X_{3}
X_{2}

Figure 8.
Construction of the martingale
(|u(X_{j})|,\mathfrak{L}_{j},\mathfrak{e}_{j},X_{j+1},\mathcal{Z}_{j})
.

We set the length of the martingale to be
T=\frac{1}{10}L
. As in Section
4.5
, we define

(A.13)

\mathcal{Y}_{j}=\mathcal{X}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}j=\mathcal{Z}_{1}+\mathcal{Z}_{2}+\cdots+\mathcal{Z}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}j,\ 1\leq j\leq T.

Then
(\mathcal{Y}_{j})_{1\leq j\leq T}
becomes a submartingale. Applying Azuma’s inequality yields

(A.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{X}_{T}\leq\frac{1}{10}T\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{Y}_{T}\leq-(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{10})T\right)\leq\exp\{-T\}=\exp\{-\frac{1}{10}L\}.

Clearly, both the probabilistic estimate (
A.14
) and the previous discussion depend on the value of
\lambda
and the initial data
u_{0}
. Furthermore, if the event
\mathcal{X}_{T}>\frac{1}{10}T
happens, then there are more than
\frac{1}{10}T=\frac{1}{100}L
many
j
’s such that

|u(\mathfrak{e}_{j})|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{j})|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta\gamma^{\frac{1}{10}L}|u(0)|.

That is to say, if we define

\Omega(\lambda,u_{0},\mathcal{I}_{L})=\ \#\left(\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap\{x:\ |u(x)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta\gamma^{\frac{1}{10}L}|u(0)|\}\right)\ {\rm is\ larger\ than\ }\frac{L}{100},

then

(A.15)

\mathbb{P}\left(\Omega(\lambda,u_{0},\mathcal{I}_{L})\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{X}_{T}>\frac{1}{10}T\right)\geq 1-\exp\{-\frac{L}{10}\}.

The estimate (
A.15
) is only about the randomness in
\mathcal{I}_{L}
.

Note that (
A.15
) only provides a lower bound on the number of sites along the side boundary on which e
u
is bounded from below. Define

L_{s}=\frac{L}{10}+10s,\ 0\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100}.

Then the side boundaries
\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L_{s}},0\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100}
are mutually disjoint, and are all contained in
Q_{L}(0)
. By (
A.15
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{1\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100}}\Omega(\lambda,u_{0},\mathcal{I}_{L_{s}})\right)\geq 1-\sum_{0\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100}}\exp\{-\frac{L_{s}}{10}\}\geq 1-\exp\{-\frac{L}{101}\},

which means that for each
0\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100}
and in
\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L_{s}},
there will be more than
\frac{L_{s}}{100}
many sites such that

(A.16)

|u(x)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta\gamma^{\frac{1}{10}L_{s}}|u(0)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta\gamma^{\frac{1}{50}L}|u(0)|\geq\exp\{{-\frac{1}{25}|\log\gamma|\cdot L}\}|u(0)|

supposing
L\gg 1
l (depending only on
h,\beta
). Therefore in
Q_{L}(0)
, the total number of the sites satisfying (
A.16
) is bigger than

\sum_{0\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100}}\frac{L_{s}}{100}\geq 10^{-5}L^{2}.

This implies

(A.17)

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{uc}}(L,u_{0},\lambda)\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{1\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100}}\Omega(\lambda,u_{0},\mathcal{I}_{L_{s}})\right)\geq 1-\exp\{-\frac{L}{1001}\}.

We have proven Theorem
2.6
on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

The argument presented above can be extended to all dimensions
d\geq 3
.

Recall that we denote the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
by
\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{e}_{d}\}
, and a site
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
can be represented as

n=(n_{1},\cdots,n_{d})=n_{1}\mathbf{e}_{1}+n_{2}\mathbf{e}_{2}+\cdots+n_{d}\mathbf{e}_{d}.

We give an order for element in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
via:

(A.18)

n<n^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow n_{i_{0}}<n_{i_{0}}^{\prime}\ {\rm with}\ i_{0}:=\min\{i:\ n_{i}\neq n_{i}^{\prime}\}.

Definition A.2
.

For a fixed length
L\gg 1
,
the elementary propagating region
of length
L
in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is defined by

\mathcal{I}_{L}:=\left\{(n_{1},\cdots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ n_{d}\geq 0\ {\rm and}\ \sum_{1\leq i\leq d-1}|n_{i}|+n_{d}\leq L+1\right\}.

We define the following tilted hyperplanes in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
:

\mathcal{T}_{k}=\{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \langle n,\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle=k\},\ \mathbf{v}_{-}=\mathbf{e}_{d}-(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{d-1}),

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{k}=\{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ \langle n,\mathbf{v}_{+}\rangle=k\},\ \mathbf{v}_{+}=\mathbf{e}_{d}+(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{d-1}).

Now for a site
n\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
with
n_{d}\gg 1
, we may iterate the local form of
H(\omega)u-\lambda u=0
along the hyperplane
\mathcal{T}_{k}
as follows.

\displaystyle u(n)

\displaystyle=-\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq d-1}u(n-\mathbf{e}_{d}-\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)+(2d+V(n-\mathbf{e}_{d})-\lambda)\cdot u(n-\mathbf{e}_{d})-\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq d-1}u(n-\mathbf{e}_{d}+\mathbf{e}_{i})+u(n-2\mathbf{e}_{d})\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{2}\cdot\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq d-1}\sum_{1\leq j\leq d-1}u(n-2\mathbf{e}_{d}-\mathbf{e}_{i}-\mathbf{e}_{j})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt+(2d+V(n-\mathbf{e}_{d})-\lambda)\cdot u(n-\mathbf{e}_{d})+(-1)\cdot\left(\sum_{1\leq i\leq d-1}(2d+V(n-2\mathbf{e}_{d}-\mathbf{e}_{i})-\lambda)\cdot u(n-2\mathbf{e}_{d}-\mathbf{e}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{T}_{k-2}}b_{y}^{(2)}\cdot u(y)

\displaystyle=\cdots

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\cdot\left(\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{k})\atop 1\leq i_{s}\leq d-1,\forall 1\leq s\leq k}u(n-k\mathbf{e}_{d}-\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}-\mathbf{e}_{i_{2}}-\cdots-\mathbf{e}_{i_{k}})\right)

(A.19)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt+\sum_{0\leq s\leq k-1}(-1)^{s}\cdot\left(\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{s})\atop 1\leq i_{r}\leq d-1,\forall 1\leq r\leq s}(2d+V(n-(s+1)\mathbf{e}_{d}-\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}-\cdots-\mathbf{e}_{i_{s}})-\lambda)\cdot u(n-(s+1)\mathbf{e}_{d}-\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}-\cdots-\mathbf{e}_{i_{s}})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.50012pt+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{T}_{k-2}}b_{y}^{(k)}\cdot u(y)

\displaystyle=\cdots

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{T}_{k-1}}a_{x}\cdot(2d+V(x)-\lambda)\cdot u(x)+\sum_{y\in\mathcal{T}_{k-2}}b_{y}\cdot u(y)+{\rm Remains}(u_{0}).

From the above formula, it’s easy to see that the coefficients involving information on
\mathcal{T}_{k-1}
will satisfy

|a_{x}|{\rm\ is\ a\ binomial\ coefficient}\ \Rightarrow\ |a_{x}|\geq 1.

Similar discussion also works for
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{k}
.

Now, choose the site
X_{0}=0\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and take the
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{B}_{0}=\{\Omega,\emptyset\}\subset\mathcal{F}_{0}=\sigma(\{0\}),\mathfrak{F}_{0}=\{0\}.

(Step 1)

By the cone property Proposition
2.1
and (
2.6
), there must be a site

X_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}(0,\mathbf{e}_{2})=\{\mathbf{e}_{d}\pm\mathbf{e}_{i}:\ 1\leq i\leq d-1\}\cup\{2\mathbf{e}_{d}\}

such that
|u(X_{1})|\geq\gamma|u(0)|
. Since we already know the value of
u=u_{0}
in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, we can judge that, if
X_{1}
can be in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, we choose
X_{1}
to be the smallest site under the order (
A.18
); else,
X_{1}
can only be chosen to be
2\mathbf{e}_{2}
. Thus, by our construction,
X_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable (deterministic).

(Step 2)

Define the
\sigma
-algebra

(A.20)

\mathcal{B}_{1}=\sigma\left(\mathfrak{B}_{1}\right),\ \mathfrak{B}_{1}=\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap\{0\leq n_{2}\leq(X_{1})_{d}\}\setminus\{X_{1}\}.

Now if the randomness in
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
is fixed, then the value of
u
in
(\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap\{0\leq n_{2}\leq(X_{1})_{d}+1\})\setminus\{X_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{d}\}
will be determined. In particular, the value
|u(X_{1})|
will be determined, which means
|u(X_{1})|
is
\mathcal{B}_{1}
-measurable. Moreover, if we decompose

\mathfrak{C}(X_{1},\mathbf{e}_{d})=A_{1}\cup B_{1}\cup\{C_{1},D_{1}\}

with

A_{1}=\{X_{2}+\mathbf{e}_{d}-\mathbf{e}_{i}:\ 1\leq i\leq d-1\},\ B_{1}=\{X_{2}+\mathbf{e}_{d}+\mathbf{e}_{i}:\ 1\leq i\leq d-1\},\ C_{1}=X_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{d},D_{1}=X_{1}+2\mathbf{e}_{d},

then the value of
u
in
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
will also be determined.

By the cone property, there must be a site
X_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}(X_{1},\mathbf{e}_{2})
such that
|u(X_{2})|\geq\gamma|u(X_{1})|
. We can judge that, if
X_{2}
can lie in
A_{1}\cup B_{1}
, we choose the smallest one under the order (
A.18
); else,
we must have
X_{2}\in\{C_{1},D_{1}\}
.

Next, we define the tilted hyperplane as follows. If
X_{2}\in A_{1}
or
X_{2}\in\{C_{1},D_{1}\}
, we take
\mathfrak{L}_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle}
. In this case, we have

X_{2}-X_{1}\in\{\mathbf{e}_{d}-\mathbf{e}_{i}:\ 1\leq i\leq d-1\}\cup\{\mathbf{e}_{d},2\mathbf{e}_{d}\}\Rightarrow\langle X_{2},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle>\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle.

Moreover, if we consider the point
y\in\mathfrak{C}(X_{2},\mathbf{e}_{d})
, we have

y-X_{2}\in\{\mathbf{e}_{d}\pm\mathbf{e}_{i}:\ 1\leq i\leq d-1\}\cup\{\mathbf{e}_{d},2\mathbf{e}_{d}\}\Rightarrow\langle y,\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle\geq\langle X_{2},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle.

Therefore,
X_{1}
lies in
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
, but
X_{2}
and the cone
\mathfrak{C}(X_{2},\mathbf{e}_{d})
lie in the region strictly above
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
(this ensures that the subsequent
X_{3}
will also lie strictly above
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
). Else, if
X_{2}\in B_{1}
, we take
\mathfrak{L}_{1}=\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{+}\rangle}
.
Then, similarly,
X_{1}
lies in
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
, but
X_{2}
and the cone
\mathfrak{C}(X_{2},\mathbf{e}_{d})
lie in the region strictly above
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
.

After determining
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
, we consider the set

\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{1}=(\mathfrak{L}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{d})\cap\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L},

where

\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L}:=\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap(\mathcal{T}_{L+1}\cup\mathcal{T}_{L}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{L+1}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{L})

is a part of the side boundary of
\mathcal{I}_{L}
. We construct
\mathfrak{e}_{1}\subset\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{1}
as follows: For a
n\in\mathfrak{e}_{1}
, we can expand
u(n)
(by the previous discussion) into

u(n)=\sum_{x\in\mathfrak{L}_{1}}a_{x}\cdot(2d+V(x)-\lambda)\cdot u(x)+\sum_{y\in\mathfrak{L}_{1}-\mathbf{e}_{d}}b_{y}\cdot u(y)+{\rm Remains}(u_{0}).

If the above formula involves the potential at
X_{1}
, i.e.,
V(X_{1})
, we let
n\in\mathfrak{e}_{1}
.
Thus both
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{e}_{1}
are
\mathcal{B}_{1}
-measurable.

Since both
(\mathfrak{L}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{d})
and
\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L}
are (union of)
(d-1)
-dimensional hyperplanes, intuitively we can expect
\mathfrak{e}_{1}
is a
(d-2)
-dimensional set. This can be strictly proven by the following combinatorial geometry argument.

Claim A.1
.

We have

\#\mathfrak{e}_{1}\gtrsim_{d}L^{d-2}.

Proof of Claim
A.1
.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\mathfrak{L}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{d}=\mathcal{T}_{\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle+1}
. Clearly, if
n\in\mathfrak{L}_{1}
, then the closest sites near
n
in
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
are

n\pm(\mathbf{e}_{d}+\mathbf{e}_{i}),\ 1\leq i\leq d-1.

Moreover, we have

\langle n\pm(\mathbf{e}_{d}+\mathbf{e}_{i}),\mathbf{v}_{+}\rangle=\langle n,\mathbf{v}_{+}\rangle\pm 2.

This will ensure that
(\mathfrak{L}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{d})\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{L}\neq\emptyset
or
(\mathfrak{L}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{d})\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{L+1}\neq\emptyset
, and thus
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{1}\neq\emptyset
. Without loss of generality, we assume
(\mathfrak{L}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{d})\cap\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{L+1}\neq\emptyset
. We solve the equation

\begin{cases}\langle n,\mathbf{v}_{+}\rangle=L+1,\\
\langle n,\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle=\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle+1,\end{cases}

which gives

(A.21)

\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{1}=\left\{n\in\mathcal{I}_{L}:\ n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d-1}=\frac{1}{2}(L-\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle)\ {\rm and}\ n_{d}=\frac{1}{2}(L+\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle+2)\right\}.

Now for any
y\in\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{1}
, the term (
A.19
) in our previous discussion about the expansion of
u(y)
tells us that, the sites, which lie in
\mathcal{P}_{(X_{1})_{d}}
and are involved in the expansion, are of form

x_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{s})}(y)\triangleq y-(k+1)\mathbf{e}_{k}-\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}-\mathbf{e}_{i_{2}}\cdots-\mathbf{e}_{i_{k}}\in\mathcal{P}_{(X_{1})_{d}},\ 1\leq i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\leq d-1.

This forces

y_{d}-(k+1)=\frac{1}{2}(L+\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle+2)-k-1=(X_{1})_{d}\Rightarrow k=\frac{1}{2}(L+\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle)-(X_{1})_{d}.

Therefore,

(A.22)

\langle x_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{s})},\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{d-1}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}(L-\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle)-k=\langle X_{1},\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{d-1}\rangle.

Now we define the set

\mathfrak{X}(y)=\{x_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{s})}(y):\ 1\leq i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\leq d-1\},

which is the same as

\mathfrak{W}_{k}:=\{n\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}:\ n_{1},n_{2},\cdots,n_{d-1}\geq 0,n_{d}=0,n_{1}+\cdots n_{d-1}=k\}

up to a rigid transformation (first do a reflection, and then shift the origin
0
to
y-(k+1)\mathbf{e}_{k}={\rm Proj}_{\mathcal{P}_{(X_{1})_{d}}}(y)
). We choose a special
y_{0}\in\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{1}
such that

\left|(y_{0})_{i}-\frac{1}{2(d-1)}(L-\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle)\right|\leq 1\ {\rm for}\ \forall 1\leq i\leq d-1,

which can be seen as the center of
\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{1}
. By a geometric intuition, it is natural to believe the center of

\{n\in\mathcal{I}_{L}:\ n_{d}=(X_{1})_{d},n_{1}+\cdots n_{d-1}=\langle X_{1},\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{d-1}\rangle\}

will be the center of
\mathfrak{X}(y_{0})
(See Figure
9
for an illustration in
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
), which is denoted by
x^{\prime}
and

(x^{\prime})_{d}=(X_{1})_{d},\ \left|(x^{\prime})_{i}-\frac{1}{(d-1)}\langle X_{1},\ \mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{d-1}\rangle\right|\leq 1,\ 1\leq i\leq d-1.

\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{1}
\mathbf{e}_{3}
\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}
y_{0}
x^{\prime}
X_{1}
\mathfrak{X}(y_{0})

Figure 9.
Illustration of the proof of Claim
A.1
for
d=3
.

So we compute for
1\leq i\leq d-1
,

\displaystyle\left|(x^{\prime}-y_{0})_{i}+\frac{1}{(d-1)}k\right|=\left|(x^{\prime}-y_{0})_{i}+\frac{1}{2(d-1)}(L-\langle X_{1},\mathbf{v}_{-}\rangle-2\langle X_{1},\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{d-1}\rangle)\right|\leq 2.

That proves
x^{\prime}
is the center of
\mathfrak{X}(y_{0})
, since
\frac{k}{d-1}(\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{d-1})
is the center of
\mathfrak{W}_{k}
.

Finally, assume
X_{1}\in Q_{\frac{L}{100d}}(0)
. Then we have

(A.23)

\displaystyle|X_{1}-x^{\prime}|_{1}\leq|X_{1}|_{1}+|x^{\prime}|_{1}\leq 2|X_{1}|_{1}\leq\frac{L}{50}\leq\frac{k}{10}.

That means
X_{1}
is also near the center of
\mathfrak{X}(y_{0})
. Moreover, using (
A.21
) and (
A.22
) ensures that if we shift
y_{0}
by a vector
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with

m_{1}+\cdots+m_{d-1}=0,\ y_{0}+m\in\mathcal{I}_{L},

then we still have
y_{0}+m\in\mathfrak{e}^{\prime}_{1}
and exactly

\mathfrak{X}(y_{0}+m)=\mathfrak{X}(y_{0})+m.

Since (
A.23
) ensures that for all
m\in\mathfrak{W}_{\frac{k}{50}}-{\rm center\ of\ }\mathfrak{W}_{\frac{k}{50}}
,
\mathfrak{X}(y_{0})+m
still contains
X_{1}
. Thus,

y_{0}+m\in\mathfrak{e}_{1}\ {\rm for}\ \forall m\in\mathfrak{W}_{\frac{k}{50}}-{\rm center\ of\ }\mathfrak{W}_{\frac{k}{50}}.

Therefore we conclude that (since
k\geq\frac{L}{5}
)

\#\mathfrak{e}_{1}\geq\#\mathfrak{W}_{L/250}=\binom{L/250+d-2}{d-2}\gtrsim_{d}L^{d-2}.

∎

Remark A.2
.

From the proof one readily sees that if the construction is iterated at most for
\frac{L}{200d}
many times (i.e.,
T\leq\frac{L}{200d}
), then
X_{j}\in Q_{\frac{L}{100d}}(0)\ {\rm for}\ \forall 1\leq j\leq T
. Thus the proof also applies for obtaining
\#\mathfrak{e}_{j}\gtrsim_{d}L^{d-2}\ {\rm for}\ \forall 1\leq j\leq T
.

Next, We construct the filtration element by

(A.24)

\mathcal{F}_{1}=\sigma(\mathfrak{F}_{1}),\ \mathfrak{F}_{1}:=(\mathfrak{R}_{1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{L})\cup\mathfrak{B}_{1},

where
\mathfrak{R}_{1}
is the half space under
\mathfrak{L}_{1}
.
Our construction ensures that

(A.25)

\mathfrak{B}_{1}\subset\mathfrak{F}_{1},X_{1}\notin\mathfrak{B}_{1},X_{1}\in\mathfrak{F}_{1},X_{2}\notin\mathfrak{F}_{1}.

Therefore,
\mathcal{B}_{1}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1}
.

If we further condition on
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, clearly the value of
u
in the region

\{x\in\mathcal{I}_{L}:\ x\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}\ {\rm or}\ x-\mathbf{e}_{d}\in\mathfrak{F}_{1}\}

will be determined, which contains the sites
C_{1}
and the set
\mathfrak{e}_{1}
. Hence, we can do further discussion under the case
X_{2}\in\{C_{1},D_{1}\}
: If
|u(C_{1})|\geq\gamma|u(X_{1})|
, then we let
X_{2}=C_{1}
; else, we let
X_{2}=D_{1}
. Thus the site
X_{2}
is determined.

Moreover, for any
n\in\mathfrak{e}_{1}
, the value
u(n)
is now determined. By our definition of
\mathfrak{e}_{1}
, if we further condition on the randomness in
\mathfrak{F}_{1}\setminus\{X_{1}\}
, we can express
u(n),\forall n\in\mathfrak{e}_{1}
in the following form:

(A.26)

u(\mathfrak{e}_{1})=a_{X_{1}}\cdot V(X_{1})u(X_{1})+{\rm a\ deterministic\ value},\ |a_{X_{1}}|\geq 1.

Since
V(X_{1})=V_{\text{hi}}(X_{1})+\beta\omega(X_{1})
and we have the transversality at
X_{1}
:

|u(X_{1})|\geq\gamma|u(X_{0})|=\gamma|u(0)|,

we can conclude that
|u(n)|<\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{1})|
only holds for at most one value of
\omega(X_{1})\in\{0,1\}
.
Hence, by the pigeonhole principle,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\#\{n\in\mathfrak{e}_{1}:\ |u(n)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{1})|\}\geq\frac{1}{2}\#\mathfrak{e}_{1}\ \bigg|\ \mathcal{B}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{P}_{\omega(X_{1})}\left(\#\{n\in\mathfrak{e}_{1}:\ |u(n)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{1})|\}\geq\frac{1}{2}\#\mathfrak{e}_{1}\ \bigg|\ \sigma(\mathfrak{F}_{1}\setminus\{X_{1}\})\right)\bigg|\mathcal{B}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Therefore, we define

\displaystyle{\rm Event}_{1}:

\displaystyle\ {\rm the\ event\ }\#\{n\in\mathfrak{e}_{1}:\ |u(n)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{1})|\}\geq\frac{1}{2}\#\mathfrak{e}_{1},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}=\mathbf{1}_{{\rm Event}_{1}}(\omega(X_{1})).

Then
X_{2},\mathcal{Z}_{1}
are
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-measurable, and

\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}|\mathcal{F}_{0})\geq\frac{1}{2}.

(Step 3)

\cdots

Iterate the above constructions for
T=\frac{L}{200d}
amy times. We obtain the following filtration

\mathcal{B}_{0}\subset\mathcal{F}_{0}\subset\mathcal{B}_{1}\subset\mathcal{F}_{1}\subset\mathcal{B}_{2}\subset\mathcal{F}_{2}\cdots\subset\mathcal{B}_{T}\subset\mathcal{F}_{T},

together with a (site-mixed) martingale
(|u(X_{j})|,\mathfrak{L}_{j},\mathfrak{e}_{j},X_{j+1},\mathcal{Z}_{j}),1\leq j\leq T
with the same properties of the martingale we constructed in the
d=2
case. The only tiny differences are

•

\mathfrak{e}_{j}\subset\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap(\mathfrak{L}_{j}+\mathbf{e}_{d})
is now a set with cardinality
\#\mathfrak{e}_{j}\gtrsim_{d}L^{d-2}
(by Remark
A.2
). Moreover,
\mathfrak{e}_{j}\ (1\leq j\leq T)
are mutually disjoint.

•

The definition of
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
is different. In higher dimensions, it is

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{j}=\mathbf{1}_{{\rm Event}_{j}}(\omega(X_{j})),

where
{\rm Event}_{j}
is the event that
\#\{n\in\mathfrak{e}_{j}:\ |u(n)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{j})|\}\geq\frac{1}{2}\#\mathfrak{e}_{j}
. We still have

E(\mathcal{Z}_{j+1}|\mathcal{Z}_{j})\geq\frac{1}{2}.

Therefore, by the Azuma’s inequality for the submartingale, we can conclude that, with probability larger than
1-\exp\{-T\}
, there are more than
\frac{1}{10}T=\frac{L}{2000d}
many
j
’s such that

\#\{n\in\mathfrak{e}_{j}:|u(n)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta|u(X_{j})|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta\gamma^{\frac{1}{10}L}|u(0)|\}\geq\frac{1}{2}\#\mathfrak{e}_{j}\gtrsim_{d}L^{d-2}.

That is to say, if we define

\Omega(\lambda,u_{0},\mathcal{I}_{L}):=\ \#\left(\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L}\cap\{x:\ |u(x)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta\gamma^{\frac{1}{10}L}|u(0)|\}\right)\gtrsim_{d}\frac{L}{2000d}\cdot L^{d-2},

then

(A.27)

\mathbb{P}\left(\Omega(\lambda,u_{0},\mathcal{I}_{L})\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{X}_{T}>\frac{1}{10}T\right)\geq 1-\exp\{-\frac{L}{200d}\}.

The estimate (
A.27
) is only about the randomness in
\mathcal{I}_{L}
.

Finally, by the similar trick leading to (
A.17
), we take

L_{s}=\frac{L}{10}+10s,\ 0\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100}.

Then the side boundaries
\partial^{\text{side}}\mathcal{I}_{L_{s}}\ (0\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100})
are mutually disjoint, and are all contained in
Q_{L}(0)
. By (
A.27
), the event

\cap_{1\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100}}\Omega(\lambda,u_{0},\mathcal{I}_{L_{s}})
will imply that in
Q_{L}(0)
, the total number of sites with

|u(x)|\geq\frac{1}{2}\beta\gamma^{\frac{1}{10}L_{s}}|u(0)|\geq\exp\{{-\frac{1}{25}|\log\gamma|\cdot L}\}|u(0)|,

satisfies

\gtrsim_{d}\sum_{0\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100}}L_{s}^{d-1}\gtrsim_{d}L^{d}.

This yields

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{uc}}(L,u_{0},\lambda)\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{1\leq s\leq\frac{L}{100}}\Omega(\lambda,u_{0},\mathcal{I}_{L_{s}})\right)\geq 1-\exp\{-\frac{L}{2001d}\}.

We have completed the proof Theorem
2.6
for arbitrary
d\geq 3
.

∎

Appendix B
Some useful lemmas

In this section, we present several useful linear algebra lemmas applied in our proofs. We begin with the Weyl’s inequality and its corollaries.

Lemma B.1

(
Weyl’s inequality).

Let
A,B
be self-adjoint on some
n
-dimensional inner product space
V

with all eigenvalues ordered in the descending order
\lambda_{1}(\cdot)\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}(\cdot).
Then for any
1\leq i,j\leq n
, we have

(B.1)

\lambda_{i+j-1}(A+B)\leq\lambda_{i}(A)+\lambda_{j}(B)\leq\lambda_{i+j-n}(A+B),

where we set
\lambda_{k}=-\infty
if
k>n
, and
\lambda_{k}=+\infty
if
k<1
.

The proof of the Weyl’s inequality follows directly from the min-max principle and is omitted here. Two important corollaries of the Weyl’s inequality are:

Corollary B.1

(
Spectral stability).

For each
k=1,\cdots,n
, we have

\lambda_{k}(A+B)-\lambda_{k}(A)\in[\lambda_{n}(B),\lambda_{1}(B)]

and thus

|\lambda_{k}(A+B)-\lambda_{k}(A)|\leq\|B\|.

We define the spread of a self-adjoint matrix as the difffference between its largest and smallest eigenvalues. That is

(B.2)

{\rm Spread}(\cdot)=\lambda_{1}(\cdot)-\lambda_{n}(\cdot).

Corollary B.2

(
Spread’s lower bound).

We have

{\rm Spread}(A+B)\geq|{\rm Spread}(A)-{\rm Spread}(B)|.

Proof of Corollary
B.2
.

Setting
i=n,j=1
and then
i=1,j=n
in (
B.1
) yields

\lambda_{n}(A+B)\leq\lambda_{n}(A)+\lambda_{1}(B)\leq\lambda_{1}(A+B),\ \lambda_{n}(A+B)\geq\lambda_{1}(A)+\lambda_{n}(B)\leq\lambda_{1}(A+B),

which gives

-{\rm Spread}(A+B)\leq{\rm Spread}(A)-{\rm Spread}(B)\leq{\rm Spread}(A+B).

This concludes the desired proof.
∎

Finally, we have

Lemma B.2

(
Approximate orthogonality).

Let
\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n}
be
n
vectors satisfying

\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{j}\rangle=\delta_{i,j}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon).

If
0<n\varepsilon\ll 1
, then
\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n}
are linearly independent.

Proof of Lemma
B.2
.

Consider the Gram matrix
G
of
\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n}
. We have

G=(\langle\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{j}\rangle)_{n\times n}=I+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),

where
I
is the identity matrix of order
n
.
Then by Schur’s test, we have

\|\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\|\lesssim\varepsilon\cdot n\ll 1.

Thus,
G
is invertible, which implies the linear independence of
\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{n}
.

∎

Appendix C
A coupling lemma

The following coupling lemma is standard in the multi-scale analysis, and one can find its proof in
[
LSZ25b
, Lemma 3.1, Remark 3.6]
.

Lemma C.1
.

Fix
L_{1}\sim L^{\alpha},1\ll L\ll L^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2}L_{1}
(
\alpha>1
). Let
\Lambda
be a
L_{1}
-size block. Assume
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}\subset\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
satisfying

•

\mathfrak{S}
is a union of no more than
N
many
L
-size blocks
\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}
,

•

\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is union of
L^{\prime}
-size blocks
\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}
, which is the
\frac{L^{\prime}}{10}
-neighborhood of each
\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}
. Moreover, distinct elements in
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
are at distance
\gtrsim L^{\prime}
.

Assume there is a class of
L
-size non-resonant blocks

\mathfrak{F}=\{\Lambda^{\prime}:\ \Lambda^{\prime}\subset\Lambda\}

covering
\Lambda\setminus\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, and satisfying that for each
n\in\Lambda\setminus\mathfrak{S}
, there is a
\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{F}
s.t.,

(C.1)

Q_{\frac{L}{5}}(n)\cap\Lambda\subset\Lambda^{\prime}.

Assume further for
E\in\mathbb{R}
,

(C.2)

\|G_{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}(E)\|\leq 2\exp\{(L^{\prime})^{1-\varepsilon}\}\ {\rm for}\ \forall\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}.

Then

(C.3)

\displaystyle\|G_{\Lambda}(E)\|

\displaystyle<\exp\{L_{1}^{1-\varepsilon}\},

(C.4)

\displaystyle|G_{\Lambda}(x,y;E)|

\displaystyle<\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}|x-y|\}\ {\rm for}\ \forall|x-y|\geq\frac{L_{1}}{200}.

Moreover, If we denote
S=\cup_{\mathfrak{F}}\Lambda^{\prime}
, then

(C.5)

\displaystyle\|G_{S}(E)\|

\displaystyle<\exp\{2L^{1-\varepsilon}\},

(C.6)

\displaystyle|G_{S}(x,y;E)|

\displaystyle<\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}|x-y|\}\ {\rm for}\ \forall|x-y|\geq L.
